# Changelog

## 0.75.0

- **A workspace's text file opens beside its terminals and is typed in at once.** ⏎ or a double click on a file in the files panel opens it in the tab's file pane, at the terminals' trailing side behind a divider that drags, its width remembered for that workspace; where the tab cannot give both their least, the pane takes the terminals' place and Show Terminal, or moving the keys, gives it back. Text is decided by content (no NUL and valid UTF-8 in its first 64 KiB, at most 20 MB): such a file is shown as it arrives and can be typed in once whole, with no mode; anything else is one sentence ("chart.png isn't text: a PNG image, 312 KB.") with Copy Path. A clean pane follows the file, the first visible line kept and the changed lines barred for 10 s; typed in, it carries a dot in its title and on its tree row, never takes the workspace's text by itself, and says "Changed on the workspace while you typed." with Compare and Discard Mine; undoing back to what was read makes it clean and following again. Clean means the very bytes read: CRLF, a BOM, mixed endings and no final newline come back byte for byte. Compare opens the conflict sheet: theirs and yours side by side, scrolling together, with Keep Mine, Keep Theirs and Cancel and no default. Closing the file, opening another into its pane, closing its tab and quitting ask Save, Discard or Cancel; closing the window loses nothing and asks nothing. The keys move between the tree, the terminals and the file by Select Next and Previous Split and Select Split; ⌘W in the file closes the file, never a terminal; a zoomed terminal hides it; hiding the files panel leaves it open. A card over a terminal now changes look by its layers' colours alone, painting nothing over the panes.

- **A stuck save is found in its workspace, and named with the way out.** A save whose process outlived its end was remembered only in the terminal host's memory, so a host restart, or a lock made anew after a workspace's last connection, forgot it. Before a lock's first write starts its script, and with every other write waiting for the answer, the workspace is now asked whether any process holds a file of Sail's exact temporary shape older than the save bound, leaving out the temporaries of the saves this host runs; so a save paused mid-body is never taken for a stuck one. A workspace that cannot be asked refuses the write. The refusal names the temporary: "a process there still holds <path>; when it ends, or once that file is removed, writes resume". Removing it is safe, since the stuck save's rename then fails on a missing source; a holder probe that cannot run counts as held.
- **A workspace's files are changed whole, and never over a newer one.** A files ticket's connection now saves, makes, moves and removes its workspace's files: `FilesWrite(id, path, base, size)` then the bytes as an upload's (`PutChunk`, `PutAck`, `PutEnd` with their SHA-256), answered `FilesWritten` with the version the file has now or `FilesConflict` with the present one (or `absent`); `FilesCreate`, `FilesMove` and `FilesRemove` are answered `FilesDone`. A save is one `incus exec` as the dev user: its temporary is made beside the file (`.sail-save-…`, never listed), its bytes copied and synced, and only then, under the workspace's write lock, which every connection to the workspace shares, the workspace's decision is asked again and the script told to go: it compares the file with the base, refuses one the dev user may not write, keeps its mode and renames over it in one call, or links a new name in one call that fails when the name was taken. So two of Sail's own saves of one base never both win, and a re-own, a disown or a relabel between a save's bytes and its end writes nothing and ends the connection in the decision's words; a decision that cannot be asked writes nothing. A move never replaces a name (`mv -nT`, decided by inode) nor goes into itself, and moves a link as a link; a remove takes a link as itself and says `partly_removed` for what it left; neither touches `/home/dev/workspace`. A save cut by its connection, a cancel or a full disk (`no_space`, with the bytes free) leaves the file and removes its own temporary; one a stopped workspace left goes at the next save in its folder. Sail makes only names that are UTF-8 with no control character (`unsafe`); a save carries at most 20 MB (`too_large`). One person holds at most 6 of a workspace's 8 files connections, so its owner and an admin never shut each other out; a watched path that resolves outside the home is told by name, and every watch refusal names its path as shown. A files decision, asked at a ticket, at every sweep and before every write, now asks Incus for the workspace's one instance (`incus query /1.0/instances/<name>`) instead of listing every instance's state and keeping one: the same answer, as fresh.
- **A read of a workspace's file is proved by its content.** The read hashes (SHA-256) the bytes it sends, hashes the same range of the file again after them and stats it again, and ends with its bytes only when both digests and both versions agree; its last `FilesData` frame carries that digest (`digest`, the content version of what was read, blank on the frames before), else the read is refused `changed` and the client reads again. Stats alone let a read spliced across an in-place write through whenever both stats fell inside one write's tick of a coarse clock: measured with a tight in-place writer, 271 to 807 of 2,000 reads on this devbox's ext4 and 33 to 51 on a GCP runner's, none on ZFS; the second digest caught all but 6 of them, each a file left half-written through both passes, which is what it then held. A save's base is a whole read's digest. A 20 MiB read hashed twice is measured in `WorkspaceFilesIT` and on the fleet's storage row. A digest is a SHA-256 in lowercase hex or the read fails: the script checks its two and the host the one it answers, a workspace with no `sha256sum` is said so ("dock has no sha256sum, so Sail cannot prove what it reads of …; nothing was changed."), and a failing `dd` fails the read rather than reading as `changed`. The last frame says whether the read was whole (`whole`: from 0 to the file's end), the only digest a save takes as its base, so a file over 20 MiB, never read whole, is never saved. Within the same unreleased `SAILPTY7`: `FilesData` gains `whole` and `digest`.
- **The drive gates what lies over the terminal panes, not only how long they wait.** Timing could not tell #491's card edge drawn as one full-card bitmap (its four-pane step's worst pane 87.3 and 113.9 ms) from a busy runner (110.0 ms on main's own run). Over every pane of the four-pane steps, before the look switches and after each, so in both looks, nothing but the pane's own dimming scrim and the dividers (no thicker than their 7 pt hit area) may cover more than 1 pt in one dimension or 8 × 8 pt of its terminal, a view or a bare layer on a view above it or on one of its own ancestors; nothing known covering more may draw itself, hold a bitmap or be a shape layer; no bitmap may be given to the layers covering more during the switches, each one counted; and what the step expects is found (four panes, each unfocused one's scrim, a divider by each, the card's edge in dark), so a walk that finds nothing fails. Other windows (sheets, the user menu's panel, tooltips, popovers) are not looked at, nor a layer that comes and goes within a switch. The rule holds for panes attached with no card and no floating panel; the pane's own card and the files panel floating over the terminals are named exceptions that may cover a pane but may not be given a bitmap during a switch. The rule is `OverTerminals` in the shell, and its tests re-create #491's two shapes and fail both in either look while main's scrim, dividers and card edge pass. The look switch's CPU figure now keeps the sampler one look past the last switch's end, and says the sampler stopped, not "not covered", if it ever did.
- **The look switch's holds wait for the main thread's clock to tick after the last switch.** A hold is recorded only by the tick that ends it, every 10 ms, and the step read the holds as soon as the last switch ended: a switch shorter than that tick, with nothing after it that let the run loop turn, read as not measured. Main's run at 3314ed38b failed `s32-panes4` so ("switches 4 not measured", a 5.0 ms median) and skipped every later drive script. The step now waits up to 1 s for a tick at or after the last switch's end before it reads them, so a stall running past that end is counted too; a clock that never ticks again still fails the switch.
- **The drive judges a look switch's main-thread hold at the median of switches 2 to 4.** `s32-panes4` failed when any one of switches 2 to 4 held the main thread over 50 ms: passing runs reached 38.1 ms at most, and a runner whose CPUs had none idle read 41.2 ms. The step now fails when the median of the three is over 50 ms (passing runs at most 34.8 ms, that runner 36.4 ms), or when the main thread's clock stopped ticking before a switch ended so the switch measured nothing, and says each switch's hold, the median and the worst with its switch.
- **The drive judges a look switch's pane wait over the four switches.** `s32-panes4` failed when any pane waited over 100 ms to show a change at any of its four switches: one reading against a hard bound, which main's own run at f24d9bc6f, a Java-only change, missed at 110 ms on a runner whose CPUs had none idle, while a PR on the same commit passed. Each switch now clears the panes' figures, waits for its frames to be told presented, and judges its worst pane; the step fails when the median of the four switches' worst panes is over 80 ms (the worst over all four switches was at most 71.2 ms in 166 passing runs of the two four-pane steps), says every switch's figure and always the worst, with its pane and why it waited.
- **A template whose systemd answers `offline` while it boots is waited for, not failed.** A booting systemd answers `systemctl is-system-running` with `offline` until it has made `/run/systemd/system`, and the build's boot wait took that for a final state, failing the build ("its boot did not settle: systemd says it is offline"). `offline` is now waited out within the bound while PID 1 is systemd, and said at once only when PID 1 is something else ("its PID 1 is sleep, not systemd, so it has no boot to wait for"); every other state a wait can turn into `running` is waited out too, and only `maintenance` and `stopping` end the wait at once.
- **A command's timeout is never outlasted.** A command whose own process ended while one it left behind still held its output (`sh -c 'sleep 6 & exit 0'`) held its caller until that process ended; what is left of the timeout now bounds the wait for its output and input too, and passing it is a timeout. A command whose caller is interrupted is ended with it, so an interrupted diagnosis question leaves no `incus info` running. Whether a command took its input is read from the write's own end, never raced. A timed-out start or stop's sentence now repeats systemd's state only when it is one systemd names ("an unknown state" otherwise, since whoever runs in the instance can replace its `systemctl`), and a question with less than a millisecond of the diagnosis's budget left is not asked.
- **A workspace's files hold their gate, their bytes and their names.** Every sweep of the terminal host asks each files connection's workspace again, as its ticket did: a re-own, a disown or a relabel ends a connection its person may no longer enter (`forbidden`), a workspace that went or stopped ends it (`not_found`, `workspace_stopped`), an outage ends nothing. A read stats its file again after its bytes and ends with them only when that version and the byte count are the ones it began with, else it is refused with the new kind `changed` and the client reads again; its bytes are read without waiting, so a pipe swapped in mid-read ends it. A name is carried as its bytes in every files frame (Java `FileNames`, SailKit `FileName`, shown with `U+FFFD`), so a name that is not UTF-8 is listed, read, stat-ed and watched as itself and two such names never merge. A path with a `.` or `..` part, or an empty one, is refused before anything runs, and the watch resolves each path and drops one outside the home. A workspace holds at most 8 files connections and a person 16 (`limited` at the ticket, `rate_limited` when presented). A handover or a stop of the host ends every files connection saying so (`terminal_unavailable`). A watch that cannot start ends its connection saying so. Within the same unreleased `SAILPTY7`: the files frames' names are bytes, and `FileRefusal.changed` is new.
- **A workspace's tab shows its files, live.** ⌃⌘S (View ▸ Show Files) opens a 236 pt panel at the tab's leading edge: a plain tree of `/home/dev/workspace`, rows only, no header, title or count. Rows are 24 pt, folders first and sorted as Finder sorts, an 11 pt triangle on folders only, the chosen row semibold with the accent bar. Folders open lazily with →, Space or a double-click; ← shuts one or goes up. The tree follows the workspace through the runner's watch: a change in a shown folder lists it again whole, keeps the selection and the scroll, and marks an entry that appeared with a small teal square for 10 s (never one of a first listing). The panel speaks in one quiet line at its top only when something is not normal, in the reason the workspace gave where it gave one: "Listing ~/workspace" after 300 ms, "Still listing ~/workspace (6 s). The terminal still works.", "Not live since 14:02." with the tree dimmed, adding "Reconnecting." only while it truly is (a connection silent for 4 s is closed and tried again, so a slept laptop recovers in seconds), "Can't reach the files." with Try Again, a stopped workspace's own record with Start (looking never starts a workspace) over a dimmed tree, one connection over the runner's cap in its words with Try Again, "~/workspace is empty.", and past 1,024 open folders "Following the 1,024 most recent folders live.". A folder the workspace refuses says so under its own row; a broken link says where it leads when opened. In a tab too narrow for the terminals beside it the panel floats over them; its being shown is remembered per workspace. The files connection rides out a drop on the same reconnect waits as the tab's terminals, now one value both use. Names are held by their bytes, so a name that is not UTF-8 shows with replacement characters and is never confused with one that reads alike; VoiceOver hears the tree as an outline, each row's depth and whether it is open. The connection runs only while the files are on screen: not with the window closed, in the Dock or the app hidden (⌘H). A tree is live once the host speaks after a connection is admitted, a tick or a listing, not at admission alone, so a host whose watch has stalled never shows the tree live. A folder that changed while it was listed is asked again at the next tick, so one that never stops changing (an expanded `node_modules` under `npm install`) is listed once a tick and holds back nothing else: other folders' changes arrive and a folder opened meanwhile is watched. Named again by the host meanwhile, it still waits for the tick, and after three listings refused in a row the line says so: "node_modules keeps changing; shown as of 14:02." ⌃⌘S is one View item for what is in front: Show or Hide Files in a workspace's tab, Show or Hide Sidebar on the Workspaces page, off with its reason elsewhere.
- **The app's lists reuse their rows.** A row that scrolls out of a list gives its view and cell to the next row that enters, made over whole (content, actions, note and its tooltip, chosen and hover state, progress line, what VoiceOver reads, direction, look), and every row is placed by arithmetic, no constraint: scrolling a 5,000-entry folder fast and a burst of 2,000 changes in a workspace's files are held by structure: the scrolls make no more rows new than a screenful and 4, taking the rest from the list's pool, draw at most 1.1 rows per row that enters view and build at most one name per row drawn, and a burst makes row views only for the rows it changes and its fills. The main thread's own CPU in its most costly run loop turn, and its longest turn on the wall clock, each the least of three tries, stay under 40 ms, which only a real slowdown reaches (12 to 22 ms on healthy runs); the drive reports each try and the worst beside budgets of 24 ms, 16 ms and a 120 Hz frame's 8.3 ms, and the Workspaces list scrolls the same way. Each row is one layer, its frames on whole pixels as before, and a files line ends on a whole point, so its hairline is one pixel and the rows under it start on the pixel grid. A changed row is made again whole and keeps the pointer's hover while the pointer is still over it; a row being edited in place is never made again around its edit, and a fill (a look or direction changed) ends the edit. A row made over for another enters view with its actions away rather than fading out, its old note's tooltip put away, and a view an item hands every row that shows it (a sidebar project's, the creating row's spinner) is never taken from the row showing it when the cell that showed it before is reused; two items handing the list one view stop the app, saying so. A right-to-left list now draws right to left whole: the chosen row's bar sat on the trailing edge and every row 2 pt off the mirror of left to right.
- **A workspace's tab shows its files, live.** ⌃⌘S (View ▸ Show Files) opens a 236 pt panel at the tab's leading edge: a plain tree of `/home/dev/workspace`, rows only, no header, title or count. Rows are 24 pt, folders first and sorted as Finder sorts, an 11 pt triangle on folders only, the chosen row semibold with the accent bar. Folders open lazily with →, Space or a double-click; ← shuts one or goes up. The tree follows the workspace through the runner's watch: a change in a shown folder lists it again whole, keeps the selection and the scroll, and marks an entry that appeared with a small teal square for 10 s (never one of a first listing). The panel speaks in one quiet line at its top only when something is not normal, in the reason the workspace gave where it gave one: "Listing ~/workspace" after 300 ms, "Still listing ~/workspace (6 s). The terminal still works.", "Not live since 14:02. Reconnecting." with the tree dimmed, "Can't reach the files." with Try Again, a stopped workspace's own record with Start (looking never starts a workspace), "~/workspace is empty.", and past 1,024 open folders "Following the 1,024 most recent folders live.". A folder the workspace refuses says so under its own row; a broken link says where it leads when opened. In a tab too narrow for the terminals beside it the panel floats over them; its being shown is remembered per workspace. The files connection rides out a drop on the same reconnect waits as the tab's terminals, now one value both use. ⌃⌘S is one View item for what is in front: Show or Hide Files in a workspace's tab, Show or Hide Sidebar on the Workspaces page, off with its reason elsewhere.
- **A field the person corrected stays corrected.** After a refusal, typing in the refused field clears its crimson edge, and a change of look no longer draws it refused again; a field left as it was stays refused.
- **A workspace's files are read through its terminal door.** `POST /v1/terminal/tickets {"action": "files", "project": ..., "workspace": ...}` issues a ticket decided exactly as an open in that workspace is (its owner or an admin, the project read first, and on a node the same check that refuses a workspace labelled for a person the node does not hold, in its own words: "..., so its files are not read"). It names a workspace only: the project's own container is refused (422 `invalid_request`, "A files ticket names a workspace of the project: ..."). It never starts one: a stopped workspace is refused `workspace_stopped` (409) with the remedy that starts it. A connection it admits serves only the files frames, for that workspace: `FilesList` answered by `FilesListing` (each entry's name, kind `file`/`folder`/`link`/`other`, a link's target kind `file`/`folder`/`other`/`missing`/`loop`, size, mtime in ns, mode; unsorted; in frames of about 512 KiB of entries; cut at 50,000 with `truncated`), `FilesStat` by `FilesEntry`, `FilesRead` by `FilesData` frames of 256 KiB, the last at the file's end saying so (one read takes at most 20 MiB), `FilesCancel`, and `FilesWatch`, which replaces the connection's watch set, each path from the version the client holds of it, answered by `FilesChanged` and a `FilesTick` each second. A request is refused with `FilesRefused(id, kind, sentence)`: `not_found`, `not_permitted`, `outside_home`, `is_folder`, `not_a_folder`, `not_a_file`, `loop` or `failed`. Nothing is installed in a workspace: the host sends bash scripts with each `incus exec` as the dev user, every path on their standard input, never on a command line, each resolved and refused when it leaves `/home/dev`, a link included; the watch is one loop a connection that stats only the paths shown, every 250 ms, and ends when its input closes; listings and the watch leave out a save's temporaries (`.sail-save-*`). At most four scripts run for a connection, two of them reads, the rest waiting in the order asked. Every script and the watch end when the connection ends, however it ends, when a sweep finds its person no longer admitted, and when the workspace is stopped or deleted, which tells it the stop's own sentence (`Err`, then the connection closes); a script that fails because the workspace stopped or went ends the connection saying so (`workspace_stopped`, `not_found`). A stop or delete now ends a workspace's terminals and its files connections in one frame to the host (`YieldWorkspace`), so a session opened as the list was read can no longer outlive it. Measured on a GCP e2-standard-4 runner in a real workspace: an `incus exec` costs about 60 ms, listing 5,000 entries 112 ms at p95 idle and 198 ms with the workspace's CPUs saturated, the first 256 KiB of a file 78 ms and 126 ms. SailKit's `SailTerminalWire` has the frames, `TicketAction.files`, `TicketRefusal.stopped`, `FileKind`, `FileRefusal`, and `ErrorCode.workspaceStopped`. **Breaking (terminal wire): `SAILPTY7`.** An app and a runner of this release speak only to each other; one of 0.74 is said as another version of Sail.
- **The drive's four-pane step waits for the tabs it closes to go.** The workbench keeps a pane until its host confirms the end, but `s32-panes4` went on as soon as the Close sheet went, so its last split's tab could still be ending when the next four-pane step counted the tabs; that step then waited for one more tab than it counted, which its ⌘T reached only if it attached before the old tab went. On #504's run `s32-panes4-dark` began some 30 ms after `s32-panes4` and failed "⌘T made no attached tab" over a fourth tab attached, its prompt drawn. Each close now waits up to the end's own limit for the tab to go, and the step fails naming any that stays; ⌘T's tab is found as the one not there before, a failure says how many tabs there were and its session's phase, and the time from ⌘T to an attached tab is printed.
- **A command Sail hands input to is timed, read and fed at once.** `ShellExecutor` wrote a command's whole input before it read the command's output or started its timeout, so a command that never read its input and never exited ran past its timeout for ever (one of 20 s with a 2 s timeout came back as exit 0), one that wrote back as it read deadlocked, and one that refused before reading came back as a broken pipe instead of its own refusal. Its output is now read and its input written on their own threads under one wait with the timeout, which kills the command's processes and ends the write; a command that ends without taking all its input answers with its own exit status, and one that exits 0 without taking it is raised as not having taken it, naming the command and never the input.
- **An `incus start` or `incus stop` that times out says why.** When a start or a stop of an instance Sail runs (a workspace's, a template's, a project's container) runs out of time, its error now says what the box said of the instance just after: systemd's state inside it and how many jobs it had queued, or that it no longer answered a command, and where the rest is (for example "Command timed out after 120s: incus stop acme. Just after, systemd in acme said it is stopping, with 2 jobs queued; Incus's log of acme and the rest are in /var/log/sail/containers.log."). The rest, Incus's log cut to its last 60 lines and the jobs' units, goes only to `/var/log/sail/containers.log` for root and `~/.sail/logs/containers.log` for anyone else (its folders made owner-only when missing), since the sentence can reach a node's other members; the whole diagnosis takes at most 10 s, Incus's log asked beside systemd, as it runs under the workspace's lock; the Incus integration lanes print and keep it when a job fails. It is the same diagnosis `sail init`'s timed-out steps use (`TimeoutDiagnosis`), one seam for both. Both logs are now made owner-only (0600) even when they already existed, and their directory is refused when its group or anyone else may write it. A symbolic link at the sail account's home is refused naming the bind mount that keeps the home on another disk, instead of "move it aside".
- **`sail agent attach` says a cold start before it starts.** An attach whose spec's workspace is stopped now prints "Starting <workspace>..." before the start, then its resuming or fresh line once the workspace is reached; an attach to the project's own container, or a resumed host session, says it only once the container is found running, never before a refusal. A word of why the pass did not reach a workspace that Incus refuses to store is now said once on the server's log, with Incus's reason.
- **A field the person corrected stays corrected.** After a refusal, typing in the refused field clears its crimson edge, and a change of look no longer draws it refused again; a field left as it was stays refused.
- **The Account pane speaks the user menu's language.** Signed in: the person's name as the display heading, `@handle · Role` under a SIGNED IN AS eyebrow, the control plane as the menu's own line under CONTROL PLANE (its address, a state square and word, live as a check starts and ends: Connected, Reconnecting, Can’t reach), the session's end in quiet text, and Sign Out as the secondary button. Signed out: "Sign in to Sail", the control plane's field and the ticket's under their eyebrows, Sign In with Passkey the one primary and Enroll with a Ticket secondary. A refusal is said under the field it is about, the field drawn refused (a crimson edge until it is edited again) and the sentence after the error's mark in crimson, the rest in the secondary ink, never a tinted row. A long name or handle is cut at its end and said in full on hover; the session's end wraps. The menu and the pane draw the control plane with one view and say who and where from one source, so they cannot disagree.
- **The closed user menu is no element to VoiceOver.** The key catcher is the menu, with its items as children, only while it is open.
- **Real clicks stay in the drive.** The user menu's click-outside test calls the menu's own handler, a test helper hands the view its own handler for every mouse button, and a sweep fails any app unit test that sends a mouse event through `sendEvent`, the app's or a window's. The menu's tests hold nothing that is the whole process's: each shell's menu is told its window gave up the keys by a notice of the test's own, and its Appearance goes through a recorder, so a plain local `swift test` passes beside every other suite. A sweep fails any view of the app that changes, in a layout pass (`layout()`, `updateConstraints`, `viewDidLayout`, `resizeSubviews`, `setFrameSize`), what forces another pass, which once crashed the drive; and the tests and the drive now write any exception AppKit reports or ends the app on, with its reason and stack.
- **A refusal of a workspace no one may act on never promises the next pass.** With no word of a failed strip on the workspace, a try not yet made or one whose word Incus refused, its doors now say the act waits for the pass ("gone is zed's workspace, and zed is no longer active here, so it is not stopped until the box's pass leaves it nobody's."), never that the next pass leaves it nobody's. A strip's long error is written on the workspace as one line cut at 200 characters, so Incus takes it. The pass's wait after no failure is an idle period, never a negative one. `sail agent attach` says it is resuming or attaching fresh only once the workspace is within reach, never before a refusal.
- **A node never acts as its own person on another's workspace.** On a node, a workspace labelled for a person the node does not hold by the id its label stores, one gone from it or another person given the same handle, was decided as the node's own person's: they could open a shell in it, stop it and delete it (with force, its work included), and start one whose handle was reused. Now no door acts on it there, its own person included, until the box's pass strips it and makes it nobody's: a shell, a terminal ticket, start, stop, delete with or without force, what can start and an agent start are each refused before Incus is asked, `owner_gone` for a person gone ("gone is zed's workspace, and zed is no longer active here, so it is not stopped; until the box's pass leaves it nobody's.") and `not_your_workspace` for another person of the handle ("old is labelled for a person this box does not know as ada, so no terminal opens in it; …"), which also replaces "no one's workspace" for such a workspace at an agent start on main. A label with no id is said as one, "labelled for ada without the id that says which ada". Another member on the node is refused such a workspace in the same words, where before they were told it belonged to the node's person. `sail agent attach` of a spec's workspace asks the same, running or stopped, before it starts or enters it. A node offers no delete of it, not even to the box's root; once the pass has cleared its label, the node's person deletes it. The pass no longer gives up on a workspace it cannot strip after three tries until sail-api restarts: it tries again an idle period after the first failure, then at twice the wait each time, at most an hour apart, until it is reached, and each failure writes on the workspace why and when it tries next, so its doors say "the box's pass has not been able to leave it nobody's (Error: …) and tries again at …" rather than promising the next pass. Only new entries are refused: a session opened before the label stopped resolving runs on, and its opener may attach to it again, after the host's handover too, until it ends; the pass strips the files under it. On main an admin still stops and deletes a gone person's workspace, and a start there says it in the same words.
- **`sail init` creates the sail account's home empty, and a host step that times out says why.** On #500's terminal lane `sudo sail init` stopped after 120 s on its first host step, `id -u sail || useradd --system --create-home …`, with no reason. On the lane's 23 other runs that day the same step took 18–73 s, because `--create-home` copies `/etc/skel` and GitHub's Ubuntu runner installs Rust, nvm and dotnet tools there. The account only runs forced commands, so init now creates it with `--no-create-home`, and its next step makes `/home/sail` with `install -d -m 700 -o sail -g sail` in place of its `chmod 700`, which also sets an existing home's owner and mode. That step first refuses anything at `/home/sail` that is not a directory, a symbolic link included, naming what is there: `install -d` would follow a link and give its target to `sail`. The native fleet harness now makes its main's account from those same steps (`SshIdentityProvisioner.account()`), so the account its SSH logins reach is the product's. Separately, a command an init step runs that times out now carries `ps`'s listing of its processes, taken before they are killed (state, kernel wait channel, arguments); no other caller asks for it, so no other timed-out command pays for it. When an init host step times out, init also asks the box, just after, each question bounded to 5 s: `lslocks`, the account database's lock files, `systemctl is-active systemd-userdbd`, and `getent passwd sail`. It appends all of it to `/var/log/sail/init.log`, opened without following a link and created 0600 in a directory it refuses unless it is a real directory that root owns, and prints one sentence under the ✗ line, naming the process the step was stuck in, what it waited on, whether the account lookup answered and userdbd's state. The terminal lane prints that log when it fails.

## 0.74.0

- **The rail's user button opens a menu of who is signed in, where, and how the app looks.** The 260 pt menu, drawn in paper and ink over the button, says the person (an initial in a 32 pt ink disc, the name, `@handle · Role`) and the control plane with its state (Connected, Reconnecting, Can’t reach), then offers Account (⌘,, also in the app's menu), Logins (not yet usable, and says why when pointed at or clicked: "Logins come in a later version of Sail."), Appearance Light / Dark / Auto, and Sign Out in a section of its own; signed out, a person's outline, "Not signed in" over the control plane last used (or "no control plane yet"), Sign In and Appearance; while the app is still asking who it is signed in as, "Checking…", never "Not signed in". The state is told again as a check starts and ends, so it never says Reconnecting once a check has failed. The item under the pointer or the keys and the chosen Appearance are turned over to ink, with no accent, so the window's count of terracotta marks is the same with the menu open. Appearance is remembered on this Mac and taken before the window shows at the next launch; Auto, the default, follows the Mac's as it changes. Opened by the pointer it highlights nothing, as a Mac menu; by the keys or VoiceOver, its first item: ↓, ↑ and Tab walk the items and Appearance without leaving the menu, ← and → choose an Appearance, Return chooses, Escape closes and gives the keys back to the button, or to where they were (a field with its selection as it was; the shown pane's own when that view has gone). A press let go after the menu closed chooses nothing. A click outside, the button again, a choice (but an Appearance) or the window giving up the keys closes it. VoiceOver hears the rail's button as opening a menu, the menu open and close, its items as menu items with the highlighted one selected and focused (Logins dimmed, saying why), and Appearance as a group of three choices; right to left it is mirrored and opens from the button's right edge. The button is outlined while its menu is open. The Workspaces panel's foot, which said who was signed in, is gone: its projects run to its bottom edge. The drive works the menu with real clicks and keys, signs out from it in the workspaces run, and switches the looks of the four flooding panes by clicks on its Appearance.
- **The Account pane's Enroll with a Ticket and Sign Out are the library's secondary button,** an ink outline with no fill in either look; in dark they were the system's filled grey bezel.
- **In dark, the panel's chosen bar starts a hairline in,** clear of the card's edge drawn over the panel's leading edge, which covered the bar's outer half. A link's words are set to the layout's leading edge, so right to left its underline stays under them.
- **A template is stripped and searched for its builder's tokens once its boot has settled.** The search ran while the template was still booting, and its boot's clean of `/tmp` removed directories it had listed (`/tmp/.XIM-unix`, `/tmp/.ICE-unix` on CI's Incus shard 2), so grep exited 2 and the build was refused as "could not tell what it holds". The build now waits for `systemctl is-system-running --wait` (at most 120 s, `running` or `degraded`) before the strip and the search, and says systemd's state when the boot never settles. As a guard, grep's "No such file or directory" alone no longer fails the search (a file gone holds nothing), while a token found beside it is still refused and any other error (a file it cannot read, whatever it is named, the two together, an exit 2 that says nothing) still fails the build, saying what grep said. grep's errors are kept in a `0600` file on `/dev/shm`, removed on every path, read in the C locale. The strip and the search run with `PATH=/usr/sbin:/usr/bin:/sbin:/bin`, so a tool a template installed earlier on its path cannot answer for them.
- **A runner says what can start in a workspace.** `GET /v1/projects/{p}/workspaces/{w}/agents` answers its owner, or an admin, whose the workspace is and whether it runs (a stopped one is no refusal: starting a session starts it), and then every harness Sail knows: its modes, each with its label and what it does in the harness's own words, the first the one it starts in until a person chooses; why it has no others (Codex: "Sail starts Codex CLI in a workspace with full access, running every command without asking and without its sandbox: that sandbox, bubblewrap, cannot start in a workspace's container."); the kinds of login it takes and how to make one; and the person's default login it starts signed in with, by name and kind, never its value, or why there is none (`login_not_stored`, "No Claude Code login stored for ana, so a Claude Code session cannot start in dock.", or "ana has no default … ; stored: home.", each with the remedy that stores one or chooses one), with a start that signs in inside still offered. A harness the project does not install is refused with `harness_not_installed`. A workspace that is not the asker's is refused whole with `not_your_workspace`, nothing offered and nothing of its person's logins shown, which is what an admin sees in another person's; one whose person is no longer active with `owner_gone`, at the route and at a terminal `Create` alike; on a node, its own person starts an agent only in a workspace no one is labelled for, never in one labelled for someone the node does not know. Another member is refused the workspace as every workspace door refuses them. The route and a terminal `Create` of an agent make one decision (`AgentStarts`), so the pty `Err` now carries `not_your_workspace` and `harness_not_installed` too, and a workspace start refused for a gone person carries `owner_gone` at every door. **Modes pass through:** Claude Code starts with no flag (`default`: the project's default mode, else Auto where available, else Manual) or in one of its own permission modes, passed as it names them (`manual`, `acceptEdits`, `plan`, `auto`, `dontAsk`, `bypassPermissions`, the last replacing `full`); Codex's one mode is `full-access`, replacing `full`. A harness's modes are listed in one place beside the rest of its description. SailKit has `Endpoint.agents(workspace:project:)`, `WorkspaceAgents`, `AgentOffer` and the three error codes. Within the same unreleased `SAILPTY6`.
- **A template is stripped and searched for its builder's tokens once its boot has settled.** The search ran while the template was still booting, and its boot's clean of `/tmp` removed directories it had listed (`/tmp/.XIM-unix`, `/tmp/.ICE-unix` on CI's Incus shard 2), so grep exited 2 and the build was refused as "could not tell what it holds". The build now waits for `systemctl is-system-running --wait` (at most 120 s, `running` or `degraded`) before the strip and the search, and says systemd's state when the boot never settles, after one wait (the failed build is marked unproven without waiting again); a template with no `systemctl`, or one `incus exec` cannot reach, is said at once. As a guard, grep's "No such file or directory" alone no longer fails the search (a file gone holds nothing), while a token found beside it is still refused and any other error (a file it cannot read, whatever it is named, the two together, an exit 2 that says nothing) still fails the build, saying what grep said. grep's errors are kept in a `0600` file on `/dev/shm`, removed on every path, read in the C locale. The strip and the search run with `PATH=/usr/sbin:/usr/bin:/sbin:/bin`, so a tool a template installed earlier on its path cannot answer for them.
- **The drive's split budget says what the app controls.** `s32-panes4` timed ⌘D to a new pane's first frame as one number, which a single wait of macOS's for the new layer's first drawable could fail (153.7 ms of a 163 ms split on #491's run, and 509 ms once before, about one split in six hundred, with our part under 10 ms). It now fails when our own time in any of its ten splits (the split less that wait and the drive's own thread) is over 75 ms, or when the median of the ten first-drawable waits is over 50 ms (a change that slows every first drawable), and it prints each split's whole time, the drawable's wait and ours every run.
- **The app is paper and ink: terracotta only where a person acts, and no grey.** As the website does, nothing is filled at rest but the one primary action. The window's chrome (the title band and the rail) is a warm ground at half the old peach's strength (#F2EBE7 light, #201919 dark), so the paper card has a ground to stand on: no outline round the card in light, a 1 pt hairline in dark. The rail's chosen item stands on the chrome's wash one step deeper (#E6E0DB / #2B2423) with its bar. A chosen row, project or tab has no fill: the 2 pt terracotta bar (a tab's underline) and its name at full weight mark it, and a row under the pointer takes full weight too, with no fill. A list's group heads ("YOURS 2") lose their band: each is its mono eyebrow, 34 pt, under a hairline; every eyebrow is tracked at .2em. Every project's letter well is an outline with a quiet letter, the chosen project's outlined and lettered in full ink; a person's initial beside their handle is an outline too. The quieter text is the site's pencil (#6D7277 in light). Terracotta marks only the one primary action, the bars and underlines, the caret, the cursor of the pane with the keys, focus and a link's underline: a plain Workspaces page draws 4 such marks, a terminal page with a split 3. Errors are crimson (#B91C3C light, #F2788D dark) and a refused create row is not tinted; the progress line under a workspace being created is teal; the empty project page's one primary is the empty state's New Workspace (the header's stays secondary). The accent's tint is left only under selected text (`textSelection`, 20 % / 30 %) and in the focus halo; the terminal draws a selection in the look's fixed selection colours, readable over any colours a program set, and a cursor that marks no keys is hollow ink. One primary button remains (`AccentButton` is gone). The marks that could turn over to ink later (the rail's chosen item, the chosen well, a person's initial) are one token each. Every control that draws the accent, the error ink or a neutral fill says so by token, and a test counts them on the Workspaces pages and a terminal page: 4 and 3 terracotta marks, and 0 pt² of grey at rest.
- **The app lane's drive no longer depends on the runner's Dock or its clock for the Workspaces section.** The lane hides the Dock, so every runner leaves the design's 1024 × 684 window room (a runner whose Dock stood 2 pt taller cut it to 682, and the window step now says the room the screen leaves when it falls short). A screen shorter than the design gives a shorter window with nothing clipped, down to the window's least frame, 480 by 624, now said in one place (`ShellApp.leastFrame`) that the drive and the tests read: AppKit adds the unified toolbar's 24 pt row above `minSize`, which is set from it. The slow first list, the slow create and the slow stop are held until the drive has seen and shot what is under way, then released, in place of answers delayed 3 or 4 s that a run already slowed by a failure (whose later waits are cut to 3 s) read as hung.
- **The drive gates how long four flooding panes wait for drawables.** `s32-panes4` fails when the least of its three floods waited over 60 ms for a drawable in its longest gap between frames (healthy code waits about 1 ms; a busy runner has made unchanged code wait up to 48 ms; a layer over the panes once made it 80 to 100 ms), and it says every view drawn over the flooding panes.
- **The runner starts Claude Code or Codex in a person's own workspace, signed in as them.** A terminal `Create` may name a harness and its mode (`agent: {harness, mode, inside}`) instead of a command, which must then be empty: the host composes the command from the harness and never runs a client's line as an agent. P1 knows each harness's own default mode and `full`; Codex takes only `full` in a workspace. It starts only in a workspace, and only for the person the workspace is labelled for: an admin who may enter another person's workspace opens a shell there, never an agent ("dock is ana's workspace, so an agent starts in it only as ana, with ana's login."). A harness the project's `agent.install` does not list is refused, and so is an owner with no default stored login for it ("No Codex CLI login stored for ana, so a Codex CLI session cannot start in dock."), with the runs' own typed remedy and the error code `login_not_stored` on the refusal, so an app acts on the code, never the words. Every refusal, the host's own (a live session of that name, a session cap, shutting down) included, comes before the workspace is started or anything is written. The login is the owner's default, looked up by the FDE id the workspace is labelled for, never the box's or another person's; it is written under the workspace's lock once its person is read again there, so a re-own while it was entered refuses it. It reaches the workspace only on the dev user's stdin, as `~/.sail/sessions/<instance>/login.env` (`0600`), which the session's shell reads into the harness's environment and removes before the harness starts, and which is withdrawn too when the hand-over fails, the session is refused, or the session ends. Nothing of a login Sail hands over rests in the workspace: Claude Code's API key is printed by the session's own `apiKeyHelper` from a variable of Sail's, so Claude Code neither asks to approve it nor keeps its last 20 characters in `~/.claude.json`, as a key in `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` would; its token is read from its variable; Codex reads its key from the environment through a provider of its own config (`model_providers.sail`), so `codex login` never runs and no `auth.json` is written, and the key is set empty in the shell Codex starts for its commands (`shell_environment_policy.set.CODEX_API_KEY`, one key by its dotted path, so a project's own shell policy, its `exclude` list included, stands), whose snapshot Codex writes to `~/.codex/shell_snapshots` (`0644`) with every exported variable in it. A session already running keeps the login it started with until it ends; the CLI and API say so when a login is replaced, removed or made the default. `inside` starts the harness with no login handed over, for a person who signs in with the harness's own login there (a Claude subscriber's `/login`), which Sail never holds; it stays in that harness's state across the person's own starts and goes when the workspace leaves them: a re-own, a disown and a template build now strip Claude Code's sign-in and state (`~/.claude/.credentials.json`, `~/.claude.json`) and Codex's store (`~/.codex/auth.json`), and every give removes `~/.sail/sessions`. Every process in a workspace is its dev user, so any session there (an admin's shell included) can read a running harness's environment, as it can the owner's git token. Of a command the agent runs, Claude Code keeps a token out but not an API key, and Codex gives its shell commands the key empty; Claude Code's `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` is no remedy in a workspace, where its sandbox is refused and so every command fails. An admin who takes the keyboard of an agent session drives an agent signed in as its owner, who is told. Sail adds no permission rule to these sessions: what the agent may do is the project's own settings and the mode the person chooses. The sessions listing gains `agent {harness, mode, login}` and shows the harness's own command; its doc now says the login shell's is `["bash","-l"]`. SailKit has `SessionCreate.agent`, `SessionInfo.agent`, `RunnerSession.agent` and `HostRefusal.code`. **Breaking (terminal wire), within the same unreleased `SAILPTY6` as the record of an ended session below:** a create also names its harness, a listed session the harness it runs, and a refusal its code.
- **A runner terminal's keyboard, said to everyone watching it.** Someone other than the session's opener (an admin) left watching a keyboard no one holds is told "No one holds the keyboard." with Take Keyboard, and a key typed there sounds the alert once; the opener is told nothing, since their typing takes it; when who opened it cannot be learned, it is said anyway, and the opener's first key takes the keyboard. A long tab name in "… is still running: the keyboard is with …" is cut in its middle, measured at the card's own width, so the sentence keeps to two lines; when the words around it need more on their own, the name is left whole. The client lanes fail when a suite or a test started and never ended, read from Swift Testing's event stream.
- **The app comes back to a person's sessions on the runner.**
  - **Quitting and signing out end nothing there.** Quitting detaches every terminal and asks nothing. Signing out takes the runner tabs from the window and keeps their places for the same person's next sign-in, which brings them back attached; another person's sign-in drops them. The quit's and sign-out's runner questions (`RunnerEnds`) are gone.
  - **At launch, once signed in,** each runner tab the arrangement kept for that person is attached again through a new ticket, showing what its program printed meanwhile. The arrangement records whose runner tabs it holds. A restore, a sign-in coming back and a reconnect (a Mac waking, the network back) never take the keyboard from the person's other Mac while it types there: the tab watches and says so; only an Open or Take Keyboard takes it.
  - **A tab the runner would not attach** says why from the workspace's listing: "This terminal ended: Workspace dock of harbor was stopped by ana.", or "This terminal is no longer on the runner: its workspace was stopped or deleted, or the runner restarted.", with Open a New One and Close; or that its workspace is not on this runner (Close), or the runner's terminal host is busy (Try Again).
  - **Open on a workspace** attaches a tab to each of the person's live sessions there that no tab holds, newest first; when all are in tabs already it chooses the newest's tab and starts nothing (a split of one of its panes starts one); with none, it opens a new one. A running workspace of theirs says how many of their sessions run in it ("2 sessions", "1 session"), counted again when a tab there opens or goes.
  - **Close on a runner tab** asks by the same rule as This Mac's, saying "Quitting Sail instead leaves every terminal running." While its keyboard is elsewhere, Close only detaches the tab and ends nothing, saying "harbor · dock 2 is still running: the keyboard is with your other window or Mac."; the session is ended where its keyboard is, or after Take Keyboard.
  - **The keyboard elsewhere.** When someone else takes a runner terminal's keyboard, this person's other Mac included, the tab says so with Take Keyboard, which takes it back: "Typing here is paused: you took the keyboard in another window or on another Mac." when it is the same person, and "…the keyboard is with uday now." when it is someone else. Return stays with the terminal, and a key typed meanwhile sounds the alert once. The host says which client holds the keyboard, so two Macs pressing Take Keyboard at once, or someone else taking it in that moment, are each told truly. When the holder leaves (quits Sail, or its connection is found dead), every client still attached hears no one holds the keyboard: the paused card goes, Close ends again by its rule, and the opener's tab takes it by typing. A Take Keyboard pressed with no connection says so.
  - **The terminal's cards are the controls library's** (`Card`): drawn as a banner is, information on the quiet surface and a warning or an error in its tone, with its mark, a primary action and a secondary one, in place of the old card's stock button.
- **An ended session is listed as what happened, once, in the past tense.** A yield carries a record apart from the line its live terminal is shown: a workspace's stop or delete lists "Workspace dock of harbor was stopped by ana.", a run's claim "Run R of spec S took over its conversation.", and any other end is said from its reason ("Its program exited with status 0."). The live terminal still reads the present-tense line at the moment. **Breaking (terminal wire): `SAILPTY6`.** `Yield` carries the record, `WriterChanged` says whether the keyboard is the receiving client's, and `Attach` may ask for the keyboard only if no client of the same person is typing, so an app and a runner of this release speak only to each other; one of 0.73 is said as another version of Sail.
- **A workspace is stopped or deleted from its row.** Its ⋯ menu offers Stop and Delete (⌘⌫) to whoever opens it, its owner or an admin. Both ask in the library's new sheet what they end, from the person's terminals in it counted as they choose: "Your terminal in it ends." A stop that ends nothing stops at once. Delete always asks: "Its files on the runner go with it… Commits you pushed are kept by the repo host." A delete refused because the workspace holds uncommitted work asks again in the server's own words, with Delete Anyway. While either runs, the row's pill says Stopping or Deleting and its actions wait; a refusal is said over its own project's page.
- **CI's terminal and native fleet lanes install nothing at test time.** On PR #484's run the Ubuntu mirror stalled: "Terminal end to end" timed out on its project's `apt-get install podman`, and a native fleet part hit its 20-minute limit rebuilding its Podman image test after test. Both lanes now take what they install from images prepared once a week and kept in the cache: the terminal lane makes its project from the Incus lanes' prepared image (Ubuntu 24.04 with every package a project's provisioning installs), and the native fleet's boxes start from their own image, saved and loaded with Podman. Each image is baked from one recipe in `ci/images` (`prepared-image bake`, `fleet-image bake`), before any test, each stage tried 3 times for at most 5 minutes, and a failed bake stops the job there once; `AbstractIncusIT` and `NativeFleet` no longer bake, and a missing image fails every test at once, naming the bake. The runner's own incus and ZFS tools are installed from `.deb` files kept per runner image and week, fetched with bounded tries on a miss, with no `apt-get update` on a hit. The one run-time touch left is the product's own `apt-get update` in a project's provisioning, which finds every package already installed.
- **The Incus shards split a class too long for one shard by its methods, and balance on real times.** `ci/it-seconds` recorded `WorkspaceIT` at 110 s when it takes 620 to 1009 s, so its shard ran past the 20-minute limit on the slower runners. It now holds each class's slowest time in main's last runs, and `ci/check shard` splits a class over 360 s into parts of its methods (`ci/check methods`).
- **What CI prepares is kept apart from `ci/check` and refilled each week.** The recipes of the prepared Incus image, the native fleet's image and the runner's packages move into `ci/images`, so their caches turn cold only when a recipe changes, not with every edit of `ci/check`; the fleet image's steps are `.github/actions/fleet-image`. A new workflow, `warm.yml`, runs on main at 00:30 UTC each Monday (and by hand) and refills the caches in three jobs: the two images are one cache each, but the runner's packages are kept per runner image, and GitHub chooses the machine per job, so a runner image none of the three lands on is filled by the first lane that does, once.
- **The app lane's download check reads the one listing it waited on.** On #491's run the wait step found the app's zip through GitHub's artifact API, and a second later `download-artifact`'s own listing of the run did not hold it ("None of the provided artifact IDs were found"). The step now downloads the zip from that same API by its id, tried for up to 30 s and saying when it had to try again, failing with a sentence naming GitHub's artifact service if it never serves it, and passes only when the downloaded bytes' SHA-256 is both the upload's digest and the shipped zip's; the download action is gone from the lane.

## 0.73.0

- **A person's terminal sessions in a workspace are listed, for an app to come back to them.** `GET /v1/projects/{p}/workspaces/{w}/sessions` (`main` for the project's container) answers the sessions there the caller may attach to: the terminal host lists them for the caller's own credential and decides as it decides that caller's attach (their own, or every one for an admin, in a project they may read). A member asking another person's workspace lists nothing of theirs; a credential that stands for no person (a machine token, the host's own token, a run's) is refused as a ticket is; a project the caller may not read, or a workspace this runner does not hold, is not found. Each gives its name, whether it lives, its clients, who holds its keyboard, its opener, its command, `created_at`, and once ended `ended_reason` in the host's words, newest first; none from an earlier workspace of the same name. A workspace's stop, and a run that displaces a session, now leave it listed as ended for 10 minutes or until the host restarts, in the stop's sentence ("Workspace dock of harbor is being stopped by ana, which ends this terminal."), instead of removing it at once; its output is still deleted then. An ended entry counts toward no one's session limit and holds its name for no one: anyone may open a session of that name. A listing asked while the host hands its sessions over is refused (503), never cut short. SailKit has `Endpoint.sessions(workspace:project:)` and `RunnerSession`. **Breaking (terminal wire): `SAILPTY5`.** A listed session carries its creation time and ended reason, so an app and a runner of this release speak only to each other; one of 0.72 is said as another version of Sail.
- **The Workspaces section is rebuilt on the app's controls: a left panel of projects and a project's own view.** The panel is the app's one left panel, 236 pt wide and dragged between 200 and 360 pt (remembered on this Mac), with each project's tile and template pill and who is signed in at its foot; under a 720 pt card it hides and View ▸ Show Sidebar (⌃⌘S) shows it over the content, shadowed as a floating panel, and on a wider card ⌃⌘S hides and shows it. Choosing a project shows its workspaces, yours then other people's, with Open (Return) or Start and Open and ⋯ for Copy Name (⌘C); a row says only what is true of the server's data (no owner, an owner whose account is not active, not created, Incus's error). New Workspace (⌘N) opens a row for the name with the server's rule under it; while the workspace is created the row is the one it will be (Creating, about 3 s, a spinner); a refusal is said under the name with "Show dock or choose another name." for a name already listed. A create belongs to its project: its answer lands there wherever the person is, and the name field keeps the keys across redraws; an answer never takes the keys while the person is elsewhere. Only a project whose template is current offers New Workspace; no template, a failed build and a build under way each say so, and a rebuild keeps the project's workspaces listed, with Open. Check Again and ⌘R ask for the projects and the list again, and a failed refresh is said in a banner. No workspaces, no project, the control plane out of reach ("main.acme.dev did not answer.", when it was last tried) and a stale list ("listed 4 min ago") each say what is true; loading shows skeletons. A change of look reaches every view. Nothing a person reads names a command or a route. What the view shows is one model in SailKit, tested on Linux against the server's recorded answers; the old Workspaces pane is gone. The list scrolls inside its frame and the panel's projects between its header and its foot, each with the app's quiet scroller (Mast's terminal scrollbar: a 6 pt thumb that fades once scrolling settles), the chosen row scrolled into view. The loading panel keeps its PROJECTS head, and the foot sets the handle apart from the role. A long taken name is cut in "Show …", never the words after it; an owner whose account is not active reads "inactive" in the owner's column.

- **A change of look does less of the app's own work.** A switch between light and dark used to resolve the window's SF Symbols again and have Core Animation tint a copy of each (`CATintedImage`), and re-declare and diff the constraints of the stack view behind every prominent button; the drive's stacks of main's switches show both. Neither happens now:
  - The prominent button draws its label in the current look's ink, so its stack view no longer declares and diffs its constraints at every switch.
  - A tab sets its title's font only when its selection changes.
  - The Account pane and the Workspaces section, when hidden, are painted when next shown, once, however many looks were switched meanwhile.
  - The rail's and the tab strip's symbols are coloured once per look (`SymbolInk`, non-template images), so a switch between looks already shown resolves none.
  What is left of a switch is AppKit redrawing every text layer and button in the new look. The drive judges what holds on any machine: no symbol resolved and no layout of the workbench's tabs during a switch, and every pane drawing every look. With nothing of the drive's own on the main thread during a switch, and each stall clipped to its switch, every switch after the first holds the main thread at most 50 ms, judged; the first, AppKit measuring the panes' text in the other look for the first time, is reported with its main-thread CPU time. Whether a switch hitches with four panes open is judged on a real Mac. The drive's standard output is line-buffered, so no printed line is lost when it crashes or is ended.
- **A ⌘D whose first frame is late says what its new view was doing.** For any split over 100 ms, the drive prints the new view's drawable asks, its refreshes by outcome, the drawables it threw for their size and whether the window read as visible; its budget line says that a p95 over ten splits is the slowest of them.
- **A close the host answers late takes nothing from the person.** An unconfirmed end shows its pane, its tab selected and the pane focused, only when the focus has not moved since the close (moving away and back counts as moving); once the person has moved on, the pane says so where it is and its tab is marked with a warning, the keys staying in the pane the person is in. Closing a zoomed pane ends the zoom as the focus moves on, so the keys go to a pane the person sees; VoiceOver says the tab's mark; End Again clears the old sentence as the new end begins, and the workbench is checked to say "Ending this terminal…" for an end the host holds.
- **Another version's terminal host is said once, with how to end it, and no second host is started beside it.** When what answers at this Mac's terminal socket speaks another version of Sail's terminal protocol (an older build's host left running across an update, as 0.71.1's was under 0.72.1), the app said "this Mac's terminal host stopped as it started", having started a host of its own that could only lose the socket's lock. It now says "This Mac's terminal host is from another version of Sail, and this one cannot talk to it. End it in Terminal with kill <pid>, then try again.", starts nothing, and Try Again works once it is ended. A runner whose host speaks another version is said as "This app and that runner are different versions of Sail", at once. Neither guesses which side is older, and the app ends no other version's host itself: until Sail is used officially, crossing a protocol change is ended by hand. Gone with that: the list of older magics and the direction a mismatch was said in, and the rule that took a host run from inside an app bundle (before builds ran a copy) as Sail's.
- **The app lane's download check waits for the artifact service, and a release builds from the native build's plugin cache.** The macOS app lane downloads the zip it has just uploaded, to check what a person gets; the artifact service once answered "No artifacts found for ID" 0.4 s after accepting it (Clients run 44464696183), failing a green lane. The lane now asks the service for the zip by its id, through the API the download then uses, until it answers, up to 30 s, saying when it had to wait, and fails naming GitHub's artifact service if it never does; the download still checks the bytes against the digest the upload took. A release's binary build restored a cache of another kind at every version bump (a tag reads only main's caches, never an earlier tag's), so it fetched the GraalVM native plugin afresh each time, and Maven Central's wrong answers for it failed 0.72.1's Linux build twice. It now restores the native build's own cache (`native`, which CI's native lane and Clients' darwin sail keep on main), and Maven retries a remote that answers 429, 500, 502, 503 or 504 five times (`.mvn/maven.config`).

## 0.72.1

- **A box caches the container image of Ubuntu 24.04, never its virtual-machine image, and one that cached the wrong one is healed.** Every earlier release cached the base image with `incus image copy images:ubuntu/24.04 local: --alias ubuntu-24.04`, which names no type: Incus looks for the alias's container image and, when that lookup fails for any reason, takes the virtual-machine image of the same alias. A box that got it (one of the 0.72.0 gate's boxes did, a minute before another got the container image) could launch nothing: every project and template failed with Incus's "Instance type "virtual-machine" is not supported on this server: KVM support is missing", and a template build said only "The runner could not finish that." `host init` (and so `init`) now finds the container image in the image server's listing by its type, copies it by fingerprint, and checks the type Incus holds; a box holding another type under the alias has it replaced, and step 7 says so ("replaced the virtual-machine image … with the container image"). A launch from an image that is not a container image is refused before Incus is asked, saying "This runner's image ubuntu-24.04 is the virtual-machine image …, which it cannot run as a container." with the command that heals the box, `sudo sail host init --storage <backend> --pool <pool> --yes` (a re-run keeps the pool); the template build's record carries the same reason and remedy. To heal a box from an earlier release, run that command once.

## 0.72.0

- **The renderer bench gates on CI only at one 120 Hz frame, and reports the rest every run.** Its 4 ms budget, the figure a person's Mac is held to, failed two of nine healthy CI runs (4.02 and 4.40 ms) and was never a fault of the renderer; on these runners no reference tracks the redraw closely enough to judge it on the machine's speed: over ten runs, all mid-tier, the redraw ran 3.22 to 4.02 times a fixed loop timed beside each sample, a loop 22 % slower came with a redraw 60 % slower, and a walk through 16 MB of memory tracked it no better. So the step now fails only over 8 ms (the worst healthy run seen was 5.25 ms), and says on every run the 4 ms figure ("over the 4 ms budget a person's Mac is held to; reported, not gated on this runner" when it is) and each sample over its loop, paired. The cost, plainly: CI no longer catches a redraw regression under about twice on its own; the reported figures are the trend, and the fix, main's renderer and the branch's drawn in the same job, is the spec's next step if a regression slips or is asked for. A verdict prints a fractional limit as it is, and whether the display tells presentation times is counted over the view's life, so a latency reset does not hide it.
- **Closing a This Mac terminal waits for the host to end it, and says so when it does not.** A pane attached to this Mac's host was ended by a Kill written to its own connection: a write that failed was swallowed, the host's answer was never read, and the pane went at once, so a Kill lost or refused left the session running with no pane, and the next launch opened it as an extra last tab. Every pane now ends as a runner tab does: a Kill on a connection of its own, the host's answer awaited within the end's 10 s. The focus moves to the next pane as the end begins, so a second ⌘W closes the next pane, and a pane whose host answers within 200 ms goes without a word; one that takes longer says it is ending, and when the host does not confirm, the pane stays, shown and saying the terminal may still be running on this Mac, with End Again and Close Anyway. A Kill refused because the host no longer holds the session counts as ended, by the host's own list.
- **The drive checks that no close leaves a session behind at the host, and its screenshot taker ends quietly.** A relaunch gives a tab to every live This Mac session the kept arrangement does not hold, so a session left at the host by a close comes back as an extra tab at the next launch; main's run 44314357528 met one (the second script restored its three tabs and a fourth). The first script now checks, after each group of closes and as it quits, that the host holds no live This Mac session that no pane holds, and names any it does, and that closing an attached pane while the host is paused keeps the pane until the host answers. Every run prints the arrangement the app saved, and a failed one the sessions the host holds beside it. The screenshot taker holds its FIFO open, exits quietly when ended and is reaped, where it used to print "Interrupted system call" and "Terminated: 15" on the way out.
- **The app's renderer bench names the budget it missed, and says how fast the machine ran.** Main went red at 29de429d on `renderer-bench`, whose line listed every figure and named none: the one judged, the full 200x60 redraw's CPU p95, was 5.25 ms against its 4 ms limit. Nothing in the renderer changed since a green main; the runner ran slow: that job's two builds took 136 and 128 s where green jobs took 41 to 50 and 59 to 71 s, and every figure of the bench, its p50 included, was 1.4 to 1.9 times a mid-tier runner's (about twice only a fast one's). The step now leads with its verdict ("missed: full redraw 200x60 cpu p95 5.25 ms, over its 4 ms limit"), takes 400 samples, the count the probe's bench takes (one `Sampled` in SailMeasures for both), and times a fixed reference loop beside each sample on the same thread, saying its p50 and the redraw's p95 in references. The redraw stays judged on its thread's CPU time, the floor of the probe's rule, which no look can excuse. A failed step's drawing state no longer lists frames "not yet told presented" where the display tells no presentation time, as a CI runner's never does: it counts them and says so.
- **A re-run of the app's job keeps its zip.** On a re-run attempt of the Clients workflow, the app job failed after its drive passed: "None of the provided artifact IDs were found" for the zip it had just uploaded (run 44300832101, attempt 3). A run's artifacts outlive their attempt. `download-artifact` keeps one artifact per name, the one with the highest id, and an id is not ordered by time: that attempt's zip had a lower id than attempt 2's of the same name, so it was dropped before its own id was looked for. The zip, and so its artifact, is now named for its attempt after the first (`Sail-<sha>-attempt3.zip`), so each attempt downloads the zip it uploaded. A release dispatched from a branch, not a tag, publishes its binaries but ships no app; its app's check now says so.
- **The published macOS `sail` declares macOS 15 again.** Every `sail-darwin-arm64` published from v0.13.164 to v0.71.1 was built on GitHub's `macos-latest`, which is macOS 26, and so declared macOS 26.0 as its minimum (its Mach-O `LC_BUILD_VERSION`, read from each release's asset). They did run on macOS 15: v0.68.0's self-tests passed there in CI, since macOS does not enforce a command-line program's declared minimum. Nothing guaranteed it, though, as they were built against macOS 26's SDK. From this release the darwin binary is built on macOS 15 and declares it. Every macOS CI job now names `macos-15` instead of `macos-latest`. The release and every pull request's darwin build check the binary's minimum against the app's `LSMinimumSystemVersion` and fail otherwise.
- **A release's app bundles that release's own `sail`.** The release workflow now builds the app too, by the Clients workflow's app job alone, and the app bundles the `sail-darwin-arm64` the same run built and signed, used only once its sha256 is the one the release publishes, its cosign signature is the release workflow's at that tag, its version is the tag's and its minimum macOS the app's: one binary, built and attested once, in the release and in the app. Until now a release built no app: one was built only by the Clients workflow, bundling a darwin `sail` that workflow built itself. The release runs the app's unit tests and its signing and notarization checks, not the drive, which the same commit's Clients run on main runs; a release does not wait for it. The app's zip is the release run's artifact; the release job takes only the binaries' artifacts.
- **Open terminals follow a light/dark change while they run.** When macOS switches between light and dark, every open terminal (every tab and split, focused or not) takes the other look's colours at once. What it shows, its scrollback, its selection and its cursor are kept, and colours a program set itself (OSC 4, 10, 11) stay as it set them. Before this build a terminal kept the colours it opened in until its next session, so a dark Mac showed light terminals. A program that asks for the colours (OSC 10, 11, 4) or the scheme (`CSI ? 996 n`) is answered with the new ones. A program subscribed to scheme reports (mode 2031) is told of the change, and told the scheme once more whenever this client attaches, reattaches or takes the write, so a change made while the client was away is not left unsaid; a program that did not subscribe learns only by asking. Only the client holding the write token tells the program: with two people attached in different looks the program hears the writer's, and an observer's look never reaches it. The drive switches the look four times with four panes' scrollback full and fails on any wait, or main-thread hold, over 100 ms counted from each screen's change; its screenshots are taken in each look switched while the app runs, once every terminal shown has drawn it.
- **The selected tab carries the accent bar.** The selected tab keeps its soft selected fill and gains a 2 pt bar along its bottom edge in the look's accent for text and marks (`accentText`: #FF7350 dark, #C8321F light), as the rail marks its chosen item and Mast underlines its active tab; its title is in the primary text at medium weight. The bar stands at 6.44:1 against the dark band and 4.53:1 against the light (5.29:1 and 3.96:1 against the tab's own selected fill), where the fill alone stood at 1.22:1 and 1.20:1. The rail's chosen item takes the same bar colour, so its bar changed in both looks: in the light look from the brighter terracotta #FC4926 (2.91:1, under 3:1) to #C8321F, and in the dark look from #FC4926 (5.04:1) to the lighter #FF7350.
- **Renaming a tab in the background goes on until the person ends it.** A tab renamed from its menu while another tab was selected ended its rename at the workbench's next redraw, of any cause (a focus change, a shell ending in another tab), keeping what was typed so far; Escape after it restored nothing. A redraw now ends a rename only when another tab was chosen (the tab selected before is still there: a selection moved by its tab closing is no one's choice) or when the renamed tab itself is gone. Return, Escape and the field losing the keys to another view end it as before. A tab's close button never selects the tab: it closes it where it is, as its menu's Close Tab does, so closing another tab chooses none, and when that tab is not the one shown the question names it ("Close api?") rather than saying "this tab".
- **A file pasted into a terminal reaches the session's own workspace, ready to paste its path.** The terminal protocol is `SAILPTY4`: a client uploads a file on the connection it already holds (`PutBegin`, `PutChunk`, `PutEnd`, `PutCancel`, answered `PutAck`, `PutStored` or `PutRefused`), so its bytes go only to the host that runs the session, never through the control plane, and stream straight into the workspace: nothing of a file is kept on the host. The host stores it at `/var/lib/sail-paste/<session instance>/<n>-<name>` in the session's workspace (its own instance, `<project>-<workspace>`), the dev user's alone (`0700` directory, `0600` file) under a root-owned directory, its name reduced to letters, digits, `.`, `_` and `-`, never overwriting a file or writing through a link; only the connection holding the write token uploads, checked at every chunk; 64 MB a file and 256 files and 1 GB a session, and room on the workspace's disk, enforced by the host (a full disk's refusal says how much is free); a file appears only whole and matching its SHA-256, renamed from a temporary, and a cancel, a refusal, a full disk or a lost connection leaves nothing; a workspace whose disk stops taking a file never holds up the connection: the host hands each chunk to the upload's own thread, refuses a chunk, or the file's end, not written within 30 s (`stalled`, "stopped taking"), gives up an upload left idle for 2 minutes, ends a session's copies when it ends (inside the workspace too, where a copy outlives its `incus exec`), and keeps at most 16 uploads under way on the host (`busy`), its only such bound; every script in the workspace is given up at its timeout, and a failed commit or removal is logged by its exit status; a session's files go when it ends, or at the next session made in its container. A session in a project's shared container, which every member's terminal reads, takes no file: it is refused, and the person pastes into a terminal in their own workspace of that project. SailKit has `TerminalSession.upload`, paced so a key typed meanwhile waits behind at most three 32 KiB chunks; a connection that drops loses the upload, and says so: nothing was saved, before the end; whether it was saved is not known, after it.
