It has one sentence for each refusal (`UploadRefusal.sentence`), and the paste rules the app will use (`PasteContent.classify`, `ShellEscape.escape`, `UploadName.reduce`); `sail-term-probe` has an `upload` step that records every ticket it asks for. A client of this release refuses a host before it ("the host is older"), and an older client is refused by this host. The macOS client lanes now run against the darwin `sail` built from the same commit, not a pinned release, whenever the Java modules or the build files change too.
- **The drive's flood line says on which side a late wake waited.** A view's wake stamps its hand-off to the main queue on both sides, before the hand-off and once it returned, so the line splits the longest wake's wait into the screen taking the output to the hand-off (the reader's side) and the hand-off to the main thread taking it (the main queue's side), each said as a range; a stamp the main thread overtook is said unknown, never stood in for. A session's reader thread is named (`sh.standardapplied.sail.terminal.reader`) and the session tells its quality of service; the drive's watcher looks at each reader every 5 ms, and the line says, across that wake's wait, at how many looks the reader was runnable or waiting and how much CPU it got. Each stack sample the drive takes while the main thread is stalled also records the main run loop's mode, and each judged wait names the modes its samples saw. The mode is read on a helper thread, never from the real-time watcher nor on every look: reading it takes the main run loop's lock, which on a CI runner waited up to 7 ms. The helper also reads each sample's slow part (the machine's ticks and the process's threads), finds the readers once a second, and checks that the watcher still runs real-time; the drive's last line says a demotion it found and the longest the mode read took. A script that measures nothing never starts the sampler and says so ("the main thread's sampler never ran"), where it used to start the watcher at its end and judge its CPU share over microseconds.
- **A change of look recolours and lays nothing out.** The workbench re-laid out every tab on a change of look only to give the dividers their new colour; now each tab's dividers take it where they are, at once.
- **The drive floods before and after its colour steps, measures a look switch, and its excuses count the terminal host as ours.** The first script floods once before its colour steps and once after them. Each flood's command is typed up to its Return, the measure is reset, and only then is the Return typed, so the measure starts before the flood's first output however fast the app draws; the flood's step fails unless that Return is seen echoed inside the measure. The four-pane look switch fails on any wait, or main-thread hold, over 100 ms counted from each screen's change, and reports the panes' draws and each main-thread hold over 60 ms with its stacks. The machine's ticks are read per CPU (`host_processor_info`): `host_statistics` answered the sampler's looks with its rate-limited last answer, so most spans read no ticks at all and a few read a second of them. A look reads the ticks, and our processes' CPU, only while the main thread is runnable, the only looks an excuse rests on, and a stack sample reuses its look's ticks. A look that took longer than the 15 ms two looks may be apart and still prove anything is not evidence: it is left out of every proof and counted ("N looks too slow to count"). The drive fails when a look costs more on average than the watcher's 250 µs computation, when the watcher takes more than its 5% of a CPU, or when it is demoted out of real time; the worst look is printed. Our processes' CPU is the app's, the terminal host's and the host's session shims' (`proc_pid_rusage`), so a host spinning beside the app is never excused as the machine's. Starvation proven over more than half a wait is inconclusive: none of it is excused, so a wait over its budget fails. The drive's own thread's stall over a wait is its own measured work and is excused as before, never capped. Every judged wait says the machine's idle and busy ticks across it beside what its CPUs make in that time, and each excused stretch says the same. The drive's own work no longer counts as the app's: a typed command yields between keys and every step yields once after it, so the main thread does what a step queued before the next, as it would for a person, and a `line`, `top` or `text` wait reads the rows the view drew instead of refreshing the screen under its lock every 10 ms, as the check after each step no longer does inside a measure.
- **The drive launches the app as a person's Mac does, and fails if it ever does not.** `ci/drive` exec'd the drive build from its shell. The system then reported it with no application role (`TASK_UNSPECIFIED`, parent `/bin/bash`), its main thread at QoS utility and every thread at priority 20, the utility band. XNU's source explains this; the effective clamp is not readable without privilege, so it is inferred, not read: a child inherits its parent's QoS clamp (`task_policy.c`), a thread's QoS, and so its priority, is held to the task's clamp (`thread_policy.c`), and a utility clamp sets the task's timer latency to tier 3, so its timers are coalesced. The role alone would have held it at 31. Every script is now opened by LaunchServices (`open -W -n -a`, without `-F`, as Finder or the Dock opens Sail): the app is `TASK_FOREGROUND_APPLICATION` with `PROC_FLAG_APPLICATION`, its parent `launchd`, its main thread user-interactive at priority 47. In an idle A/B of two floods (one run each way), the run loop's `Timer` had 28 gaps over 30 ms exec'd and none under LaunchServices, the main thread's longest hold in a flood fell from 25–27 ms to 0.7–1.5 ms, and the wakes posted from a real-time thread reached 34.6 ms exec'd against 9.5 ms. A `machineState` step at the first script's start fails unless the app is a foreground application whose parent is launchd and whose main thread is user-interactive, and it prints the process's BSD flags (`PROC_FLAG_SUPPRESSED` among them, App Nap's), the task's role and base QoS tiers, every thread's scheduled, base and greatest priority, and which of the drive's environment names reached it. `open` passes no exit status on, so a run passes only when its report says so, the app left the mark a clean exit writes (an `atexit` handler), no new SailDrive crash report appeared and `open` ended before the alarm. Each run's folder is removed first, so an earlier run's report never passes, and a drive still running after a timeout or on any exit of `ci/drive` is ended (TERM, then KILL after 10 s). Opened so, the app is its own responsible process for the privacy grants and holds no Screen Recording (`screencapture`: "could not create image from window"), so `ci/drive`'s shell, which holds it, takes the drive's screenshots on request: `screen FILE`, `window ID FILE` or `menu FILE` on a FIFO, the arguments built by the shell, FILE inside the output with links resolved. The enrollment ticket is handed in a file only the drive reads, never in the environment a launch passes on: after two launches by LaunchServices with the ticket in their environment, the second script's sweep found a secret in the system log. The sweep now says which kind of secret it found where. The drive no longer holds a process activity: with the app in front, App Nap does not take it (`PROC_FLAG_SUPPRESSED` was clear with the activity and without, one run each), and the run measures the app as a person runs it. Measured so, the four-pane look switch counts 48.1–99.5 ms of its 100 ms budget across five runs (the main thread held 64.6–82.9 ms with the drive's own thread held about 2 ms of it, and used 27–44 ms of CPU by the last sample): the switch's cost is the main thread's own work, and the budget stays 100 ms and gated.
- **The app's drive excuses a starved main thread only on evidence it prints, and counts every excuse.** Its sampler now looks at the main thread every 5 ms throughout, not only once it is late, from a real-time thread of the drive's own (period 5 ms, computation 0.25 ms; it may take at most 5% of one CPU, and the drive fails if it takes more), so it keeps looking while every core is busy, recording the thread's run state and CPU time, this process's CPU time and the machine's CPU ticks. A judged wait is the machine's for a stretch only when at least 4 looks in a row, none more than 15 ms apart, find the main thread runnable, its CPU time flat (under a tenth of the time) and no CPU idle. Missing looks, a blocked thread or one that used CPU excuse nothing. Each flood's line names the looks that excused each stretch, and the drive's last line counts every stretch it excused and says what the sampler cost.
- **An admin builds a project's template through the API, and every build keeps a record both doors read.**
  - `POST /v1/projects/{p}/template:build` builds it as the caller, an admin (Access's new Build a template: viewer no, member no, admin yes), with the caller's own stored git tokens, through the same lane as `sail project template build`. Its refusals come before anything changes, each with a typed remedy: not an admin (`forbidden_admin_only`, "Only an admin builds harbor's template.", a node's own person who is a member among them), a read-only credential, on a node anyone but its person ("… where only client builds a template; harbor's template for your workspaces is built on main."), a definition with no resources ("harbor's definition sets no CPU, memory or disk, so its template cannot be built."), a runner never set up, and a repo host that asks for a token the caller keeps none for (`login_not_stored`). It answers `202` once the build holds the template's lock and runs on in sail-api, `200` with the template as a read would answer, and `outcome: "current"`, when its version was built from the definition as it is, and `202` with `build.joined: true` when a build is already under way, the CLI's included (a request that arrives before this runner's build holds the lock waits for it). The command line's build refuses a definition without resources and a runner never set up in the same words, from the same checks.
  - `GET /v1/projects/{p}/template` answers `state` (`ready` while a version is current, `building`, `failed` when none is current and the last build failed or was cut short, else `missing`), `version`, `stale`, `steps` (20) and `build`, the last build's record (`by`, `started_at`, `step`, `steps`, `step_started_at`, `kept`, and once ended `outcome` `built`, `failed` or `interrupted`, `ended_at`, `reason`, `remedy`). `GET /v1/projects` gives each project's `template` state.
  - The record is `template-build.json` in the project's directory, written whole and durably (flushed, renamed into place, its directory flushed) at every step by either door, the CLI's naming the person it clones as. Steps 1 to 19 are the provisioner's and step 20 the lane's finish. A failure's reason is kept only when written for a person; a tool's output goes to the log, and the record says "The runner could not finish that.".
  - A build cut short (sail-api restarted mid-build, a CLI build killed) reads at once as `interrupted`: "The build stopped before it finished; its template is being cleaned, or is cleaned within a minute." A read never changes or waits on anything. sail-api ends each one as it starts and then once a minute, so a CLI build killed while sail-api runs is ended within a minute: its template is stripped, searched for its builder's tokens and stopped before its record says "The build stopped before it finished: the runner restarted, or its process ended."; a build whose process still holds the lock is never touched. A template cut before its dev user was made (step 6) is stripped like any other. No token survives in a template, structurally: workspaces clone a version's snapshot, made only after that build's strip and token search passed; a build that failed or was cut short, whose strip or search failed or found a token, or whose template could not be stopped (its record says which), marks its template `user.sail.template.unproven`, and the next build restores its newest version's snapshot (or launches it anew) before it builds. Building again resumes at the step that did not finish.
- **A create from the command line no longer waits on a template build without end.** Like the API's, `sail project workspace create` meeting another build of its template now waits up to 60 s and is refused as busy: "acme's template is being built (step 9 of 20, 2 min 5 s in), and no workspace is created from it until it is. Try again once it is built: a build takes a few minutes." A create used to wait for the whole build (136 s measured on a 4-core runner). A spec's run and a destroy meeting a build say the same.
- **A build that resumes is safe at every step.** A template build (or a `project apply`) that picks up after a step that did not finish skipped the network wait it had passed before, so on a template started again since (a restart of sail-api cut its build short, and its cleanup stopped it) the next download failed: "Could not resolve host: packages.adoptium.net" at the JDK step. The wait now runs whenever a later step does. A run that resumes first finishes what dpkg was left doing (`dpkg --configure -a`): a build cut while apt installed left "dpkg was interrupted", which refused the next apt call. Every apt call waits up to five minutes for dpkg's lock (`DPkg::Lock::Timeout`), and that finish asks again each second while the lock is held. The JDK step imports Adoptium's key with `gpg --batch --yes`, so a keyring a cut run left is replaced: it asked "Overwrite?" on a terminal the build has none of. A repo is cloned beside its directory and renamed into place, so a run cut mid-clone resumes to a whole repo, never the half a killed clone left. A test runs all nineteen steps twice.
- **A failed clone says what failed, in a person's words.** The provisioner's clone step said "Failed to clone <url>: <git's output>", which the API now keeps from a person. It asks again by exit status alone (`git ls-remote --exit-code` through the same credentials, then the host's own answer to a plain request): a repo read whose clone did not finish (a cause no status names: a dropped connection, a timeout, the disk), a branch it does not have, a host that refused ("github.com did not give vault's repo app to the token stored for it: the token may not read the repo, or the repo may not exist.", with the typed remedy that stores a token), or a host not reached. Git's own words stay in the log.
- **A node's round never deletes a template while it is being built.** A round that takes a project main no longer grants off a node held the workspace lock of the template's name, which a build does not hold, so a template could be deleted mid-build. It now holds the template's build lock, as a destroy does, and leaves the template to the round after its build.
- **`PtySessionHostTest`'s TCP cap case waits for each refused connection to end before the next.** It read a refused ticket's `Err` and connected again at once, while the host releases a connection's slot only as it ends it, so under load two refused connections could still hold the cap of two and the next was reset ("Connection reset" in `PtyWire.handshake`). It now reads each one to its end, which the host gives only once the slot is free.
- **A workspace or a project is created, never made, in every sentence Sail says.** The API's refusals and remedies, the CLI's output and the rooms' lines now say "create" and "created" where they said "make" and "made": "This runner is creating another workspace, which takes about a minute.", "This box is client's node, where only client creates or enters a workspace; …", "Workspaces are created on main: create yours there.", "This credential names no person, and a workspace is created for the person who asks.", "Workspace auth of acme could not be readied: …", "Workspace 'try' could not be finished: …", "… was never finished: its create did not complete.", "… so no new workspace is created.", "Next: create its container with …", a dispatch's "Workspace auth created in 3.2 s", and Access's "Enter a workspace: open a terminal in a workspace; create, start, stop or delete one". A test reads every module's sentences so a new "make" about a workspace or a project is caught.
- **The app's drive counts only arrangement writes when it checks that a title burst writes none.** Its title and divider steps counted every change to the app's settings for a fixed time after a fixed pause, so one unrelated late write failed a run. They now count only changes to the arrangement, from once no earlier save is pending until the step's own action is seen processed (the pane's last title, or the divider's first save) and no save is pending, on two looks a main-queue turn apart; a step that cannot begin or settle within 10 s fails saying which; any other setting that changed meanwhile is named in the step's line and not counted.
- **The probe's bench excuses a late frame only on proof its thread was starved, and says each one.** On PR #439's head the bench missed its 4 ms p95 for `frame()` with no Swift changed since a green run: of 54 frames, two (7.5 and 24 ms) waited for no lock, and the probe's own heartbeat was held 44 and 83 ms over them; in the green run too the probe's sleeping threads woke 30 to 100 ms late, so the runner was starving the process and where that landed decided the verdict. Each frame now records its own thread's CPU time, and a real-time looker, as the drive's watcher, looks at that thread every 5 ms: its run state and CPU time and the machine's idle and busy ticks. The p95 is taken of each frame less only the time its thread was proven runnable with no CPU while none was idle (four looks or more in a row, the drive's rule), never less its own CPU time; the raw p95 and max are reported, every excuse is counted, and each frame over 4 ms is said with its CPU, its wait and what was proven or why nothing was. A frame whose thread used the time fails as before. Four looks 5 ms apart prove at least 15 ms, so a frame a starved runner made late by less is never excused (#439's 7.5 ms one could not be): so each series now takes 400 frames, while the feeder feeds throughout, and its nearest-rank p95 leaves 400 − ⌈0.95 × 400⌉ = 20 of them over the limit where 54 frames left 2 (#439's red run had 3; every series kept since, 4 of 338, 1.2 %, about 5 of 400). That costs about 3.6 s a series at 120 Hz; on CI's macOS runner the two series took 15.5 and 14.6 s. A series that excuses more frames than those 20 is inconclusive and fails, saying so. Replayed through this judging, #439's red run still fails: its samples hold no looks, so nothing is excused and its p95 stays 5.245 ms. The drive's watcher and the bench share one way of looking (`Looker`, `Mach` in SailMeasures).
- **Sign In is never held by the app checking who is signed in.** Each time the app became active it asked the control plane it last used who was signed in, and while it asked it disabled Sign In, Enroll and Sign Out without a word; a click in that moment did nothing and nothing was said. Only an action a person started now holds the buttons, from the click or Return that starts it, so a second press (Sign Out's included) starts nothing. An answer to the check that comes during a person's action waits for it to end, so the pane never changes under a passkey sheet; it is then dropped if the action signed someone in or out, and otherwise shown, said after what the action said. A sign-in this Mac remembers that the control plane did not confirm now says "Not yet confirmed" and why, instead of "Signed in until", and "Sign in with it." is never said beside someone signed in. The Workspaces pane likewise no longer disables Open and Create, without a word, while it lists projects and workspaces: only a workspace being created holds them. The pane says Create, Creating… and "was created from an older template" where it said Make, and a workspace's state reads "not created" and "not whole, create it again"; a test of the clients' sources keeps make, made and making off every sentence about a workspace or a project. The app's drive now fails a step whose click lands on a disabled button or starts nothing, saying so, and waits for the person's action to end rather than for the pane to go quiet; every run but the second starts with none of the app's settings.
- **The Sail app has one set of controls, drawn from Mast, and a gallery that shows each in every state.** A new `SailControls` library holds the button (primary, secondary, quiet, destructive and destructive-quiet; 26, 32 and 36 pt; a leading symbol, a shortcut hint, icon-only), the text field (an eyebrow label, help or an error with its mark, a secure and a small variant), the state pills for workspaces and templates, the keycap, the initial, the spinner and the skeleton bar. Each is drawn by the app, so neither the system accent nor a stock bezel reaches it: hover turns a bordered control's edge to ink, a press darkens it, a control with the keys has a 3 pt terracotta halo outside its box (a 1 pt canvas gap first on a button), disabled is 45%, and a button in flight shows a spinner and its "-ing" word, keeps its width and ignores presses. A button takes the keys only with keyboard navigation on, as on macOS, and Space presses it. VoiceOver reads each control's role and label, a field's help or error, and hears an error when it appears. Reduce Motion holds spinners and pulses still; Increase Contrast has its own look (stronger hairlines and tertiary text, an opaque halo). What each control is in each state is a table in SailKit's new `SailDesign`, tested on Linux; the look's tokens moved there too and gained the design's new ones (`surfacePop`, `textTertiary`, `gridLine`, `fillPressed`, `fillSelectedQuiet`, `focusHalo`, the accent's hover and pressed, `error` with its hover, pressed and `onError`, `warning`, `success`, `info`, `ink` and `onInk`). Two shell tokens change as the design says: the light selected fill is 12% (was 16%), and the dark accent text is #FF7350 (was the accent), for 4.5:1 on the surface. The app now also ships JetBrains Mono Medium (2.304, the release its other faces come from, same licence), for the controls' eyebrows, pills, keycaps and hints as the design sets them. The light tertiary text is #666B70, not Mast's #6D7277, which is 4.15:1 on a chosen row. The drive's new `kit` run shows every control in every state in both looks and under Increase Contrast, left to right and right to left, taken as the screen shows the window and cut into one image per state. Nothing in a pane uses the controls yet.
- **A test suite that dies says how.** `ci/test-suites` says a suite that ended without a result by its exit status or the signal that ended it ("was ended by signal 6"), prints the crashing thread of any crash report macOS wrote during its run, and keeps the report with the suites' logs, which the macOS lane now uploads. `ci/drive`'s `stop_drive` prints each process it ends with its parent, and its wait no longer finds itself: the pattern rode in the wait's own arguments, so every stop waited its full 10 s and a later stop could end that wait as if it were a drive.
- **A drive step that hangs is named, its threads sampled, and the run fails as that step.** `ci/drive` watches each run's output while LaunchServices runs the drive: a step that prints nothing past its budget (the larger of 60 s and the step's own `seconds` plus 30, so a step is never ended inside its own time; the four-pane measure declares 300 s) is named ("step 6, kit-list-rows hung"), every thread of the drive is sampled for 5 s into the artifacts with the main thread printed, the drive is ended and the run fails with status 125. Before, a hung step ran silent to the 600 s alarm and left no evidence.
- **The app's controls gain lists, tooltips, banners and empty states, and draw the design's own icons.** `SailList` draws the two kinds of row: the main list's (52 pt, a hairline between rows, a 2 × 24 pt accent bar on the chosen row) and the sidebar's, which is the approved left panel's project row (30 pt, 6 pt in from each side, no gap between rows, a 2 × 14 pt bar, the project's tile after the disclosure's slot, the name in medium ink, a small pill for a template that is not ready at the trailing edge). Group heads are mono capitals with their count. A chosen row's fill is full while its list holds the keys and half otherwise; a row's actions fade in on hover or choice, the first one primary on the chosen row with ⏎ while its list holds the keys, the rest behind ⋯; ↑↓, Home, End, type-select, Return and each action's own key work on the chosen row, and VoiceOver offers every action. A project's tile takes the design's hue for its first letter (the nine letters it shows), any other letter one of the four hues by its place in the alphabet. An icon-only button shows the design's tooltip (an ink chip, its shortcut as a keycap, after 0.6 s, at once within 1 s of the last), never the system's. A banner says info, a warning or an error in the tone's colours with at most one action; an empty state is a 56 pt gridded tile (dashed when something is missing), a Newsreader title, a body and its actions. A project's tile sets its initial in JetBrains Mono SemiBold (2.304, now shipped beside Medium), as the design does. A click on an icon-only button puts its tooltip away; the chip stays 10 pt inside the window, then the screen, and beside a rail item goes to its left right to left, as the ⋯ menu then hangs from its button's right corner. A row's hidden actions take no click and Tab passes them; new rows keep the chosen item by its id; a row with no actions opens no menu. Every icon a control shows is now one of the design's line icons, drawn from its SVG paths (refresh, hammer, plus, ⋯, info, warning, error, box, layers), in place of SF Symbols. The gallery has sheets for each, and its keys include Home and End.

## 0.71.1

- **Open on a workspace from the app starts its terminal.** In 0.71.0 a workspace tab said "Could not start session …: Cannot run program "/usr/local/bin/sail" (in directory "")": the app names no folder for a workspace tab, and the runner passed that empty folder to the process it starts. A session in a container or a workspace now always starts from `/` on the box, its shell in the workspace's own `/home/dev/workspace`, whatever folder its request names; a session on the box itself opens at the host's home when no folder is named, and a folder that is not one there is refused naming the folder. A session whose process cannot start says so in a sentence, and what the system said goes to the runner's log, not to the person. The terminal end-to-end lane now opens a workspace with the app's own request, built by the one function the app calls (`Place.sessionCreate`).

## 0.71.0

**Updating from a build before this one** (its terminal host runs from inside its app bundle, so Finder cannot replace it while that host runs, error -47): quit Sail; open the new build once from where it unzipped, which ends the old build's host when no terminal is attached to it; then quit it and move it to Applications.

- **A terminal's screen changes reach the main thread without Swift's shared thread pool.** This is a robustness change; it does not explain the four-pane flood failures, which were the drive blocking its own main thread (fixed in 0.70.0) and, in one run, threads at default priority getting no CPU. The view took each change through an `AsyncStream` read by a task, and a task's wait on the stream resumes on Swift's shared pool, at the task's priority, before it reaches the main thread: with the pool's threads at that priority busy or starved, a change on the screen waited before a frame was asked for. Now the session tells the view on its own reader thread (`TerminalSession.onScreenChange`, replacing `screenChanges`); that thread takes one lock and, when no wake is outstanding, posts one block to the main queue, so one wake per view waits on the main queue however fast its screen changes. The main thread takes the wake, marks the oldest change it carries (output to shown is the longest any of them waited) and, when it carries more than one, the newest (so a key whose echo came in a later change of the same wake is put to it, not held for the next unrelated output), and draws the screen as it is then. A synchronized-output hold left open now ends on a dispatch timer of its own, not a task sleeping on the shared pool. The view's latency figures count the wakes and the changes they carried and keep the longest hop from the screen to the main thread; the drive prints them, and prints the faulting thread of a crash report when it dies. A function a session calls for every output (the view's listener, the probe's output tap) is held in a `Relay`: one held in a `Mutex` directly was wrapped in another pair of reabstraction thunks each time it was read, so the drive's flood ran its listener through thousands of them and overflowed the session's reader thread (a bus error).
- **A workspace no longer loses its network while it takes its own identity.** Making a workspace replaced the machine-id it cloned from its template by removing the file and writing a new one. The clone's `systemd-networkd` reads that file while it configures `eth0`, and when it read it in that moment it found none, failed the link for good and never took the address dnsmasq had given it. The make then waited its 30 s and failed with "… got no address on eth0 within 30 s". It was met when a workspace's network setup ran into the make's identity step: on a busy or slow box, and on a bridge that serves IPv6 router advertisements (Incus's own default; a bridge Sail's `host init` creates serves none), which make networkd read it later. It hit the CI lane's Incus tests about once a run. The new machine-id is now written beside the old one and renamed over it, so there is never a moment without one. The refusal also says what `systemd-networkd` says of the link ("(networkd says carrier, failed)").
- **The Sail app's window is laid out as Mast's shell, and its title bar row is the title bar's.** One band runs across the window's top in the chrome: an empty toolbar in the unified style, so AppKit sets its height and centres the window buttons in it. The rail starts under the band, and the selected pane is a card with 6 pt corners and 2 pt of chrome around it. The tab strip is a title bar accessory after the window buttons, so the band's empty part is AppKit's own title bar view, which takes a drag and the double-click System Settings names; the tabs start past a 96 pt inset for the buttons. The toolbar cannot be hidden and offers no display modes. In full screen the window draws the band, the tabs 8 pt in. A tab's title is centred in the tab and cut in the middle when long, with its close button on the leading edge while the pointer is on the tab, as macOS's own window tabs draw it; VoiceOver offers Close Tab and Rename on each tab.
- **Right-click menus.** In a terminal: Copy, Paste, Select All, Split Right, Split Down, Zoom Split and Close; on a tab: Rename, Close Tab, Close Other Tabs and New Tab, acting on that tab without switching to it. Each is the menu bar's own command; one that would do nothing is left out, Copy and Paste dimmed instead. A right-click on a pane that is not focused focuses it first, and a program that tracks the mouse still gets the right button in a focused pane (Shift gives the menu). File gains Close Other Tabs, and Edit > Select All selects everything a terminal holds. Rename on a tab's menu edits its name where it is, as a double-click on it does: in the tab's own pill, ringed in the accent, the text where the title was; a change of appearance does not end it, and Escape restores the name. The window is never made wider or taller than its screen.
- **The cursor blinks**, as the program asks: blinking unless it asks for a steady cursor (DECSCUSR 2, 4 or 6, or mode 12 reset), at the system's insertion-point rate (500 ms each way when unset), only in the focused pane of a shown window, shown at once while keys are typed, and steady and hollow when the pane is not focused. A blink lays out no row again. Idle, each half-blink wakes the main thread twice: its timer, and the drawable it asks for at once, so 4 wakeups a second with no display-link refresh (before this build the blink resumed the display link, which fired twice a half-blink and asked for a second drawable it never used: the drive measured about 10 main-thread wakeups a second, 1.8 timer and 8.0 display refreshes, with one pane). The drive fails a blinking idle pane whose display link fires.
- **This Mac's terminal host runs from a copy, and another copy of Sail's host is ended only when nobody uses it.** The app runs the host from a copy of its bundled `sail` under `~/Library/Application Support/Sail/Host/<checksum>/`, made again when the bundled binary's SHA-256 differs; older copies are removed once this build's host serves. A host found serving that another Sail app made (a copy there, or one inside an app bundle) is ended only when no client holds any of its sessions: the person is told first that its terminals are closing (when it has any), and the app waits for that process to exit before starting its own. When a client holds one, nothing is ended and the person is told how many terminals are held: "This Mac's terminal host is in use by another program, attached to one of its terminals. Close it, then try again." (the host knows which terminals have a client, not whose client it is). A host the person started from a CLI install is used as it is. A host stopped by `SIGTERM` (another copy of Sail, Activity Monitor, `kill`) is not restarted, and its panes say "This Mac's terminal host was stopped, and with it these terminals." with Try Again; a host that crashed is started again. Each signal is sent only while the process still runs the executable it was found running. An app opened from the temporary copy macOS makes of a download says once to move it to Applications.
- **The Sail app opens a terminal in your workspace on the runner.**
  - Terminals in workspaces need main at 0.71.0 or later. Against an older main, closing a runner tab cannot end its session, and some refusals may name a command.
  - Signed in, the rail shows Workspaces. Under PROJECT (with Refresh, ⌘R) a project's workspaces on main's runner are a list that hugs its rows: yours first, each its name, its state as a dot and a word, and another person's handle with a person symbol; the chosen row takes the selected fill with the rail's bar, a row under the pointer the hover fill. Where there is no list the pane says why: asking who is signed in, signed out (with Sign In), no project open to you, none in this project yet, or the list could not be read (with Try Again). Under NEW WORKSPACE, Make makes your own, enabled once a name is typed, and says "Making…" while it does.
  - Open (or Return, or a double click; "Start and Open" for a stopped one, which opening starts) makes a tab named `acme · try` beside this Mac's own, the same terminal in every way; a second tab in one workspace is `acme · try 2`. Why Open is not offered is said under it. The latest list asked wins: Open acts on the project its rows were listed for, and Make on the project shown.
  - A dropped connection says "Reconnecting to acme · try…" with Reconnect Now and keeps trying for at least a minute, the time each attempt takes counted (this Mac's own terminals keep their 15.5 s); a ticket never presented is used again, so a dead door does not spend the person's tickets.
  - A terminal ended from elsewhere keeps its last screen with the server's sentence and offers Open Again, which only a new ticket can mend; one ended while Sail was away says so. A lapsed sign-in offers Sign In, and every runner tab asks its tickets as whoever is signed in now, never as another person; signed out, or as someone else, is the app's own condition, never dressed as the server's refusal.
  - A refusal, from the API or the terminal host, travels whole (its reason and typed remedy, never flattened), and the app shows the sentence and, for a fix it knows, who acts in words; a missing git token or email of a workspace's owner is set by an admin for now. No command line and no route reaches a person: a call that fails is said by its kind ("The control plane answered with an error (502)."). Only words written for a person pass the workspace doors and the terminal door, a workspace's start from a tab included: a tool's own output (Incus failing, say) goes to the server's log, and the person reads that the runner could not read its workspaces, or could not finish that, and that an admin looks at the runner; Incus failing or not answering in time is unavailable, never refused.
  - A runner terminal outlives neither the app nor the sign-in. Closing its tab ends it at the runner; when the runner does not confirm, the tab stays and says what is true: it may still be running, and if it is, it keeps running there, and keeps its workspace running, until someone ends it or stops the workspace; End Again, or Close Anyway, which leaves it there. Quitting or signing out with any open asks once ("Quit and End 2 Runner Terminals"), ends them all at once within 10 s, and names by their tab's title, in that sentence, those that may still run; Go On Now says the same of those not yet answered before the quit or sign-out goes on. A tab still opening (a workspace starting) counts: its end waits, inside the 10 s, for the runner to answer, then ends what it made, so nothing is left at the runner unsaid. A log out, restart or shut down asks nothing. Closing the window keeps the app and its tabs; the Dock brings it back.
  - Every text field of the app's panes, the sign-in pane's included, is one style (the surface, 6 pt corners, a 1 pt ring, 2 pt in the accent while edited); a disabled prominent button is grey.
  - The terminal host lets a ticket's connection end the one session it opened or attached, only while it holds that session's write token (an observer ends none; the host's ownership check still decides), and says so when it does not: "This connection does not hold the terminal's keyboard, so it was not ended; someone else may hold it." Its refusals and a ticket refused carry their reason and remedy apart, so the API answers the remedy typed.
  - A test reads what a person in the app can read, every refusal there and the access refusals' sentences: none names a command line (a `sail` and one of the CLI's own verbs) or a route, and none written for a person carries a tool's stderr, directly or through a helper in its file. It reads every module's sources less what only an operator reads (computed from who calls what), and of the routes only those the app calls.
  - `sail-term-probe --workspace NAME` opens its `--api` session in a workspace; its `reconnectNow`, `hold` and `close` steps, and `endWhileOpening`, make the real-runner proof repeatable.
- **No refusal names an HTTP call, and "no terminal here" has its own code.** On a node, a person's workspace refusal now says "Workspaces are made on main: make yours there." instead of naming `POST /v1/projects/<project>/workspaces`, and a delete that holds work names `sail project workspace delete <p> <w> --force` as a typed remedy instead of `DELETE …?force=true`. A ticket asked of a box that offers no terminal over the network is refused `404 terminal_not_offered` ("This runner offers no terminal over the network; ask an admin.") instead of the router's `not_found`, so a client tells it from a workspace that does not exist.
- **A program's clipboard copy (OSC 52) reaches the Mac's clipboard** from a session this app writes to (it holds the write token): text only, valid UTF-8 up to 1 MiB, checked before any text is made; reads stay refused, and a session only watched writes nothing. Kitty graphics are switched off until the app draws images, so a program asking whether they work gets no answer, as from a terminal without them; and XTVERSION answers "Sail", not "libghostty", since programs that choose an image protocol by the terminal's name (yazi) took "ghostty" for Kitty graphics.
- **The Sail app counts a key's echo from when its bytes began to go out.** View > Show Latency and the drive's latency measures stamped a key written only after its write returned, and counted an output as its echo only after that stamp. An echo that reached the screen in between was put to the next output, overstating that key's latency, and the last key typed was never counted, so a measure waited its full 5 s. The view now takes the time before the write, an echo is the first output since then, and a key whose echo was already told, drawn or not yet presented, is put to it.
- **Tests no longer use the developer's real Sail.** Every test JVM, under Maven's unit and integration runs, has a home and a Sail of its own, and never takes a box's provisioned `/var/lib/sail`, so a test run cannot share lock files with another test JVM or block a Sail running on the same machine; a test started any other way (an IDE without those settings) is refused at its first class, saying which real Sail it would have used and how to start it.
- **CI runs only the lanes a pull request can affect.** `ci/changes`, the one decision both workflows now take, reads a pull request's changed paths against its base (a rename counts as both of its paths). One that touches only the app (`clients/SailApp/`), `CHANGELOG.md`, `ARCHITECTURE.md` or the Clients workflow, which no CI lane reads, skips every CI lane; one that adds SailKit outside its goldens runs only the native build and the terminal end to end, the one CI lane that builds SailKit (SailKit's goldens still run the unit lanes too, since `ClientGoldensTest` reads them). `CI passed` still decides: it fails unless the detection succeeded and every lane it did not rule out succeeded, and says which lanes it skipped. A push to main, a dispatch, an empty change or one that cannot be computed runs every lane. The Clients workflow takes the same decision (`clients=`), which now also runs its lanes for an empty change and when `ci/changes` changes. Standard runners cost a public repository nothing, so what this saves is time: a pull request that touches only the app gets `CI passed` in about a minute instead of about eleven, and nineteen jobs no longer queue for it.
- **`SkillFoldersTest` passes whatever the umask of the account running it.** The two cases that read a skill folder asserted `0644` on files they made without setting it, so under a `0002` umask they read `0664` and failed; they now set the modes they assert.
- **The probe's bench keeps each frame it gates, so a run over its limit says where its time went.** Its report (`macos-bench.json`) now holds, for each gated `frame()` series, every frame's time, its wait for the terminal's lock, the 16 KiB slices it waited behind and the probe's own stall over it, with that thread's stall windows. Its budgets and limits are unchanged.

## 0.70.0

- **Reinstall every box to take this release: its database gained a column (`run_delivered_messages.state`, schema v1004 still) and no database before it is converted.** A box on an earlier build refuses to start on 0.70.0 with "Database … was made by a build of schema v1004 before run_delivered_messages.state, and none is converted: the box is reinstalled." and the remedy to move the database aside and run `sail init`. The `sail-api` unit now sets `TimeoutStopSec=90`, so a stop is sized against a known limit instead of the host's default.
- **A message a room's live member was handed and never answered is answered by the next member, and the room says when it waits.** A message written to a live member that ended before it answered it (written while the agent answered the one before, or between a turn's start and its first answer, which Claude Code answers in its next turn; or taken by the container's exec client after the agent had exited) was recorded as delivered to the ended run, nothing was posted, and the next member counted it as read, so no one answered it. Such messages are now given back, in the same transaction as whatever finishes the run: its member's end, a restart's reclaim of a member left running, the sweep of runs whose process is gone, or a stop. A message is recorded as handed before it is written, so a turn that took it and answered it is never mistaken for one that did not, and a run already finished is handed nothing. If a harness's stream stops marking its turns (no `init` before a turn's answer), the log says so once per run at its first such turn, since every message it answers would be handed again. When the run was ended from elsewhere and nothing holds its repos, the next member starts at once and answers them, and nothing is said. Otherwise (a crash, an end this box made, a start refused) they wait for the room's next message and the room says so once: "The room's agent ended before it answered uday's message; the room's next message starts it again, and it answers that one then." When the member was removed from the room, the room is told instead that the message is unanswered and no agent is in the room, and how to add one (in a spec's room, the `sail spec engage` command). A message a turn had taken before the member ended is recorded on the run as unanswered, so the member that resumes that session is told, in one line, that its session may already show it, even after sail restarts. Stopping `sail-api` waits for every member's reader under one deadline inside the unit's stop timeout, and names in the log any it could not wait for.
- **A workspace is its owner's: only they or an admin enter it, and any member may make their own on main.** A new Access action, **Enter a workspace** (open a terminal in a workspace; make, start, stop or delete one), is owner for a member, yes for an admin, and no for a viewer, every agent and the in-container credential. A workspace's owner is the person whose id its label stores (`user.sail.owner_id`): a label with no id is no one's, as is one whose id is not its handle's active person's, everywhere a label is read (the doors, a run's entry, `workspace list`, the removal pass, and the reach of a person's token and key). On a node every person but its own acts as a viewer, so they are refused one there, naming main; the node's own person keeps the workspaces no one owns on it. A terminal opens in a workspace through a `workspace` field on `PtyMessage.Create` and `IssueTicket` (and SailKit's `SessionCreate.workspace`, the ticket request's `workspace`), decided as entering it, not as operating the box; a stopped one is started and given its owner's credentials first, and a ticket opens only the project and workspace it was issued for. `SessionInfo` carries the session's `project`, `workspace` and `opener` after its other fields, and a `Sessions` page carries its sessions' after its cursor (wire). The API gains `POST /v1/projects/{p}/workspaces` (`{"name"}`, the caller's own, cloned from the current template, never building one; a credential naming no person makes none), `POST /v1/projects/{p}/workspaces/{w}/start` and `/stop`, and `DELETE /v1/projects/{p}/workspaces/{w}[?force=true]` (SailKit's `Endpoint.createWorkspace`, `.startWorkspace`, `.stopWorkspace` and `.deleteWorkspace`, answering `WorkspaceCreated` and `WorkspaceChanged`). Each holds the workspace's lock from its decision to its act, waiting no longer than 60 s, then `runner_busy`. A delete, at the API and the CLI (`sail project workspace delete … --force`) alike, first reads under that lock what the workspace holds with the idle sweep's reads, and whether anything runs in it, and is refused with the new code `workspace_holds_work` (409; SailKit's `ErrorCode.workspaceHoldsWork`) naming it, its owner when the caller is not, or why it could not be read, unless forced. A stop and a forced delete end the workspace's terminals, each told who did it. The host CLI's workspace verbs, dry runs included, ask the same question; `workspace shell` starts a stopped workspace.
- **A workspace that gets no network address is not made.** A workspace whose `eth0` gets no IPv4 address within the 30 s its make waits for one was made anyway, silently, without network (a repo with a URL remote then failed its fetch; one with a path remote was "made" with none). Its create now fails naming the wait and what to check, "acme-try got no address on eth0 within 30 s, so its repos cannot be fetched; check that the network Incus gives it (…) serves addresses", and the half-made workspace is removed as any failed create's is; a dispatch that needed it gives back what it claimed and says to dispatch again.
- **A node no longer deletes a workspace that Incus lists while it is stopping.** This defect is in 0.67.0 through 0.69.1. It exposed a node restricted to some projects: a node whose person main scopes to a list of projects, not main and not a node granted every project. Each such node's round lists the box's instances about once a minute. Incus lists an instance it cannot render, as it can for a moment while one stops, with only its name and an `Error` status, and no config. When a round's listing caught a workspace or a template of a project in scope at that moment, the round took it for the container of a project named after it, a project the box was not granted. It force-deleted it, and what was lost was the whole workspace: its uncommitted changes, untracked files, stashes and commits that no remote had, or, for a template, the template, until it was built again. The journal said "project <instance>: its container deleted, workspace and uncommitted work included — main no longer grants this box the project". A project's own container is now launched carrying `user.sail.project` of its name, and keeps it through `sail project rename`. A round deletes only an instance it can tell by its keys: a project's own container, or a workspace or template of a project that is leaving the box. Before deleting one it takes that instance's lock, the one a workspace's make, entry, stop and delete hold, and reads the instance again. When the lock is held, it leaves the instance to the next round. When the instance no longer reads as part of the leaving project, it is not deleted. An instance Incus cannot render is left as it is, and nothing is said of it. A container with no `user.sail.project` key is never deleted, the device it carries notwithstanding: a container an earlier release launched has none, and is kept and named as one Sail did not provision.
- **`sail project destroy` deletes a project's workspaces and template only once nothing else can change them.** It takes each workspace's lock and the template's build lock, then the box's create lock, and reads them again before it deletes anything. It waits for each lock no longer than 60 s, and a create, start, stop, delete or build that still holds one by then refuses the destroy, saying what holds it and to destroy again once that is done. A workspace made after the destroy read what it would delete refuses it too, naming it, so a destroy deletes only what it showed. A create that had not reached its clone finds no template and fails, so no workspace is left of a project that is gone.
- **`sail host status --json` says what each private door is set to.** Each `listen` entry now has `set` beside `setting` and `state`: the value as `sail host config set` takes it, or `none`.
- **`sail fde key list` shows every registered key again, with its person's state** (a new `STATE` column: `active` or `disabled`). In 0.69.0 it shared the SSH gateway's query, and so hid a disabled person's keys; the gateway's `authorized_keys` still holds only active people's.
- **`sail init` and `sail host init` try a package install again when the mirror answers 404 for a file its own index names.** A cloud's regional Ubuntu mirror was measured missing 2 to 4 of the 115 files of an Incus install on two runs in three, different files each time, from several of its addresses; apt's own retries do not cover a 404, so setup stopped at `[2/8] Installing Incus` with "Fix the cause above", naming nothing a person could fix. Now, when apt fails and every error it names is an `E: Failed to fetch <url>  404` line (besides its closing `Unable to fetch some archives`), the step says so on its progress line (`the mirror <host> answered 404 for 2 of its files; trying again (2 of 4)`), every later progress line of that install starts `try 2 of 4 after a 404:`, apt's index is refreshed once, before the second try, and the install runs again; a 404 beside any other error (dpkg failing, a full disk) fails at once with apt's own reason, and a refresh that fails between tries says the install was being tried again for the mirror's 404; a failed run keeps what it fetched, so each try asks only for the files still missing. After 4 tries it stops: `the mirror <host> is not serving files its own index names (4 tries; the last answered 404 for <file>). That is the mirror's fault, not this box's: run the same command again, and apt fetches only the files still missing.` The rule is in the one place Sail runs apt on its host, so it covers Incus, the ZFS utilities, the missing prerequisites `host init` installs, and `sail host incus upgrade`'s move of held packages.
- **The drive no longer blocks the main thread it measures.** Inside a measured window the four-pane step listed processes with a synchronous `ps` on the main thread, while the floods still wrote; on a busy runner that held every pane's frames for 170 to 290 ms and failed the flood gate as the app's (sail#434's and sail#439's runs). `ps`, the CPU shares it feeds and the floods' stop file now run off the main thread, and a step inside a `resetLatency`…`shownWithin` window saves its screen's text off the main thread and takes no snapshot. The gates are unchanged.
- **The Sail app's terminal never waits for the window server on its main thread.** A frame asks for its drawable on a background queue at the moment it needs one and is drawn when it arrives, so typing, menus and window dragging stay live while every drawable is in use (before, the ask waited on the main thread up to a second, then retried every 0.25 s); nothing about what is drawn changes. Where the display gives no presentation time, an echo or output counts as shown when its frame's command buffer completed, and View > Show Latency says which measure it used.

## 0.69.1

- **A main at a new address is the same main.** 0.69.0 took `sudo sail init --main <address>` on a box that already follows a main as following a different one: it warned, needed `--yes`, and forgot which database the box follows, so re-pinning a node to its main restored on a new box was refused without a terminal, and with `--yes` a node pointed at an empty rebuilt main no longer refused it as another org. The re-pin is again what it was before 0.69.0: no question, nothing forgotten, and the next round admits the new address only if it serves the database the box synced with, refusing anything else. Following a different main is now said explicitly, `sudo sail init --main <address> --another-main`, the only path that prints what that does (with its counts), asks or takes `--yes`, and forgets the old main; the first round's refusal of a handle the new main gives someone else, and its remedies, hang off it as before. The refusal of another database names both ways: restore main's database from a copy, or follow a different main on purpose with `--another-main`.

## 0.69.0

- **Reinstall every box to take this release: its database schema changed (v1004) and no database before it is converted.** A box on 0.68.0 refuses to start on 0.69.0 with "Database schema v1003 was made by an earlier Sail release; this release opens only v1004 and converts none: the box is reinstalled." and the remedy to move the database aside and run `sail init`. The sync wire is protocol 9: a 0.69.0 main refuses a 0.68.0 node at the hello and tells it to reinstall, so no run crosses through a box that drops the login it started with. After reinstalling, each person stores their harness login again (below), or their spec runs are refused.
- **A run of a spec starts its harness with a login its person stored, and no secret of a run is ever on a command line.** `sail agent login <harness>` reads an API key or a long-lived token from stdin (a terminal echoes nothing) and stores it as `default`, or under `--name`, in root-only files on the runner (`/var/lib/sail/logins/<fde-id>/<harness>/<name>`, `0700` directories, `0600` files, written whole and renamed), outside the database and never synced; it prints the name, kind, whether it is the default and the first eight hex digits of the value's SHA-256, never the value. Claude Code takes a `token` from `claude setup-token` (`CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`) or an `api-key` (`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`); Codex an `api-key` (`CODEX_API_KEY`, which every `codex exec` reads first). A value is one line of printable ASCII with no space, quote, backslash, `$` or backtick, at most 16 KiB; a refusal names the rule, never the value. `sail agent login list [--fde <handle>] [--json]`, `sail agent login use <harness> <name>` and `sail agent login remove <harness> <name> [--fde <handle>]` list, switch and remove; a switch applies to launches after it, and removing a login live runs started with says they finish on it. On the host they are root's and act as the box's person; anyone else reaches the same doors (`GET /v1/logins`, `PUT /v1/logins/{harness}/{name}`, `POST /v1/logins/{harness}/{name}:use`, `DELETE /v1/logins/{harness}/{name}`) through the gateway lane or Mast. A new Access action, **Keep logins**, is yes for every person, a viewer included, and no for agents and the credential in a project container; another person's logins are listed or removed only by an admin (Manage people). Each change is announced (`login_stored`, `login_default`, `login_removed`) with its person, harness, name, kind and fingerprint.
- **Runs of spec work no longer use a login made by hand in the project's container.** A build, review, fix and every lane of a spec's room starts with the stored default login of the person it acts for (an admin's dispatch of another person's spec spends that person's login), and is refused before anything is reserved when there is none: "No Claude Code login stored for uday, so a run of a spec cannot start: a login made by hand in the project's container is not used by runs.", naming how to make each kind and the command that stores it, with its own error code, `login_not_stored` (409; SailKit's `ErrorCode.loginNotStored`). A dry-run dispatch is refused the same way. When a room wake, a resumed build, a review, a fix or a dispatch is refused so, the spec's room is told once, naming the person, the harness and the commands that fix it, and not again for the same cause until a run of the spec starts. An ad-hoc run and a member of a room with no spec still use the container's own login. The run records `login` (the name, or `container`), `login_kind` and `dispatched_by` (the person whose request launched it: a dispatch's caller, or the person whose message woke a room's run; none for Sail's own machinery: reviews, fixes and resumed builds), synced with the run.
- **The run credential and the login reach a run only in its `launch.env`.** Each launch pushes `/home/dev/.sail/runs/<run>/launch.env` (`0600`, the dev user's) through the one push helper (`ContainerFilePush.pushAsDev`, which `project apply` and the `gh` login use too), and every launch shape's wrapper starts with the same fixed text, which turns tracing off, exports each line as it is and removes the file before the harness starts, or ends the launch when there is no file. The file sets every other credential variable the harness reads empty, so a key the container's profile exports cannot outrank the stored login. `systemd-run --setenv SAIL_RUN_CREDENTIAL`, the foreground wrapper's assignment and the live member's `incus exec --env SAIL_RUN_CREDENTIAL` are gone, and with them the redaction of launch commands shown to listeners. The room lane's read-deny rules gain `launch.env`. On the host every pushed file is staged in the box's own staging directory beside its database (`/var/lib/sail/staging`, `0700`, the server's account's, under a directory no other account may write, so none can make it first), and a launch released before its agent started removes its `launch.env` at once. The missed-stop reconciler's 60-second pass removes the `launch.env` of every run of the box that is not live (asking only containers a run started or finished in since two minutes before its last pass), what a killed push left in the staging directory, and the logins of every person the roster no longer holds (asking the roster just before each delete, so a person added meanwhile keeps theirs); removing a person deletes their logins once the removal commits.
- **A refused login is said once and only once, on every box.** A person's own dispatch refused for want of their login is posted in the spec's room as Sail's machinery, as the loop narrates; it was refused on a node before ("'eng' may not post as 'sail'"). A review, fix or answered build refused for want of a login says nothing more than that one message: it no longer escalates or adds "The build could not go on with your answer … Re-dispatch it with --restart", and storing the login (or making one the default) launches the reviewer or fix, or resumes the answered build, on the box that drives it. A quoted reason that ends in a full stop is no longer followed by another.
- **On a node, a refusal is posted only where it stays.** A refusal for want of a login is posted in the spec's room only where the box's own sync would keep it: on main, or on a node holding a run of its person in that conversation. On a node with none, where sync would withdraw it a minute later, it is not posted at all; the person is told on their terminal.
- **A run whose harness refused, limited or passed over its login says so in one sentence.** When a finished run's own report says Claude Code refused the login it was given (a 401) or that its account hit its limit, or that it took its credential from another source it declares (`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `apiKeyHelper`, `/login managed key`), or Codex refused its key or hit its usage limit (the `task_complete` of the rollout's last turn, so an earlier run of a resumed session never speaks for this one), the spec's room is told once, naming the person's login by name and kind and the command that replaces it as pasted (for example "Claude Code refused uday's login 'default' (long-lived token), so its run stopped. Run claude setup-token on a machine where Claude Code is signed in, then sail agent login claude-code."); a limit says until when, as Sail renders a time it parsed, and, for a build or a fix, the branch the work stays on. A 401 on a credential Claude Code took from one of those other sources is said as that credential's, naming it, saying the stored login was never used, with no command to replace it. The same sentence is the `detail` of a new `agent_login_outcome` event (SailKit's `EventType.agentLoginOutcome`), whose `outcome` is one of `refused`, `refused_other`, `limited`, `overridden` and `ending`. It is read with the usage in the one container read a finished run already makes, and said by the write that recorded it, in that write's transaction, so the tracker, the review pipeline, a foreground run and the reconciler (its finish and its late read) say the same sentence once; a live member says it once, without saying its run stopped. Nothing the harness printed is repeated; anything else it said, "Not logged in" on a run given a stored login included (Sail's delivery failed: said to the operator on the server's log), is an ordinary failure. A reviewer or fix agent whose login was refused, its report read at once or late, holds its review while that login is still the one its person's runs start with, and the reconciler never replays its stop, instead of being retried three times and escalated with a remedy that could not work; once the person's default login for the harness is another or was stored after the run started, the next stop, `login_stored` event or reconciler pass launches it again, once, so an event lost to a restart or heard before the refusal was recorded costs one pass at most. A limit holds nothing: it resets by itself, so the run is retried and escalated as before. A reconciler pass reads the unread reports of at most three reviewers or fix agents (newest first, starting none after ten seconds, skipping one whose early read went unanswered on the pass before) before its first replay, and every other unread report after its replays; each refusal of the same review is released by the pass, not only the first. A run that ran on another credential goes on, is said once a spec, and records the source in the new `runs.login_overridden` column, part of this release's v1004 schema with no version bump, which takes only those three names, so a node's run row naming any other text is refused; a v1004 database made by a build before the column is refused at start with the reinstall named. A build's refusal ends with its next step, the `sail spec dispatch … --restart` the CLI prints for a spec not pending. A build on a Claude Code token in the last 14 of its 365 days is warned with the same remedy in the dispatch's own output, as an `agent_login_outcome` event (`ending`) and in the room, on a node at a spec's first dispatch too; its days are counted by date. `sail spec dispatch --json` carries it as `login_ending`. `GET /v1/runs` and `sail agent sessions --json` carry `login_overridden`. `RunUsageReader` is now `RunReportReader`.
- **A dry-run dispatch says what a real one would do.** It prints "Dry run: nothing was changed." and, in the conditional, the spec, agent, lane, branch and whether it would be created or checked out, and whether the spec would be restarted, with no launch banner and no command for a run that does not exist. Its `--json` is `{"dry_run": true, …, "would_create_branch", "would_restart"}`, and a live dispatch's preview now says `"dry_run": false`.
- **A dry-run dispatch reads the container as it is, and changes nothing.** Its read-only probes now run even in a dry run, so it no longer says "Project 'acme' has no container on this box" of a running container, and it reaches the login check. It rehearses the claim in a transaction it rolls back, so a dry run, `--restart` included, no longer moves the spec to `in_progress`, stamps its branch or supersedes its reviews, publishes no lifecycle event, takes no snapshot, checks out no branch and posts nothing.
- **A container's cloud-provider switch cannot outrank the stored login.** `launch.env` also sets Claude Code's `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK`, `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_VERTEX` and `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_FOUNDRY` empty: cloud-provider credentials are not supported for runs. A project's own tools that read `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` no longer see it in a run started on a token, since it is set empty there; before this release that same variable silently replaced the run's login.
- **A read-only room member's shell is narrower.** Its allow-rules name the `spec` subcommands it uses (`show`, `list`, `board`, `comments`, `comment`, `whoami`) instead of every `spec` command, and deny-rules refuse `--body-file` and `--plan-file` anywhere in a `spec` command, so a glob such as `/proc/self/enviro?` can never be handed to it.
- **A refusal for want of a login is said once per room, not per process.** The room itself decides: it is not said again while it is the newest thing Sail said there and no run of the spec has started since, across CLI attempts and restarts.
- **`launch.env` is never readable by another user, at any instant.** A run's directory is made the dev user's alone (`0700`) before its `launch.env` is pushed into it; `incus file push` writes the file before it sets its `0600`.
- **A room's run records who woke it.** `dispatched_by` is the person whose message woke a room wake or a live member; only Sail's own machinery records none.
- **No hook puts a credential on a command line.** The event helper, the session report, the room relay, the stop gate and the spec, skill and portal helpers sent `Authorization: Bearer <credential>` as a `curl -H` argument, so every hook call showed the run credential in the host's and the container's process lists. Each now sends it through one shell function, `sail_curl`, which hands curl the header as a config on a file descriptor fed by the shell's built-in `printf`: in no argv, no environment of a process it starts and no file.
- **A login's directories are the runner account's alone, `0700`, whatever the parent gives.** Each directory under `/var/lib/sail/logins` is set to this account's user and group and mode exactly `0700` when it is made and whenever it is found otherwise, so a setgid data directory no longer leaves one `2700` with the parent's group.
- **The Claude Code token's remedy no longer says `sudo claude setup-token`.** It says to run `claude setup-token` on a machine where Claude Code is signed in, then store the token with `sail agent login claude-code`.
- **`sail agent login --dry-run` says what it would store** (harness, name, kind, the default or not) and reads and stores nothing; `remove` of a login not stored says there was nothing to remove and succeeds. A thin client runs every storing `sail agent login` over a terminal, so a typed value is never echoed, and piped input is said to be read from stdin.
- **The fleet's loop stores each agent's key with `sail agent login <harness> --kind api-key`** and writes no key into the container; it checks that the build's run says `login=default`, `login_kind=api-key`, and that no key is in any process's command line while it builds.
- **A spec's run lives in that spec's own workspace, so specs no longer wait on each other's repos.** Every run of a spec (its build, reviews, fixes, a resumed build, a room wake and a live member) runs in the workspace `<p>-<spec id>`, cloned from the project's template on the box's ZFS pool when the spec's first run starts and reused, tree and all, by every run after it; a run that names no spec (an ad hoc `sail agent run`, a member of a room with no spec) still runs in the project's own container. A spec id too long for Incus's 63 characters, named `main`, ending in `-` or making a template's name is cut and given six hex characters of its SHA-256; when the project's name leaves no room for one character of a cut id (a name over 54 characters, with an id that needs the cut), the spec's dispatch is refused, naming that limit. The repo-overlap gate now compares only runs in one place: two specs over the same repo run at once, each in its own workspace under its own unit, and the gate still decides between runs of one spec and between spec-less runs in the project's container. The container lease and a restore of the project's container no longer wait on a spec's live run, and a spec's claim displaces no terminal conversation.
- **A spec's dispatch is refused before anything is reserved when its workspace cannot be had.** A box with no ZFS pool is refused naming `sudo sail host init --storage zfs`; a project with no template, naming `sudo sail project template build <p>`; a pool ZFS calls neither online nor degraded, naming `zpool status`; and a person's workspace or another spec's under the spec's workspace name, naming it. Dispatch never builds a template: one older than the definition is cloned as it is, and the spec's room is told once, naming the build that freshens it; one a build is making is waited for, and the version it built is cloned. A degraded mirror goes on, said once. A new error code, `runner_busy` (503; SailKit's `ErrorCode.runnerBusy`), refuses one more workspace when its pool is under 10% free, when as many spec workspaces hold a live run as the box's memory holds of the project's memory limit (at least one), and when another create holds the box's one create lock (`workspace-create.lock`) for more than 60 s; the run reserved for it is released.
- **One branch rule for every run of a spec.** A fresh build whose branch already exists, locally or on the remote, is refused naming `--restart`; a repo already on the branch is left as it is, uncommitted work included; a repo elsewhere with uncommitted work is refused naming where it is, and is never carried onto the branch or discarded; a clean one is put on the branch, the local one, else the remote's, else, for a build, a new one from the latest base. A workspace made for a run starts with every repo detached at its fetched base, so a room's turn on a spec never dispatched makes no branch. The dispatch's `git checkout -f` restart path is gone; ad hoc `sail agent run` checks out a branch as before and refuses one that already exists.
- **Dispatch takes no snapshot.** `sail spec dispatch --snapshot` / `--no-snapshot` and the definition's `agent.auto_snapshot` are gone, and with them the dispatch's `snapshot_created`: a spec's run never touches the project's container, and its workspace is its tree. A definition naming `auto_snapshot` is refused like any key `agent` does not have (the examples drop it), and `GET /v1/projects/{p}` no longer returns `agent.auto_snapshot`. Dispatch says `Workspace <w> made in 3.2 s` or `Workspace <w> reused`, and a dry run `Would make (or reuse) workspace <w> (<instance>)`, adding when it would clone a stale template. **Wire:** `POST /v1/projects/{p}/dispatch` answers `workspace` (the instance) where it answered `snapshot`; `sail spec dispatch --json` carries `workspace` (instance, workspace, reused, timings, stale) and a dry run `workspace` and `would_reuse_workspace`. The database schema and the sync protocol are unchanged.
- **A spec's workspace is its spec's and its person's.** The copy carries `user.sail.spec` and `user.sail.owner`, and a reused workspace becomes the owner of the run before anything of the run is staged. `sail project workspace list` (and `GET /v1/projects/{p}/workspaces`) adds `spec` and `owner` to each row; `create` records its creator as the owner; `stop` and `delete` refuse a workspace a live run of its spec is in, naming the run; `project destroy` takes the run workspaces with the rest, and a run live in one ends as any killed run does.
- **Stop and watch by spec.** `sail agent stop <p> --spec <id>` stops that spec's run; a stop naming only a project with several live runs is refused, naming each spec and the `--spec` form. The watcher's command line names `--spec`, so it probes the run's own workspace (`sail agent watch --spec`). `sail agent log` and the run log route, `sail agent status` and the agent status route, and `sail agent report` and its route read a spec's run in its workspace. `sail agent attach` resumes a spec's run's conversation in its workspace over a raw tty, as `incus exec -t <workspace>`, its process named in `~/.sail/terminal.pid`: as on main, the terminal is no run, and any dispatch, reviewer, fix or full room turn that claims the workspace ends it (the conversation stays resumable), so a person's open terminal never holds the loop. The two sides need no lock between them: a claim takes its run, then ends the terminal its pid file names; the terminal names its process, then looks for a claim and, finding one, ends itself, saying the run it yielded to and the attach that resumes it (as one a claim ends later says too). A terminal that ends removes its pid file while it still names it, so a later claim never signals a pid given to another process. A live member starts no second process on its own session while a terminal holds it, and a read-only member's turn on its own conversation runs beside a terminal, as on main (it writes no tree); the attach is refused while a run of the spec is live there; and `attach --dry-run` prints the command it runs, quoted so it runs as printed. A conversation of the project's container is resumed in a host session as before. `sail agent stop`, `log`, `status`, `report` and `attach` take one selector, `--spec <id>` for a spec's run or `--run <id>` for a run of no spec such as an ad-hoc session, and every banner and remedy that names one of them for a run prints it with its selector (the dispatch banner's `Log:` and `Stop:` lines included), so it works as printed however many runs are live; a refused stop names the command that stops each live run. A spec with several live runs (a room turn beside its build) names no one of them: `--spec` is refused naming each run's `--run`, and `--run` names a run of the project it is given with, a run of another being not found. A stop says the workspace it stopped the run in, and a stop of a run another box runs says where it runs and fails, rather than saying no session runs. A `--json` dispatch or stop refusal is JSON in the API's own error envelope, `{"error": {"code", "message", "action", …}}`, the API's own code and remedy included (a refusal the API answers is no longer `internal`), and the text form says a dispatch refusal's fix on a line of its own, as every other command does.
- **A build lost with no exit anyone saw is a failed build, never reviewed.** A build the missed-stop sweep finds gone while still recorded running, with no exit or watcher's reason, is lost only when it never recorded a process (its dispatch killed while making its workspace) or its workspace is stopped or missing when the sweep looks; one that exited with no watcher to see it (sail-api restarted) is finished and reviewed, as before. A lost build is published with `lost: true`: no review starts, the spec stays in progress, and its room says so, naming `sail spec dispatch -p <p> --spec <id> --restart`. Before, such a build was reviewed as finished, an empty branch included, and parked at `awaiting_merge`; the project's container took the same path, unchanged by the workspace move. A half-made workspace is never entered: the next entry deletes it and makes it again.
- **A spec's run that cannot go on says so in its room, and gives back what it took.** Every refusal of a spec's run at its workspace (no ZFS pool, no template, a faulted pool, a workspace name another holds, `runner_busy`) is said in the spec's room in the words the CLI and the API give, once, with the remedy of the lane it was said for: a dispatch is dispatched again, a reviewer or fix is retried by the loop, and a room's turn is engaged or posted again, never told to dispatch. A degraded pool's notice is said once, by the dispatch that made the workspace. A dispatch that cannot launch after its reservation (the box busy, the create failed, a branch collision) leaves the spec pending, so "dispatch again" works as printed. A review or fix lane that meets `runner_busy` (after at most 60 s on the create lock) ends its review in error, which the loop retries at the reconciler's pace and escalates to a person after three, as any errored review. A dispatch that meets its template's build waits for it at most 60 s, then is `runner_busy` naming the build. The cap of live spec workspaces is counted again under the box's create lock, so two creates never both pass it. The branch rule reads every repo before it moves any, so a refused build leaves no repo on its branch.
