A spec workspace name another project's instance holds is refused naming that owner and asking for another spec id.
- **A member of a spec's room is seated in the spec's own workspace.** `sail spec engage` checks the spec's workspace as a dispatch does, and asks a running one for its harness, but makes nothing: the member's first turn makes the workspace once its run is reserved, and nothing is cloned on an HTTP request. The project's container is neither required running nor snapshotted. An engage snapshot is refused for a spec's room ("A member of a spec's room works in the spec's own workspace, which a snapshot of the project's container does not hold, so no engage snapshot is taken."), `sail spec engage --snapshot` is gone, and the answer of `POST /v1/rooms/{id}/members` no longer carries `snapshot` (wire). `snapshot_created` is gone: nothing publishes it, clients may not, `agent.notifications.events` no longer accepts it (a definition naming it is refused as any unknown event is), webhooks render it no more, and SailKit's `EventType.snapshotCreated` is removed.
- **The fleet's loop gives eng a ZFS pool and acme's template before it dispatches,** and dispatches a second spec of the same repo while the first builds: it checks that both reach `awaiting_merge`, that their builds overlapped in time, and that each ran in its own workspace of acme, eng's. The access matrix's dry-run dispatches drop `--no-snapshot`.
- **A spec's workspace stops when no run needs it and goes when its spec is done, losing nothing its runs made.** A sweep of its own, each minute, decides every spec's workspace on the box from what the stores and Incus say now, never from an event, so a status that arrives by sync, a restart or a workspace stopped by hand is met on a later pass; it never holds up the missed-stop pass. A workspace is idle when no run of its spec is live on the box (a room's live member is one), Incus runs no operation on it (an exec held open by `sail project workspace shell`, `sail agent attach`, a process or a person keeps it, however long), and 10 minutes have passed since the latest of its spec's last run there ending, its last start, sail-api starting and the last minute's pass that found something in it. An idle workspace is stopped, whatever its spec's status, and also when its spec is not on the box; `sail spec dispatch -p <p> --spec <id> --restart` starts it again with its branch and tree as they were. An idle workspace whose spec is `done` or `archived` is decided once: started if stopped (and given its owner's credentials, as every start is), each run's unread usage read and recorded, and, in each repo the project's definition configures, the runs' branch pushed once when it holds commits origin lacks; then in every place git names, each worktree `git worktree list` gives (one under an ignored `.claude/worktrees/` included) and each submodule `git submodule foreach --recursive` reaches, `git status --porcelain --untracked-files=all`, `git stash list` and one read of commits over every ref (`git rev-list --exclude=refs/stash --all --not --remotes=origin`: branches, tags, every worktree's checked-out commit, another remote's refs) must all be empty (only `origin` counts), with no fetch, so a branch the forge deleted after its merge counts as pushed and is not brought back. Not read: commits only the reflog holds, files git ignores, and files outside the configured repos. Then the workspace is deleted, its dataset with it, provided the spec is still done and nothing is live when read again just before. When the reads found something (a place holding uncommitted changes, untracked files, a stash or commits origin lacks, a configured repo missing, a push origin refused), it is kept, stopped, with `user.sail.kept=<what each place holds>`, and its room is told once, as Sail, naming `sail project workspace start`, `shell` and `delete`. A failure decides nothing (a start, an Incus or git read, a usage read that fails or times out, a definition not readable here, a push that does not reach origin): the workspace is left as it was, stopped again if the sweep started it, logged once per cause, and tried again once idle, with no mark and no room line; the third failure of one cause keeps it, said once in the room in plain words (for example "could not reach origin to push <branch> from <repo>, 3 times"), so it is started at most three times (the count is in memory and starts again with sail-api). A submodule counts as pushed on any of its own remotes. A workspace whose owner was taken off the box holds no token, so a branch it cannot push keeps it at once, saying so; the next run re-owns it and clears the mark. A run's report the sweep reads late tells a done spec's room to reopen it with `sail spec dispatch -p <p> --spec <id> --restart`, not to build it again. Sail never starts, reads or deletes a kept workspace again; it stops it when idle, and its person deletes it; a run of the spec entering it clears the mark, so the spec, done again, is decided again; an attach leaves it. A spec or project erased everywhere has its workspaces deleted at once, ending any agent in them. A person's workspace, and one never made whole, are never touched. A room's member or wake no longer resumes a session that a deleted workspace took with it: it starts a new conversation, whatever deleted the workspace, and also when Incus cannot say. A dispatch, review, fix, room wake or `sail agent attach` that arrives while the sweep stops or deletes the workspace waits for it (at most 60 s, then `runner_busy` with "Then dispatch again."), and is never refused as if the spec's own instance were a stranger's. `sail agent attach` takes the workspace's lock, finds the spec's workspace and starts it when stopped, and nothing else: no ZFS pool, no template, no wait on a template build, the kept mark left; a spec with no workspace is refused naming `sail spec dispatch -p <p> --spec <id> --restart`, whose run makes one and which reopens a done spec. Every other refusal for a stopped one names `sail project workspace start <p> <w>`. A run entering a workspace the sweep stopped after its check starts it only within the box's cap. An instance Incus lists without its keys past the wait names `sudo incus info <instance>`. The launch-file tidy says nothing of a deleted or stopped workspace; a stopped one is remembered and tidied once it runs, and no longer holds the tidy's window open, so a running container is not asked again on every pass. No schema or wire change. The fleet's loop watches the second spec's workspace in the background while the row goes on: still running a minute before its idle period ends and stopped within 3 minutes after it; then, marked done, it and its dataset go within 3 minutes. The watch starts at the second spec's dispatch, so its idle period runs under the first spec's question, answer and review, and it marks the spec done only once the row has listed both workspaces; the row reads both verdicts at its end, waiting at most 40 and 5 minutes for them.
- **A dead launch's `launch.env` is no longer left in its container.** The reconciler's tidy took a stopped container's answer for done and asked a container only on the first pass after a run there started or finished, when its file could still be too young for the tidy's one-minute age filter. A container that does not run the tidy is now asked again on a later pass, and each pass asks every container a run started or finished in since two minutes before the previous pass, so a file is asked for until it is old enough to go.
- **Codex takes only an `api-key` login.** The `token` kind (`CODEX_ACCESS_TOKEN`) is no longer offered: Codex reads that variable as an agent identity, and nothing showed a run starts on one. `sail agent login codex --kind token` is refused: "Codex CLI takes no login of kind 'token'; it takes api-key." A run's `launch.env` still sets `CODEX_ACCESS_TOKEN` empty, so a value the container exports cannot outrank the stored key.
- **A read-only room member is refused the shell's environment readers by Sail's own rules.** Its deny-rules now name `printenv`, `env`, `set`, `export`, `declare` and every read under `/proc`, which Claude Code's own checks refuse today, so they stay refused whatever a later Claude Code approves. Codex has no read-only lane.
- **A run records only a login kind a harness takes.** The v1004 schema checks `runs.login_kind` is `api-key` or `token` (or none), so a launch or a synced run naming any other is refused.
- **One failed read of this process's ids no longer fails every later login store and push.** `PrivateDirectory` reads `/proc/self/status` on each call instead of once in a static initializer, where a failure made every later call throw `NoClassDefFoundError` until the process restarted.
- **The fleet gate no longer registers a workstation key nothing uses.** The `eng registers its workstation key` check went with the in-container SSH step that needed it.
- **A native terminal view on the Mac draws libghostty-vt's screen with Metal and types into it as a native terminal does; a "Sail Terminal Preview" app is built for the owner to judge.** A new Swift package, `clients/SailApp` (macOS 15, Apple silicon), holds `SailTerminalView`, an AppKit view over a terminal session: JetBrains Mono 2.304 (vendored with its licence, pinned by checksum) at 15 pt, ⌘+ ⌘− ⌘0 through Mast's ladder from 10 to 32; Mast's terminal colours dark and light; box drawing and block elements drawn as pixel sprites that meet at cell edges; wide characters and colour emoji across two cells, an emoji sequence, a flag or a skin-toned emoji as one cluster (grapheme clustering, mode 2027, is now on in every SailVT screen from its creation and through every reset); the cursor hollow without focus; selection by drag, double and triple click and Shift (a Shift-click or a drag after a double or triple click extends by whole words or lines), copy with wrapped lines joined, paste through the screen's encoder from the bottom of the screen with a sheet naming the line count (CR, LF or CR LF) before text that could run commands, neither button answering Return (so none is drawn in the system accent) and Escape cancelling; the wheel to the program, to `less` as arrow keys, or through the history, which stays put while output arrives until the person types; hover reports once per cell to a program that asks for every motion, and a modifier alone to a program that asks for every key; input methods and dead keys composing inline with the candidate window at the cursor, nothing sent until the commit; a ⌘ chord no menu takes sends nothing; focus reports; resize at once, and a new cell size reported to a program that asked. Output that arrives while the view is idle is drawn at once, and otherwise on the next display refresh, with no batching; the display link pauses when nothing changes or no drawable comes, and a view waiting for a drawable asks again when its window is seen again (or after 0.25 s while it is visible), never on output. The cursor and composing text are placed before the rows, and a frame with anything left undrawn is drawn again at the next refresh. Input is sent off the main thread, in order. Programs cannot write the clipboard. A view draws one session for the session's life. New glyphs are rasterized 32 a frame at most, the rest on the next refreshes, and a full atlas gives up its least recently used slot in constant time. The portable part (layout, atlas bookkeeping, triple-buffer ledger, sprites, key and pointer rules, latency marks) is SailKit's new `SailSurface`, tested on Linux; SailVT adds `Screen.tracksMouse` and `Graphemes.cells(_:)`; `TerminalSession.screenChanges` now carries when the screen took the output, and `TerminalSession.setCellPixels(width:height:)` sends what a new cell size owes the program. `SailPreview` opens a shell in the home folder through the bundled `sail _pty-host --local` (started when no host answers, kept running after the app quits, its own refusal shown when it will not start) and attaches to it again on the next launch; ⌥⌘L shows the latency of key to echo on the display, and its estimate where the display gives no presentation time. The `Clients` workflow's new `SailApp on macOS` job runs every test suite the package lists, renders golden frames offscreen, drives the view with synthesized events, drives the preview against the darwin `sail`'s real local host with latency budgets, and builds and bundles the preview; it signs and notarizes it when all six signing secrets are present, and otherwise uploads it as an unsigned build. The job, and the SailKit jobs, run only when `clients/` or the workflow changed.
- **The preview's drive says whose an output's wait was.** The flood's gate, every output shown within 100 ms, records the flood's latency figures in its report like a measure step, and its line names the longest refresh, the outputs drawn late, and the longest the main thread and a thread of the drive's own went without running over the same span (`mainThreadHeld`, `ownThreadHeld`, in each measure too). A wait the drive's own thread shares was the machine's; one only the main thread shares was the main thread's, inside a refresh when the longest refresh shares it (a wait for a drawable); one neither shares was the view's. The gate is unchanged.
- **The Sail app's terminals split, tab and rename as Ghostty's do.** "This Mac" holds tabs, in the app's own strip in the title bar's row after the window buttons, and each tab a tree of splits: File > New Tab (⌘T), Split Right (⌘D), Split Down (⇧⌘D), Close (⌘W, which now closes the focused terminal, not the window), Close Tab (⌥⌘W), Close Window (⇧⌘W); View > Rename Tab; Window > Zoom Split (⇧⌘↩), Select Previous and Next Split (⌘[ ⌘]), Select Split (⌥⌘ and an arrow), Resize Split (⌃⌘= equalizes, ⌃⌘ and an arrow moves the nearest divider 10 pt), Show Previous and Next Tab (⇧⌘[ ⇧⌘]) and ⌘1 to ⌘8 and ⌘9. A divider drags, and no drag, key or equalize leaves any terminal under 10 columns by 3 rows (a window made smaller than that clips them instead, and there a move that gives a squeezed terminal room is still taken); a tab is clicked, double-clicked to rename in place (Return, a click elsewhere or another tab selected names it, Escape leaves it, an empty name clears it) and dragged to reorder; tabs share the strip down to 120 pt, and past that the strip shows the run around the selected tab, with `+` and an empty stretch that moves the window as the title bar does. A tab shows its name, else its focused terminal's program title, else "This Mac". A new tab or split opens like the focused terminal, in the folder its shell reported (OSC 7, `file://` or `kitty-shell-cwd://` on this Mac) or else where it opened. Unfocused terminals beside another lie under the terminal background at 30%; the first click on one only focuses it. Closing a terminal ends its session and asks first ("Close this terminal?", or for a tab "Its N terminals will end."), unless its shell marks its prompts and commands (OSC 133 A, B, C and D) and the cursor is at its prompt now, with no command still running; a terminal not attached (connecting, reconnecting or parked) asks too, then is ended at the host on a connection of its own, or, if the host cannot be reached, opens again as a last tab at the next launch; a program that exits closes its terminal, unless it fails within 2 s of opening, and an ended session keeps its terminal and banner until ⌘W. Quitting ends nothing, and the next launch puts back every tab and split whose session the host still lists live, with a live `mac-*` session no pane holds as a last tab; closing the last tab leaves "No terminal is open" and New Terminal. SailKit gains `SailWorkbench`, the arrangement as a value (Linux-tested, 10,000 random command sequences), kept in user defaults as `workbench.arrangement` (version 1); `terminal.session` is no longer read. The renderer compiles its shaders once per process. The workbench takes its own shortcuts before the menu bar (which highlights its title first, 80 to 180 ms on a hosted runner); the menu stays the list of commands and keys.
- **The Sail app signs in with a passkey.** The Mac app is now "Sail" (`sh.standardapplied.sail`): one window in the Sail look, an icon rail with "This Mac" (the local terminal the preview had, signed in or not) and the account. Type the control plane's address, paste a ticket from `sail fde enroll <handle>` (or the link it printed) and Enroll with a Ticket, then Sign In with Passkey: two Touch ID sheets, a passkey for `sail.standardapplied.com`. The window then says who you are, your role, the control plane and until when; the session token is kept in the data-protection Keychain and nowhere else; reopening keeps you signed in, Sign Out (also in the Sail menu) forgets the token on this Mac, and a session the server ended signs you out with a sentence. The window's title bar is the app's own (the rail and the pane run to the top edge under the system's buttons), text on the terracotta accent is white and bold in the light appearance, and the app has its icon. No redirect is ever followed: a control plane that answers with one is told so. Plain `http://` is taken only to an IP address in a private range (loopback, RFC 1918, 100.64.0.0/10, `::1`, `fc00::/7`); anything else is `https://`. Every refusal says what happened, what to do, the server's words and the system's. SailKit gains the passkey routes, the address rule and `Account` (Linux-tested); the transport is ephemeral by default. `ClientGoldensTest` records the passkey answers and their refusals. The `Clients` workflow takes a seventh secret, `APPLE_PROVISIONING_PROFILE_BASE64`: the profile is embedded and the app alone claims its App ID, team and `webcredentials:sail.standardapplied.com`; every build checks the shipped binary holds no software passkey and the bundled `sail` no entitlement, signed builds check the entitlements and the profile, the drive enrolls and signs in with a software passkey against two control planes it starts, and the artifact is the zip itself, `Sail-<sha>.zip` (or `Sail-unsigned-<sha>.zip`), uploaded unarchived so one unzip gives `Sail.app`; CI checks that download as Finder unpacks it.
- **The native app's terminal core: a Swift session reaches any host and keeps its screen in libghostty-vt.** SailKit gains `SailVT`, the screen of one terminal session kept by libghostty-vt 1.0.1 (the rows, cells, resolved colours and cursor a renderer draws, which rows changed, and the bytes a key, a paste, a focus change, the mouse or a scroll encode to, each encoder synced from the terminal's modes first), and `SailTerminal`, the session client: it opens or attaches a session on this machine's own host (`sail _pty-host --local`, its unix socket, a blank hello) or on a runner, through a fresh ticket from `POST /v1/terminal/tickets` for every TCP connection, with one code path for both, and lists a host's sessions (`SessionListing.sessions(at:)`). It keeps the session attached through drops (reconnects 0.5, 1, 2, 4 and 8 s apart, then parks until `reconnectNow()`; a connection lost within 5 s of attaching counts as a failed try, and a host that breaks the protocol ends the session naming what it sent, an applied input number no client could have sent included, so no host frame can crash the app), finds a dead TCP connection within 10 s when anything it sent is unacknowledged and within 45 s when quiet (keepalive after 30 s idle, one probe every 30 s), and at once on `reconnectNow()`, which re-sends the pty size the host has and so never resizes anything (taking the write token sends the view's size once), replays onto a clean screen, ends a session whose host restarted as "the host restarted" and a refused attach in the host's own words, drops and counts input typed while not attached (`droppedInputs`, exact) and estimates input written into a connection that died (`possiblyLostInputs`), sends a size once it has held for 60 ms, and marks each key's latency on a monotonic clock. A paste with line breaks into a program that did not ask for bracketed paste is answered "needs confirmation" and not sent. Synchronized output (mode 2026) holds the frame a renderer reads until the program's update is whole, or 1 s has passed, when the screen ticks so a renderer drawing on change draws again; a frame waits behind at most one 16 KiB slice of output however fast a program floods the screen (`frame()` is meant for once per display refresh; a reader polling in a tight loop slows output); a change of the default colours redraws every cell. `sail-term-probe` drives a session with no window from a JSON script (type, wait for text, resize, drop, detach, attach, crash the local host, measure echoed keys) and leaves a JSON report; its token comes from the environment variable `--token-env` names, never the command line. libghostty-vt is pinned by checksum: the xcframework on Apple platforms, and on Linux the release's source and deps tarballs and Zig 0.17.1, built offline by `clients/SailKit/scripts/libghostty-vt`, which refuses any download that does not match its pin. A new CI job, `Terminal end to end`, runs the terminal tests and the probe against this commit's own local host and a box's ticketed door; the macOS lane runs them against the v0.68.0 darwin binary's local host, and the overhead bench.
- **A session shim stopped for not claiming its terminal leaves nothing it started running.** `Pty.spawn` killed the shim and what it had started but waited only for the shim, so a killed child of it could still be there when the refusal returned (seen in 326 of 1,500 tries). It now waits, within the same one-second bound, for all of them.
- **A node knows a person by main's id, so a handle given to someone new never inherits what the last holder stored.** Main's roster now sends each person's id, and the ids alone of everyone else it holds; a node records main's id on the person's row (`fdes.main_id`). A person main described whose id main no longer holds (removed, or their handle given to someone new under a new id) is removed on the node with their stored harness logins, git tokens, API tokens and box claim, and the pass disowns their workspaces; the new holder of the handle starts with nothing. A person who only left a scoped node's projects is withdrawn there, disabled and their name and email forgotten, but what they stored stays and works again once they are listed again. No round that leaves out the box's own person removes anyone. A row made on the box before it joined takes main's id only when it is the box's own person or has the same, non-blank email; otherwise the box's first round with that main is refused, naming both emails and the commands that fix it (a node may now correct or remove a person main never described), and a later round gives the handle to main's person and moves the local row aside, renamed `<handle>.local-<4 hex>` and disabled with everything it stored, saying so with the command that removes it; no round deletes a person main never described. A person main never described is only disabled, and the box's own person is never removed or disabled by a round. A box whose key main refuses after it has synced is told main removed its person, and the line that brings it back; `sail host status` says so too, `sail sync --json` prints a refused round as the API's error envelope, and a person taken off every project the box holds is told so rather than to sync again. The roster's lines name whom a round removed or withdrew, never an email. A scoped node is now sent the emails of the people who share its projects, so one of them can own a workspace there. A workspace's label holds its owner's id beside the handle, so the pass judges it by that stored id at every pass and after a restart. Taking a person's credentials out of a workspace is now the give's own strip run in the container (a stopped workspace started for it), one list of what a give writes, idempotent and checked by what is left: the host-side deletes it replaced stopped at the first file that was not there on Incus 7.6 ("file does not exist") and left the gh login, the git identity and the owner label behind. A clone's fetch waits for its remote's host to resolve by asking the resolver, and the template's check reads gh's login file, instead of either reading a tool's words. The SSH gateway pins each key to its person by this box's id for them, not by handle, so after a round moves a handle aside or main gives it to someone new, the old person's key is refused rather than logging in as the handle's new holder; every sync round rewrites the gateway's keys when the roster it leaves differs, with a line for active people only, and a line written by an earlier release is read by handle until `migrate` regenerates the file. A workspace a removal cannot strip is tried three times an idle period apart, then left and said once; its next start strips it first, then makes it nobody's when the handle is now someone else's, where before a start gave it to the handle's new holder, or refuses as before when its person is not active here, where before it was left running with their credentials. Moving a person aside no longer loops for ever when every handle it draws is taken. On a node, the box's own person may now remove or correct a person main never described (`sudo sail fde rm`, `sudo sail fde update --email`) whatever their role on main, so the remedies a round prints work on a member's box; anyone else still needs to manage people. A round refused because main no longer knows the box's person is now recorded (its kind was missing from `sync_health`'s check, and the failure to record it was dropped), so `sail sync status` and `sail host status` say so with the line that brings them back; a failure that cannot be recorded is said once. A box pointed at a main it never synced with is told that main has not authorised it yet, not that it removed its person. The API's login doors tell a person taken off every project the box holds that they are, as the CLI does. `sail project workspace list` shows a workspace whose stored owner id is no active person's as `removed`, never under the handle's new holder. The move-aside line names handles only. A workspace start whose give fails stops the workspace again, so no run lands in a running workspace without its owner's credentials; every start passes through that one place. `sail init --main <other>` on a box that follows a main says what following the other does, with counts, and asks or takes `--yes`. A database an earlier build of this release made is refused at start naming the reinstall when its sync health check lacks a kind. The 24-hour count after which a disabled person's logins were erased is gone. A main rebuilt by adding its people again mints new ids, so its nodes take them as new people who store their logins again; a main restored from its database file keeps them. The roster entry gains `id` and sync protocol 9, unreleased, is not bumped; a v1004 database made before `fdes.main_id` is refused at the start, naming the reinstall.
- **A template holds no one's credentials, and each workspace gets its owner's when it is made.** A template build still clones with its caller's tokens, but sets no git identity and trusts no key, and before its stop one root step removes `/home/dev/.git-credentials`, gh's `hosts.yml`, the dev user's `user.name`/`user.email` and every `authorized_keys` line; a check then proves it holds nothing by content: none of the build's tokens anywhere on its root filesystem (grep reads them on stdin; an empty one fails the check), no git identity at system or global level, no line in any home's `authorized_keys`, no gh login. Anything found, or a search that errors, fails the build with no record and no snapshot, the version before staying current; a failed build is stripped as well, and the refusal names what it found (never a value, at most three files and how many more) and says to remove what wrote it, a step of the definition or a hand change, before building again. A template's digest is its definition alone, so a workstation key revoked or replaced no longer makes it stale, and its build no longer takes the box's pairing lock. Every workspace is given its owner's credentials after its start and before its prepare, so its fetch uses their token: their git tokens for the project's https hosts (only those, so no token of the process's environment ever lands in one), `user.name` and `user.email` from their roster row, their `fde_ssh_keys` as `authorized_keys`, and the box's workstation key only when they are the box's person; gh is logged in after the prepare, once its host resolves. A workspace another person owns has its owner cleared, is stripped and given the new owner's, and is recorded theirs last, so one cut short is stripped again and left nobody's; the owner is read again when their credentials are given, and refused there too if their token or email went since the check. A run's workspace, or a person's `project workspace create`, is refused before anything is reserved or copied when it would be made or re-owned for a person with no email ("… has no email on this box, and a workspace commits as its owner. Set it on main with sail fde update <h> --email <email>.", or for anyone but an admin "Ask an admin to run on main: …") or who keeps no token for a repo host that asks for credentials ("github.com asks for credentials to fetch acme's repo api, and ana keeps no token for it. Store one with sail git login github.com."). New: `sail git login <host>` (stdin), `sail git login list [--fde]`, `sail git login remove <host> [--fde]`, kept in `/var/lib/sail/logins/<fde-id>/git/<host>` as harness logins are, erased with the person, reachable through the gateway lane: a git token is a login of `git` named by its host, served by the login doors themselves (`PUT /v1/logins/git/{host}`, `DELETE /v1/logins/git/{host}`, listed by `GET /v1/logins`) under Keep logins, now "harness logins and git tokens", and announced as any login is (`login_stored`, `login_removed`, harness `git`). A token stored, replaced or removed reaches every running workspace of its person at once, written beside the old store and renamed over it under the workspace's lock (a strip or a disown also takes such a file left by a change cut short), never stripped, so a live run never meets a workspace without a token, and a stopped one when it next starts (`sail project workspace start`, or a run entering it); one it could not reach is said in the answer. A pairing's key, and one set with `host config set ssh-public-key`, replaces the old in every container and reaches the project's containers and the box's person's workspaces only; `sail fde key add` reaches that person's workspaces; `sail fde key rm` takes the key out of every container and workspace; a person removed from a box's roster loses their token store, gh login, keys and ownership in every workspace they owned, and the logins and git tokens they stored there, at sail-api's next pass (and at its start), which lists Incus only when someone stopped being active. Nobody stores a login for another person: `PUT /v1/logins/…?fde=<other>` is refused, an admin's too; a git token is at least 16 characters. A workspace's new SSH host keys are renamed over the template's rather than made after removing them, and sshd is restarted onto them once its unit has finished starting, saying the unit's result when it fails, which ten workspaces made at once raced before. A template build's identity step says it wrote none. A `--json` refusal of a client command now carries the API's error code and an action naming each command whole; a git token may be stored for a host with a port. The project's own container is unchanged.
- **A git token goes only to the host it was stored for, and Sail reads none from the environment.** The one source of a token is a person's `sail git login <host>`, matched to a repo's host exactly, port included: a token stored for `gitlab.com` is never sent to `gitlab.attacker.example`, and nothing is matched by a word in a host's name or by a wildcard. `sail project apply`, `repo add`, `demo`, `template build` and `workspace create` clone with the box person's stored tokens and write them into the project's container by the same step a workspace gets its owner's; they no longer read `GITHUB_TOKEN`, `GITLAB_TOKEN` or `BITBUCKET_TOKEN`, and apply no longer prompts for a token. A repo whose host asks for credentials with no token stored is refused before anything is written, naming `sail git login <host>`, and when one of those variables is set the refusal says "GITHUB_TOKEN is set but Sail does not read it. Store it: sail git login <host>." The `--git-token` option is gone from all five commands, so no token is ever on a command line. `repo add` writes the store an apply writes, every host the project's repos name: it used to write the added repo's host alone, so a fetch from an earlier repo on another host failed until the next apply. Each of the five checks first, before it creates, starts, writes or asks anything, and under `--json` the refusal carries `login_not_stored`; on a box that names no person it names the setup that makes one before the login. The project's container now loses a token as it gains one: an apply with nothing to give removes its `.git-credentials` and gh's login, and gh stays logged in to exactly the GitHub hosts with a token, by the step a workspace's token change takes. Credentials typed into the project's container by hand go too, since Sail keeps only what `sail git login` stored, and the apply says what it removed and why in one line. A repo URL that carries a credential is refused naming its host alone, never echoing it. A public repo, the demo's included, needs none. `ci/fleet`'s storage and loop rows store the forge token with `sail git login github.com` before applying and building.
- **`spec comment` takes its flags in any order.** Inside a container, `spec comment <id> --question --body <text>` was refused with "comment needs --body <text>|-" because `--body` had to come first, though that is the order Sail's own prompts and README teach an agent; a build that could not ask its question posted a plain comment, which holds nothing, and was reviewed as finished. `--body`, `--reply-to` and `--question` are now read in whatever order they come, and a comment with no `--body` is still refused.

## 0.68.0

- **Reinstall every box to take this release: its database schema changed (v1003) and no v1 database before it is converted.** A box on 0.59.0 to 0.67.0 refuses to start on 0.68.0 with "Database schema v1002 was made by an earlier Sail release; this release opens only v1003 and converts none: the box is reinstalled." and the remedy to move the database aside and run `sail init`. Nothing is half-upgraded: the refused database is left exactly as it was. The sync wire is protocol 8: a 0.68.0 main refuses a 0.67.0 node at the hello and tells it to reinstall (a 0.58 node, whose database `sail upgrade` still converts in place, is told to upgrade as before; a node on any other older release, 0.57 included, to reinstall), so a staggered reinstall never syncs runs or specs through a box that drops their cost and times. Export any measures you want to keep (`sudo sail spec list --all-projects --status done --measures --json`) before reinstalling.
- **A shipped change records what it cost where it happened: tokens and dollars per run, when it became ready and done, and the minutes people gave it.** Each harness reads its own report of a run (`Harness.usage`): Claude Code the last `result` line of the run's stream-json log (tokens summed over `modelUsage`, so subagents count, dollars from `total_cost_usd`, the model that cost most), Codex its rollout transcript's last `token_count` and model (no dollars). The finish writes it, with the model reported and the effort passed, in the update that makes the run's row terminal; a report that says nothing, or a report file that is not there, is `usage_source: none` with null counts, never zeros. A harness session's report is its running total, so a run records its share: the report less what the session's other runs were given, kept per session on the box (`session_usage`) where retention never reaches, and a resumed run's session is stamped at launch. A report below what its session already reported (an earlier run finishing late, a harness that started counting over) tells no share: it is `usage_source: unattributable`, adds nothing, and its spec's tokens and dollars are served as a lower bound. A live member sets each turn's share and keeps the last as it finishes. Runs carry `model`, `effort`, `tokens_in`, `tokens_out`, `tokens_cache_read`, `tokens_cache_write`, `cost_usd` and `usage_source`, synced with the run as its executing box's fact. The report is read in one command bounded at 10 s: for each of the harness's markers, the last lines of the file holding it (`tac | grep -m 16 -a -F` per marker, so a Codex turn's many counts never hide its model), so a long log with no result line is `none` at once. A last report line that does not parse is `none`, said once on stderr; a raw C1 control, U+FFFE or U+FFFF in it, which JSON allows, parses. Only a report whose container did not answer (stopped, missing or silent past 10 s) is left unread, never taken for none: retention keeps the run's directory, and each reconciler sweep reads it again until it is answered. An operator's stop no longer waits on reading it.
- **A spec's ready, awaiting-merge and done times are its moves' own, the same on every box.** `ready_at` is its first move into `pending`, written once; `awaiting_merge_at` its latest move into `awaiting_merge`; `done_at` its latest move into `done`, cleared when it is reopened and kept when it is archived. Each is stamped from the moving revision and carried in the snapshot like `archived_at`, so main and every node hold the author's times. Measures read them, and `collapsed_by_sync` and `history_incomplete` are gone.
- **A spec's runs are counted where retention cannot erase them from under it.** Each box keeps, on the spec, the runs, builds, fixes, runs without usage or dollars, tokens and dollars of every finished run it holds, folded as the run finishes; erasing a run lowers nothing. Build and fix runs still running are counted as rework under way. A box that may lack a run serves its run figures as a lower bound (`at_least`): one that first held a spec after main erased a run of its project made after the spec, and one that adopted main's erasure of a run it never saw finish. Tokens and dollars are exact only when every run counted reported them: a lower bound when some did not, and unknown (`runs_not_reported`) when none did. Two boxes never serve two different exact numbers for one spec.
- **The minutes people give a change are recorded on the box that saw them.** A box-local `attention(person, conversation, minute, channel)` table, never synced, holds one row per minute: a person's accepted input in a room-bound terminal (`terminal_input`, told by the terminal host once a minute per connection), a post (`post`), a review decision or a status move (`review_act`), and `POST /v1/attention {"conversation": ...}` from a client whose view is focused with recent input (`focus`). Only a person on a person's own lane counts, never the credential in a container or a run's principal; the server stamps the person and the minute, and a conversation the caller may not read answers as a missing one. Nothing else about a person is stored.
- **Measures show cost, budget and human minutes.** `sail spec show`, `sail spec list --status done --measures` and their routes now serve the runs' tokens and dollars with the runs behind each, the project's `agent.budget: {tokens, usd}` (both optional, a bad value refused naming the key) and `over_budget` (display only), human minutes as distinct (person, minute) pairs in the spec's own conversation by channel, with the count of people and the box named, always a lower bound, and "no recorded human code act" also requires no `terminal_input` minute in the spec's own conversation. The listing summarizes tokens and dollars over the values known exactly, counting the lower bounds and unknowns it leaves out, and human minutes as this box counted them.
- **A reasoning effort is passed or refused, never dropped.** Claude Code is launched with `--effort <level>` (it takes `low`, `medium`, `high` and `xhigh`); Codex keeps `model_reasoning_effort`. An effort the spec's harness does not take is refused naming the harness, as `invalid_request`, before any run is reserved: at the spec door when the agent or effort changes (a spec already holding one still moves and is retitled), the dispatch door, a room wake and the launch; the old "dropping reasoning_effort" warning is gone.
- **SailKit sends the focus heartbeat.** `Endpoint.attention(conversation:)` posts it, and `FocusHeartbeat` says when one is due: at most once a minute, only while the view is focused and the person gave input in the last minute.
- **A box with two disks gets a ZFS pool mirrored across them, so losing one disk loses no workspace.** `sudo sail host init --storage zfs --disk <a> --disk <b>` makes one pool that is a ZFS mirror of the two: Incus is handed both in its own mirror source (`incus storage create <pool> zfs source=mirror=<a>,<b>`, by their `/dev/disk/by-id` paths where the disks have a hardware identity), and runs one `zpool create -m none -O compression=on <pool> mirror <a> <b>`, never `-f`: every refusal, Sail's or zpool's checks of both devices, leaves both untouched, and a failure while zpool labels them can leave the first with its start zeroed or a new empty table and no pool (both were blank, so nothing is lost; a re-run says what it finds). A zpool already imported under the pool's name is refused before Incus is asked, for one device and a mirror, since zpool labels the devices before it finds the name taken. Each device is cleared by the one decision a single device is (`PoolDevices.cleared`): lsblk and the `/proc` walk are read once for both, every slow read of both comes before either device's probes, and at the re-check before the create the probes of both are the last commands; a refusal names which device and why, and nothing is made unless both pass. Two partitions of one disk, two paths to one disk (one WWN or serial) and two namespaces of one NVMe controller are refused: a mirror on one disk dies with it, and every NVMe persistent disk of a GCP VM is such a namespace, so those cannot be mirrored. Devices whose sizes differ by more than the 16 MiB `zpool create` mirrors without force are refused before anything is installed, naming the difference and both sizes (in bytes when they round alike); within that the output says how much of the larger goes unused. An Incus without the `storage_zfs_vdev` API extension (Ubuntu's 6.0.0) is refused before the devices are cleared again, and a dry run says so rather than what it would create; the refusal names what moves it, Zabbly's apt source and then `sudo sail host incus upgrade`, since apt itself will not replace the packages Sail holds; that command runs on a box whose first `host init` has not finished, which has no database and no session host's socket, so no agent run or terminal session for the restart to end, and it warns of none. `host init` and `host status` say `a mirror of <a> and <b>` in place of "one device: no redundancy". Re-running the command that made a mirror, in either order of its two `--disk` flags, is a no-op; one of the two, or another pair, is refused by name. What a pool is on is what ZFS lists for its zpool now, each whole-disk device as its disk, not the names Incus recorded at creation (kernel names that can move across a reboot or outlive a `zpool replace`): the re-run compares with those, and `host init` and `host status` say them; a zpool that is not imported, or does not answer, is said as Incus recorded it, saying so. A log, cache, spare, special or dedup device added with `zpool add` is no member of the mirror: the two-disk re-run still keeps the pool, and `host status` names it on a row of its own (`plus a log device /dev/sde`; `auxiliary_devices` in `--json`), while it stays a device in use. With two `--disk`, one refusal names what is wrong with each device, every command for both in the order to run them; a whole disk that holds only what a destroyed zpool left on it (zpool's table, its data partition and the 8 MiB reserved partition) is refused naming `sudo zpool labelclear <data partition>`, then `sudo wipefs --all <disk>`, which return it to blank; and the labels of a pool that can still be imported name `zpool labelclear -f`, since labelclear refuses an exported pool's device without it, for one `--disk` too. One `--disk` runs the same commands and says the same as before.
- **`host status` shows a ZFS pool's health as ZFS says it now.** Under each zfs pool's row it gives the state `zpool status -P` gives (ONLINE, DEGRADED, FAULTED, SUSPENDED, or not imported), each device that is not online on a row of its own, and on a DEGRADED pool what mends each device: `sudo zpool online <pool> <device>` for one the operator took offline, `sudo zpool replace <pool> <device> <new device>` for one gone or failed; `--json` carries it as `health` and `unhealthy`. Every read of zpool (`status`, `list`, the `import` listing) is given 10 s, and a pool whose zpool has not answered is asked nothing more in that command, so a hung pool costs 10 s and two cost 20 s: `host status` says it did not answer, shows the pool as Incus recorded it and goes on; `host init` refuses, changing nothing, as it does when the read that decides whether a device is free does not answer. Workspace and template verbs that change something go on on a DEGRADED mirror, saying once that it is, and are refused on a mirror in any other state, or whose health zpool does not tell, naming it and `sudo zpool status <pool>`; a pool of one device or a file is left to Incus there as before. Sail replaces and resilvers nothing.
- **A pool Incus did not finish making, or cannot start, is never taken for one that exists.** When incusd stops between its `zpool create` and marking the pool created, Incus 7.6 shows the pool `Pending` while it runs and `Unavailable` once it is back; a re-run of `host init` said "already exists", put the base profile on it and said "Server ready" on a box where nothing could launch. A pool Incus reports `Pending`, `Errored` or `Unavailable` is now refused, for one device, a mirror and a dir pool alike, at the flags and again before the pool step, before any profile and with no "ready", naming the status and the remedy. An `Unavailable` zfs pool whose record names no zpool is that half-made pool: `sudo incus storage delete <pool>` removes the record and leaves the zpool, then `sudo zfs list -r <pool>` and, if it lists only the pool itself, `sudo zpool destroy <pool>`, and the next run names what the zpool left on the disks. Any other `Unavailable` pool was made, and the refusal points at `sudo incus storage show <pool>` and `sudo zpool status <zpool>` to mend it. `Pending` and `Errored` name `sudo incus storage delete <pool>`, which for `Pending` leaves the zpool to destroy and for `Errored` destroys the one its record names when it holds only Incus's own datasets. `Unknown` and every other status are kept as before.
- **A template that needs a token names the sudo that keeps only that token.** Its refusal said to keep `GITHUB_TOKEN` through `sudo -E`, which keeps the whole environment, the operator's HOME and XDG directories among it, so root's incus calls left root-owned `~/.config/incus` and `~/.cache/incus` in the operator's home; it now names `sudo --preserve-env=GITHUB_TOKEN`, which keeps that variable and gives root its own home.
- **The incus CI shards run the Incus `host init` installs.** They took Ubuntu's incus 6.0.0, which cannot make a mirror; they now install Zabbly's stable repository, as every box does, unpinned: CI tests Zabbly's current stable release, and Ubuntu's 6.0.0 is covered only by the unit tests of Sail's refusal of it. Root's `incus admin init` there runs with root's own home and no XDG directories, as a box's sudo runs it, so it leaves nothing in the runner's home.

## 0.67.0

- **A project can run several workspaces on one box, each a clone of its template, made in about three seconds.** On a box with a ZFS pool, `sudo sail project workspace create <project> <name>` makes a workspace: its own container `<project>-<name>`, with its own tree, services and ports, cloned (`incus copy <project>--tmpl/v<n> <project>-<name>`, a ZFS clone whose `origin` is the snapshot) from the project's template and started, then made its own in one `incus exec`: a new machine-id, the project's name as its hostname so the in-container `spec`, event and portal helpers work unchanged, new SSH host keys made under it (a clone has the template's), every repo fetched (asked again until 10 s have passed while its host does not resolve, as a fresh clone's resolver lags its address, git in the C locale, the retries counted in `ready_steps.fetch_retries`) and switched to a branch named after the workspace (the remote's when it has one, else a new one from the repo's branch or the remote's default), sshd restarted onto the new keys, the dev user's bus waited for, the shared files pushed, sail's machinery reconciled, and last a ready mark, written with the template's own keys cleared from the clone. On a 4-vCPU cloud VM the clone takes about half a second and the whole create about three (`Created fix-login from v1 in 3.2 s (clone 0.5 s, start 0.4 s, ready 2.3 s)`), each phase measured on `System.nanoTime`; `--json` carries them as `timings` in milliseconds, the readiness step by step as `ready_steps`. A create that fails after the copy deletes the instance; one interrupted harder leaves a workspace without its ready mark, which is listed as `incomplete`, never started or entered, and replaced by the next create of its name; one whose mark was taken off by hand is refused by `create`, `start` and `shell` alike, naming `workspace delete`. Two creates of one name make one workspace and refuse the other; two of different names build a missing or stale template once and both clone it. The template is the stopped instance `<project>--tmpl`, which `sudo sail project template build <project>` (or a create that finds none or a stale one) provisions with the project's own provisioner on the box's ZFS pool (the base profile's when it is ZFS, else the one beside it; several beside a pool that is not ZFS are refused naming them; an existing template stays where it is), born with `user.sail.template.of`, recorded with `user.sail.template.current`, `user.sail.template.inputs` (the digest of the definition and the workstation key that apply records) and the highest version it was ever given, then snapshotted as `v<n>`; it does nothing when the current version was built from the definition as it is, a stale one is converged on its disk as apply converges a container, and a failed build leaves the previous version current, makes no snapshot and stops a template it started. A build whose https repos ask for credentials no token was given for is refused before it starts, naming `--git-token`, asked of every repo at once within a few seconds, following no redirect, and outside the box's pairing lock; only a `401`, or a `403` carrying an authentication challenge, counts as asking for one. `sail project workspace list <project>` lists `main`, the project's own container, then every workspace, with its state (`frozen` for a paused one, `incomplete` for an unfinished one), template version, created time and IPv4; `shell`, `stop`, `start` (which says what its resume took) and `delete` act on the one instance named `<project>-<name>` that carries that workspace's keys, and refuse, naming them, any other instance carrying them, such as a hand copy. `delete` asks once it finds the workspace, naming it and that its uncommitted work is lost (`--yes`), and refuses a missing one or `main` without asking; `create`, `delete` and `template build` are root's, as `apply` and `destroy` are. `main` is never written to, copied, snapshotted or restarted: every verb but `list` refuses it naming the project verb (`delete main` names `project destroy`). A name is the project-name rule, not `main`, not ending in `-`, at most 63 characters as an instance and no instance, project or template name already taken, each refused naming the conflict. A box with no ZFS pool refuses every verb that makes or changes a workspace before anything changes, naming `sudo sail host init --storage zfs`, and lists its default workspace alone. Every verb reads the project from the catalog as the box's person, as `project apply` does; `GET /v1/projects/{p}/workspaces` serves the same rows, read in scope (`404` for a project outside it), and `SailKit`'s goldens record its answer. The instance is the record: no row and no schema change.
- **A workspace or a template is never taken for a project.** Every command that walks the box's instances now asks one place what each is (`BoxInstances`): a project's own container, a workspace, a template, or not Sail's. A scoped box's sync round no longer deletes the workspaces and template of a project in scope (it deleted every one within a minute), and when a project leaves the box's scope it takes its workspaces and template with it, saying so for each. `project apply --all` no longer starts them or rewrites a workspace's hostname; `project list`, `GET /v1/projects` and `agent status` no longer show them as projects; `sail snaps prune` across all projects no longer deletes template versions; the boot sweep reconciles only whole workspaces; a new pairing's key reaches a project's whole workspaces. `sudo sail project destroy <project>` removes the project's template and workspaces with its container, naming each in its prompt. No new project may take a name ending `--tmpl`, and every command that addresses a container by a project's name (`project create`, `apply`, `destroy`, `start`, `stop`, `switch`, `shell`, `exec`, `logs`, `connect`, `rename`, the snapshot, resources, config, repo, service and agent verbs, and the API doors) refuses an instance of that name that is a workspace, a template or a copy of one, naming what it is and what frees the name ("Project acme-fix cannot take instance acme-fix: it is workspace fix of project acme. Delete the workspace (sudo sail project workspace delete acme fix) or give the project another name; the workspace itself is reached with sail project workspace shell acme fix."). So do a terminal open (asking Incus of that one instance by name, within 3 s, outside the terminal host's lock, and refused saying so when Incus does not answer; an attach and a ticket ask Incus nothing), `skills ls`, and every API door that reaches a container by a project's name (`409 conflict`, with the remedy). `POST /v1/projects` refuses such a name on a box that holds the instance, as `project create` does, each asking Incus of the one name within 3 s; a box that holds none cannot know, and a project of that name that reaches by sync a box that does is said there once, in the round that brings it, and never given the instance (its resources are not applied to the workspace). `project destroy`, `stop` and `switch` read Incus for real in a dry run, so `--dry-run` names every workspace and the template the real run removes. A copy of a workspace made by hand is not Sail's: it outlives its project, and the round that drops the project says once that it was kept.
- **An event stream that opens with nothing to send asks its door once, as it opens.** Its first page is read for the request that was just admitted, so no second check follows the `: subscribed` comment; every later page is still asked before it is read and again before it is written. `OpenStreamRecheckTest` had counted that second check on a slow macOS runner when it landed after the test began counting.
- **A client that was away asks for exactly what changed since the last thing it saw, and gets it in order with no gap.** `GET /v1/changes?since=<position>[&types=spec,room,message][&limit=n]` answers the specs, rooms and messages the caller may read now whose newest revision is after the position, in order, each once a page as it is now: `{seq, type, id, row}`, or `gone` or `erased`, with no content, for a row filed where the caller may read. A row carries what its own record holds, shaped as its list route shapes it less the fields that list derives from other rows (a room's specs, latest activity and open question; a spec's room wake, seated agent, latest activity and open question), which a client derives from the rows it holds; a change to one row never re-sends another. A page holds at most 500 rows' changes (100 by default), with `next` and `more`. A position is one opaque string naming the database, the change log's `seq` and the entry standing there; it survives a restart. Every list and detail read of a spec, room or message answers a `Seq` header: the position it was read at, so "read, then follow the feed from it" misses nothing. On `GET /v1/events/stream` a row change is `event: change` with `id: <position>`; a stream that sends `Last-Event-ID` (or `?after=`) is first sent every change since, a page at a time between whatever else it sends, then live ones, a change committed during the replay included. A stream reads the feed when this process commits, or when another process wakes it with `POST /v1/changes:wake`, which any authenticated caller may send, which carries nothing, which no client is ever sent, and which wakes no one when nothing was written since the last; every command that writes the box's database now sends it after its commits. No other event makes a stream read; an idle stream reads nothing. Each page is read as its caller is now and sent only if the caller is still admitted once it is read. A position that does not parse (an older client's number), names another database, stands on an entry no longer there (a restored database; an entry erased since), or is from before a change of what its reader may read is `409 resync_required` and one `event: resync` with an `id:`, each naming the newest position; a stream told so after it started is then ended. A change of what a reader may read is: a spec, room or run moving between projects (for readers of both), and a person's grants, restrictions or removal (theirs). The deletion or erasure of a message filed apart from its conversation (every message said in a room before it moved, and any later reply in one of its threads) resyncs the scoped readers of the project it is filed under, though they can read none of it. A reader that hears only a room's steering voices (an agent) is never told the erasure of a message, and keeps one it was told. Bus events no longer carry an SSE `id`, so turn deltas and pty facts never move a client's cursor. `GET /v1/events/recent` is gone: it replayed an in-memory ring a real server never filled, and `sail events` now always streams (`--follow` and `--lines` are gone with it). The socket serves `/v1/changes` to a run's agent, which reads what its credential reads today. One index is added at start when missing; no schema version change. Upgrade note: a box run on an older binary against this database raises no floor while it runs, so a client's position from before such a window is answered as if nothing of what it may read changed. SailKit reads it: `Endpoint.changes`, `Change`, `SailClient.positioned`, `StreamUpdate.change` and `.resync`, a position being a `String` it hands back as it came, and `HeldRows`, which applies the feed and derives the list fields from the rows held.
- **A box set up with ZFS storage gives Incus the pool, so its containers launch and clone.** `sudo sail host init --storage zfs` ran `zpool create -f` on the disk and never registered the pool with Incus, so the base profile named a pool Incus did not have and no container launched; every box ran `dir`. Incus now creates and owns the pool: `host init` runs one `incus storage create <pool> zfs` from a whole disk or a partition (`--disk /dev/nvme0n1p5`, `source=<device>`), from a sparse file Incus makes (`--pool-file-size 200GiB`, `size=<n>MiB`), which step 5 says once is slower than a disk and meant for trying ZFS, and Sail only reads ZFS (`zpool list`). Sail erases nothing: there is no force flag, Sail never passes `source.wipe`, and Incus never runs `zpool create -f` (it does only for `source.wipe`). Sail's own checks decide; zpool's unforced create is a last guard for the moment between Sail's re-check and the create, which refuses a device libblkid sees a filesystem, swap or a label on at the whole-device level, and does not look inside an MBR disk's partitions or refuse an empty partition table. A named device is resolved (a `/dev/mapper` name by its kernel name), judged on lsblk's whole device tree (`lsblk --json --tree --bytes`, util-linux 2.37 or newer; a flat list fails closed) and probed directly, and it is taken only when it carries nothing. It must be a plain disk (at the top of the tree, of type disk, so not an md array, a loop device, an LVM or LUKS mapping, a ZFS volume, a zram or nbd device or a disk built on another, as bcache is), or a partition of one, not removable and not empty, a partition lying inside its disk and overlapping no other (a disk with an overlapping pair refuses every partition of it, and the disk names the pair; a partition the kernel cut at its disk's end, which udev reads no type for, is refused as reaching past the end); every partition's type, its own or for a disk each of its partitions', must be Linux filesystem data (`0fc63daf-…`, `0x83`) or ZFS (`6a898cc3-…`, `0xbf`), compared without case, any other or none refusing it, since a boot, firmware, reserved or extended partition may carry no signature; nothing may be mounted on it, used as swap or built on it; neither lsblk nor `wipefs`'s listing may show a signature of any kind (a filesystem, swap, a partition table, or any member's: every `*_member`, an exported pool's included, `bcache`, `ceph_bluestore`, LUKS), nor a disk have any partition; no other holder may have it open (the refusal names what the device holds, as the second device of a btrfs root holds btrfs), found by an exclusive open that writes nothing and, with a type filter no signature matches, re-reads no partition table (`wipefs --all --no-act --types sail_exclusive_probe`; a disk's partitions are probed before the disk, whose close makes udev re-read its table, Sail waits for udev to settle after it, and a partition gone meanwhile is refused as a table that is changing, never hiding a holder), and no process may have it, a disk's partitions or a partition's whole disk open at all (read from every `/proc/<pid>/fd` and matched by device number, so a holder through any node or path is seen, naming the process; one whose open files cannot be read refuses too, and an exclusive holder no walk finds is said to be one Sail cannot name); and neither it nor another partition of its disk may be a device of an imported pool, whatever class it is listed under. Each refusal names its reason and the filesystem a refused device holds unmounted; a member of anything names the tool that frees it and never an erase (a pool `zpool import` lists: import it or `zpool labelclear` it; a destroyed pool's labels: `zpool labelclear`; a pool's whole disk is named by its pool), and a device whose only obstacle is a filesystem, swap or a bare partition table names `sudo wipefs --all <device>` for the operator to run if nothing on it is wanted. Where the device sits does not matter: a blank partition on the system's disk is taken, as on a server whose two disks both carry the system. What neither the open nor the walk sees is a consumer that does not keep the device open, a stopped virtual machine's raw disk or a closed headerless dm-crypt volume. A pool on one partition or one disk has no redundancy, which step 5 says. A disk with no partition table and no signature is blank, even where a filesystem survives further in after its table was erased. The check runs when the flags are read, a dry run included, and again just before the pool is made, which refuses unless the device is the one first cleared (WWN or serial, major:minor and size in bytes), and once more before the create that follows Incus restarting to see ZFS, each the last command before its create, the slow walk of `/proc` running before the device's own probes so they are the last look at it; a device on a disk with a WWN or an NVMe id (EUI, NGUID or vendor id) is handed to Incus by its `/dev/disk/by-id` name, and on a disk with none a disk replaced under the same name after that check is judged by `zpool create` alone (which refuses a filesystem, swap, a pool member or a device in use, not an empty partition table or filesystems inside MBR partitions), so disks must not change while `host init` runs. Zpool's offer to force a create it refused is never passed on. With no device named only blank whole disks are offered, and none found names `--disk` with a partition and `--pool-file-size`. `--disk` or `--pool-file-size` without `--storage zfs`, or `--disk` with `--pool-file-size`, are refused, and so is a pool name Incus has with another driver, before anything is installed. Step 3 caps the ZFS read cache before it installs zfsutils-linux: `/etc/modprobe.d/sail-zfs.conf` holds `options zfs zfs_arc_max=` a tenth of memory, at most 16 GiB and at least 64 MiB (with `zfs_arc_min` at half the cap where 16 GiB falls under ZFS's floor), written to the loaded module too and, where `/` is on ZFS, into the initramfs with `update-initramfs -u`; decided from what the next boot applies (`modprobe --showconfig`, every file modprobe reads and the kernel command line), a cap the operator set there or edited into Sail's own file is kept and named, a value only the loaded module holds is no reason to write nothing, nothing written, and the cap Sail wrote before is removed so the operator's is the one modprobe applies. Step 6 converges the base profile (a root disk or NIC it lacks is added, an existing root disk kept) and fails unless Incus has the pool its root disk names; `host.yaml` states that pool, its driver and its source as Incus has them, under `pool_source` (was `pool_disk`). On a box already on `dir`, `host init --storage zfs --pool <name>` adds the ZFS pool beside it and touches no container, no profile device and `host.yaml`'s storage; placing projects on it comes with workspaces. A re-run on a box whose Incus has the zfs pool keeps it, and refuses by name a `--disk` or `--pool-file-size` it would ignore; the command that made the pool, on a disk or in a file of the same size, runs again as a no-op (`--disk` is compared with the pool's source as the device each resolves to, so any name of that device, the by-id path Incus holds included, is the same disk, and a source that no longer resolves is refused as the pool's device gone), and a pool Incus no longer has by the time the pool is made is never made from its zpool. A dry run reads the profile, pools and packages the box has, so adding a pool beside shows the base profile staying where it is and a box without Incus or ZFS is told they would be installed, the bridge, pool and profile created, the image cached and the bridge rules added, ending "Dry run done: nothing was changed" rather than "Server ready", and it no longer opens the box's store to seed the demo project. An Incus that says a ZFS tool is missing is restarted only on a box with no container, and otherwise init names `systemctl restart incus` and stops; Incus finds tools installed after it started without one. `sail host status` lists every pool with its driver, source and free space, from Incus (`zpool list` and `df` are gone), and its JSON replaces `pool_disk`, `pool_size`, `disk_size` and the other usage keys with `pools` (or `storage_error`). A paused container reads as frozen, not as an error: `project list`, `project config show`, the banners and the API's `container_status` say `frozen`, and `host status` counts it (`containers_frozen`); `project apply` and `project start` resume it, `project stop` (one or all), a restore and a rename stop it first, a resize after a sync applies to it, `project demo` refuses it with "Resume it", and a door that needs it running refuses with "Project 'acme' is frozen. Resume it with: sudo sail project start acme". On ZFS a container's disk limit is enforced: Incus sets the root disk's size as the dataset's ZFS `quota`, which its snapshots count against (ARCHITECTURE.md said `refquota`, which Sail never set).
- **A connection to the terminal host frees its slot before it closes, at the unix socket and at the TCP door alike.** A client that saw its connection end and connected again at once could find the slot still held and be turned away at the cap: each door now gives the slot back before it closes the channel, so by the time a client sees its connection end, the slot is free.
- **A client reaches the API and a terminal directly over the private network, with a ticket and no SSH.** Two host settings name where a box listens beyond loopback, each a private IP address and a port: `sail host config set api-listen 100.64.0.7:7071` serves the HTTP API there too, the same routes, gates and credentials, and `sail host config set terminal-listen 100.64.0.7:7072` has the terminal host take TCP connections there beside its unix socket (restart `sail-api`, and for the terminal `sail-pty-host` too); `none` closes a door again. With neither set nothing new is bound and every answer is as before. An address must be loopback, RFC 1918, 100.64.0.0/10, or an IPv6 unique-local address, written in full with a port that has no leading zero; a wildcard, link-local, deprecated site-local (`fec0::/10`), multicast, broadcast, public or unparseable one is refused at start naming its setting ("'api-listen' is '0.0.0.0:7071', a wildcard, which would listen on every network the box is on. Name the box's address on the private network, e.g. 100.64.0.7:7071."). One that cannot be bound yet takes nothing down: loopback and the unix socket serve, the door binds once its address is up, trying again with backoff and saying so once, and one whose listener is lost later binds again the same way; the two settings may not name one address and port ("'api-listen' and 'terminal-listen' both name 100.64.0.7:7071; each door needs an address and port of its own."). `sail host status` shows what each running service is listening on, and the setting its next restart takes when that differs; with neither setting its `--json` has no `listen` key. `POST /v1/terminal/tickets` with `{"action": "open", "project": "acme"}` or `{"action": "attach", "session": "lounge"}` answers `201` with `ticket`, `address` and `expires_at`; the terminal host resolves the credential the caller presented and decides the request as it decides that open or attach. Opening a terminal now needs the project on your own box wherever it is asked: its person must operate the box (its own person, or an admin), at the unix socket too, so an FDE who reached another's box through a session token there opens no terminal on it any more ("Box 'ada' belongs to 'ada', not you. Ask ada to operate it, or have an admin do it."). A project not held is `404`, any other refusal `403` in the host's words, a ticket cap `429`, and a host that is down or not listening `503 terminal_unavailable`, a new code; an attach ticket for a session that is not the caller's, or does not exist, is the same `404` ("There is no session 'lounge' on this box that you may attach."). On a box with no `terminal-listen` the route is `404` as an unknown path. A ticket admits one TCP connection, presented as its `Hello` token within 30 s, for that one action on that one target (an open ticket creates one session in its project, and attaches to it), and then the connection speaks the unix socket's frames, each decided again; a used, expired, unknown or other ticket is refused in words before any frame is served, the ticket's credential is resolved again when it is presented and at every sweep, and a change of access severs the connection as it does a unix one. One credential holds at most 8 tickets not yet presented. A TCP connection that sends anything but a ticket is closed unanswered, one that sends none is closed 5 s after it connects, one peer holds at most 8 such connections (an IPv6 peer counted by its /64), and they share a pool of a quarter of the door's cap whose oldest, the one that connected first, is let go for a newcomer, so a peer with many addresses cannot keep a ticketed client out; refusals are logged once a second by reason, and until then the host reads at most 256 bytes of one; TCP connections have a cap of their own, apart from the unix socket's, and a ticketed one keeps TCP keepalive, so a peer that vanished is let go within about a minute. Only the box's own process asks for a ticket, on a connection that presented no token. No ticket is issued for no credential, for the host's own token or for a machine token; an agent's run credential and the box credential are refused at both doors. `sail _pty-selftest` opens a loopback door and hears one echo through a ticket, printing `tcp-selftest: ok`. `SailKit` reads the ticket answer (`Endpoint.terminalTicket`), the `terminal_unavailable` code and the three new frames (`issueTicket`, `ticket`, `ticketRefused`). There is no TLS on either listener: use them only on an encrypting overlay such as Tailscale, since on a LAN bearer tokens and tickets cross in the clear, and every project container on the box can reach them, held off by authentication alone.
- **A shipped change states its time and rework from the rows Sail already holds, and says what it cannot know.** `GET /v1/specs/{id}/measures` answers, for one spec, when it was made ready (its first move into `pending`), verified (when the current attempt's latest review completed, if it passed), moved into `awaiting_merge` and done (the move that began its latest stretch in `done`), the intervals between them in seconds, its rework (`review_rounds` that reached a verdict, `review_errors`, `build_runs`, a resumed build counting as one more, `fix_runs`, `carried_findings`), whether it is done with no human review stage decided in any attempt ("no recorded human code act", an upper bound), and cost and human minutes as `unknown`, with its count of runs without usage. Every number has one shape: `{"seconds": n}` or `{"count": n}`, `{"count": n, "at_least": true}` when a run retention erased since the spec arrived may have been its own, `{"unknown": "<reason>"}`, or `{"error": "..."}`. Times are the author's times of the revisions in this box's change log, named by the box's Sail id (`times.box`: `{"sail_id": ...}`, or `{"unknown": "no_box_id"}`), and exact only for moves written on this box (a box's own write coming back from main is that move): one whose earlier revisions the log no longer holds is `history_incomplete`, one in or behind a revision heard by sync `collapsed_by_sync`, one never reached `not_reached`, an interval missing an end `{"unknown": "inputs_missing", "missing": 1}`, and one ending before it starts `{"error": "done at … is before ready at …; a negative interval is not served."}`. A review or run the reader may not see leaves its numbers `hidden_rows`, never zero. Run counts are a lower bound once a run of a project the spec was filed under is erased after the spec arrived, on main as retention erases it and on a node as it hears of it; an erasure in another project marks nothing. A spec the caller may not read answers `404 spec_not_found`, as a missing one. `GET /v1/specs/{id}?measures=true` carries the same record as `measures`, with no ETag (a read that does not ask computes none). Reading measures is a new row of the permission table, Read measures: a person's as reading the spec, an agent's for its own work alone, in its work's project, and not the credential in a container's, which is answered the detail without them; `measures=` anything but `true` or `false` is refused, saying what to send. `sail spec show` prints a Measures section, and `sail spec list --status done --measures` (with `--done-since`/`--done-until`, dates in UTC, inclusive; `GET /v1/specs:measures`) prints one row per spec and the nearest-rank median and p90 of every number over the values known exactly, each saying how many it left out as unknown, as lower bounds and as errors; `--measures` without `--status done` is refused. A listing reads as many statements for a hundred specs as for one, and `GET /v1/specs` now reads every listed spec's dependencies and repos in two statements, not two per spec (an empty list, in one). Reading measures writes no row (the door stamps the credential's last use, as for any request). No schema change.
- **A Swift core for the native clients, proven against the server's own answers.** `clients/SailKit` is a Swift package (Swift 6, Foundation only) that the macOS and iOS apps will build on: `SailAPI` reads `whoami`, projects, rooms, messages and specs and posts a message, keeping a refusal's code, message, fix and field errors, and its models are `Codable` values an app can build and store; `SailEvents` follows `/v1/events/stream` across reconnects; `SailTerminalWire` reads and writes the `SAILPTY3` frames. A connection carries what is published while it is open, so the reader is told each time one opens, with the last event id read before it, and each time one cannot be opened. `ClientGoldensTest` writes what the server answers for each of them under `clients/SailKit/Tests/Goldens` and fails when the server would answer otherwise, naming every file that differs (they are a snapshot as the permission baseline is, so `-Dsail.access.baseline=record` rewrites them), and the new `Clients` workflow builds and tests the package on Linux and macOS. No server answer changes.
- **The terminal host's pty layer runs on macOS.** The same classes open, size and serve a session's terminal on a Mac as on Linux, through one spawn chain; only the system's constants differ, read once (Darwin's `O_NOCTTY`, `TIOCSWINSZ` and `TIOCSCTTY`, and `ioctl` called as the variadic function it is). The session shim (`sail _pty-child`) now makes each session itself: it starts a new session, claims the terminal as its controlling terminal and puts it on the command's standard input, output and error, so the host no longer runs util-linux `setsid`. On Linux the command still runs in the terminal's foreground group, sessions still cross a restart of the host, and a shim from this release run by a host started earlier takes the terminal it is handed, so upgrading ends no session. What the shim says before it has a terminal, a failure to claim one, now reaches the host's journal (its `sail-pty-host` unit's) instead of being discarded, and a shim that has not claimed its terminal within 10 s is stopped and the terminal refused ("The session shim did not claim /dev/pts/3 within 10 s, so it was stopped."). `sail _pty-selftest` checks the terminal on macOS too, printing `pty-selftest: ok` and `fdstore-selftest: skipped (no descriptor store on this system)`; sessions do not survive a restart of a host on macOS, which keeps no descriptors.
