And a main promoted on v0.49.0 to v0.52.0, or one whose role change died between writing `host.yaml` and clearing its scope, kept the scope it last heard as a node, refusing its own `spec create` and its nodes' pushes into every other project and skipping its own descriptors at upgrade; `sail migrate` and every `sail server start` now clear that scope on any box that is not a node, and only a whole `host.yaml` decides it: one absent, empty or unreadable at that moment (truncated by a writer, or being moved into `/var/lib/sail`) keeps the scope for the next start to decide, so a node never loses its grant to a file in motion. `sudo sail host update` and `sail migrate`'s box-id and `host.yaml` relocation steps now read and write `host.yaml` under that lock too, and `host update` records the Incus version on the file as it is then, not as it was before the packages upgraded, so an identity change made meanwhile is kept.
- **A scope can change after an FDE exists, and every box follows it whole.** Field rehearsal 2026-10-15 found no way to scope an FDE that joined unscoped short of `fde rm` and a re-add (which revoked its tokens, keys, sessions and passkeys), a scoped node still holding the names and emails of FDEs outside its projects, and a revoked project's container running on the node with its clone and credentials, still listed by `sail project list`. **`sudo sail fde update <handle> --scoped [--grant <project>]...`** narrows an FDE in place, in one transaction with the grants it names (`--grant` is repeatable and takes `a,b`), and **`--unscoped`** lets it see every project again and forgets its grants, so a later narrowing grants only what it names; the FDE keeps its id and every credential. Both take `--dry-run` (the real run, rolled back) and `--json` (`handle`, `scope`, `projects`, `changed`, `dry_run`), are refused on a node with the roster sentence, and refuse this box's own FDE and an admin as `fde add --scoped` does. A narrowing says what the FDE's box drops ("Scoped eng to acme. Its box drops beta, demo with their containers and unpushed work on its next sync."), a widening that its box holds every project after its next sync. `fde grant` on an unscoped FDE now names `sudo sail fde update <handle> --scoped`. A narrowing is a revocation on the node and a widening a grant, so the node drops and re-adopts as before. A scoped node now holds exactly the roster main lists to it: an FDE main stops listing is removed with its box-local credentials ("roster: removed 1 FDE(s) main no longer lists to this box"), the box's own FDE never; an unscoped node still disables such an FDE and keeps its row. Every round of a scoped node then takes off the box each project the scope does not admit and the box holds nothing of: its `~/.sail/projects/<name>` directory, and its container when Sail provisioned it (the `sail-api-sock` device, the trust gate `project apply --all` uses), as `sudo sail project destroy` without `--purge` would ("project beta: its container deleted, workspace and uncommitted work included — main no longer grants this box the project"). A container Sail did not provision is left alone and named once; when the round cannot reach Incus (a non-root `sail sync`) nothing is deleted and the round that dropped the project says the next round that can (sail-api within a minute, or `sudo sail sync`) will; a project kept for a run under way keeps both until the round after the run ends. It is decided from what the box holds, so a box an earlier release left with such a directory, container or roster row is brought in line by its first round on this release. An unchanged scoped round asks `incus list` once, and an unscoped one asks Incus nothing. `sail migrate` no longer imports the descriptor or files of a project the box's scope does not admit, so an upgrade no longer imports a dropped project only for the next round to drop it again. An open event stream (`/v1/events/stream`) or run-log stream (`/v1/runs/{id}/stream`) now asks its caller's authentication again, at most once a second, before an event or a heartbeat: a grant, a revoke or a demotion holds from that event on, and a caller removed, disabled or with its token or session revoked gets a last `event: error` naming why, then the stream ends. **Fleet note:** upgrade a scoped node before narrowing or revoking: a node of an earlier release drops the rows but keeps the roster rows and the container.
- **The box credential exists as soon as the box has a handle.** The takeover of `sail-cli-safe-defaults` found that after `sudo sail init --as-main --handle ada …` or `sudo sail init --main … --handle bob …` on a fresh box, an engineer's shell or an IDE agent in a project container got "spec: no credential: SAIL_RUN_CREDENTIAL is unset and /var/lib/sail/run/box.credential is unreadable (upgrade sail on the host and restart the server)" until sail-api restarted: the box credential was issued only when sail-api started, and every way a box takes its handle runs after that. Every command that writes this box's identity (`sudo sail init`, `sudo sail join`, `sudo sail host sync --as-main --handle`, `sudo sail host config set sync-handle`) now issues the credential for the handle before it returns, with no restart; a handle change revokes the old handle's credential and issues the new one, and a re-run puts back a missing or damaged one. The identity write and the credential are one step under the box's round lock, as sail-api's own start is, so two overlapping writes leave the credential naming the handle written last: the write that waits is refused when host.yaml no longer holds the identity it read ("Cannot write this box's sync identity: /var/lib/sail/host.yaml changed while this command ran. It read the box as standalone with no handle; the box is now main as 'ada'. Rerun the command against what the box is now."), so it cannot put the old identity back over the new one. A handle or box id set on a box with no sync role is now kept in host.yaml (it was dropped on write, so `sudo sail host config set sync-handle` on a standalone box answered "✓" and held nothing). A box with no database yet is issued it at sail-api's first start, as before. With no credential the `spec` CLI now names the cause: "… is unreadable: this box has no sync handle. Give it one on the host: sudo sail host config set sync-handle <handle>". Containers take the new sentence at their next `sudo sail project apply` or `sudo sail migrate`.
- **Each API request keeps its own caller.** On JDK 25, the release build's runtime, an `HttpExchange`'s attributes are its context's, shared by every request to that path (JDK 26 made them per exchange, JDK-7105350), so a caller authenticating on another connection could overwrite the identity, role and scope sail-api had just stamped on a request before that request read them, and an open stream's re-check could take on an admin's scope. Every context of sail-api now gives each exchange its own attributes before the rate limit and the authentication see it, and the unit tests run with JDK 26's pre-26 setting so the devbox behaves as the release does.

## 0.53.0

- **No destructive default, no ignored flag, and main's operator set up by init.** Field rehearsal round 5 found `sudo sail spec delete e-beta` with no terminal printing "Delete spec 'e-beta'? [y/N]" and then deleting it, and `sail fde rm` doing the same. Both prompts now go through `ConsoleHelper.confirmDestructive`: an empty line or EOF on a terminal cancels, and with no terminal and no `--force` the command refuses with exit 1 ("Not deleted: no terminal to confirm on. Re-run with --force." / "Not removed: …"). `sail project destroy` asks the same way: Enter no longer destroys, and with no terminal and no `--yes` it refuses ("Not destroyed: no terminal to confirm on. Re-run with --yes."). `sail spec delete --json` requires `--force` and `sail project destroy --json` requires `--yes`, as nobody answers a prompt through JSON. A source scan fails on any `: "y"` console fallback. `sail spec content <id> --body-file b.md` (or `--plan-file`) now sets the content, as the `spec` CLI documents; before, it printed "No content for <id>." and wrote nothing unless `--set` was given, which still works, and `--set` with no file is refused ("--set needs --body-file and/or --plan-file."). `sudo sail init --as-main` now takes `--handle` (asked for when left out; a re-run keeps the handle the box already acts as) and sets up main's operator: `sudo sail host sync --as-main --handle <h> [--name …] [--email …]` writes the sync handle in the same identity change as the role, so runs are stamped as that FDE, and adds the FDE to the roster as admin when it is missing ("✓ FDE added: ada (admin)"). Init gives main's roster entry `--name`/`--email`, else the git identity the box has, and refuses, before any step, a value `host sync` would refuse; a node passes its join what was given or asked, as before. An FDE already on the roster is left as it is ("FDE ada is already on the roster (admin)."; one that is not admin is told "Make it admin: sudo sail fde update ada --role admin"). A box already acting as another handle is refused before any step, naming both ("This box already acts as 'grace', not 'ada'. Re-run with --handle grace, or change its handle first: sudo sail host config set sync-handle ada"). After the README's one-line setup, `sail host sync` no longer reports "No FDE set", and the Quick start shows `--handle`.
- **Every box holds the same record, and sync never takes back what it accepted.** Field rehearsal round 5 found a scoped node accepting `sudo sail spec create -p <ungranted>` ("✓ Spec created") only for its next sync to delete it, main creating specs under a project that does not exist, every box showing its own adoption time as a spec's `created_at`, and a fresh scoped node warning twice that it had dropped unpushed work nobody made.
  - **A box takes a spec or a room only into a project it holds.** Creating one, moving one (`spec update --project`) or restoring one into a project not in this box's catalog, or on a scoped node one main does not grant it even when the catalog holds it, is refused before anything is written, on every box, for every write the box decides as its own: "Project 'beta' isn't on this box. Projects here: acme." (`not_found`), and on a scoped node "Ask an admin to grant it on main: sudo sail fde grant <handle> beta." A row that stays in its project is never refused, whatever the catalog holds. Main applies the same rule to a pushed spec or room ("Project 'beta' isn't on main. Projects there: …") as a denial the node keeps: the row stays on the node and is offered again each round, so it lands once main holds the project; a scoped node pushing outside its scope hears, as before, only that the project was not found. A spec or room in a project the node made itself waits, silently, for main to take the project (projects sync after specs). Sync drops a row this box accepted only when an admin revokes its project's grant on main.
  - **A record's times are its author's, on every box.** Each revision of a spec, project or room carries when the entity was created and when the revision was made (`_created_at`, `_updated_at`), outside the comparison, so two boxes never conflict on a clock; every box applying it writes both verbatim. A spec's `archived_at`/`cancelled_at` is the time of the revision that made the transition, which an archived or cancelled spec's revision carries as `_status_since`, so a later edit, a concurrent archive or a merge with an edit made elsewhere never moves it (schema: the two status triggers are recreated). Main and every node now show the same `created_at`, `updated_at`, archive time and board order (ties broken by id) for every row they hold, a room made on two boxes and merged included. A rebase, a resolve, a restore, a merge offered to main and a project rename's revisions are stamped when they are made. Main keeps the creation time of a row it already holds, whatever a later offer carries, and its answer carries it back, so a node never moves it; `sail conflicts show` and the `--merge` template show fields only, never the times or the author. **Fleet note:** rows written before this release keep each box's own times until main next takes a revision of them; from then on every box holds main's creation time and the revision's own update time. A box of an earlier release neither sends nor reads the times, so its rows are stamped as before. Two boxes no longer mint the same revision id for the same project definition, so such a pair converges by the node taking main's.
  - **Sync's notices are true and print once.** The demo project is seeded only on main or a box that syncs with nobody; a node gets `demo` from main like any project, when granted, so a fresh scoped node's first sync drops nothing, and `sail migrate` on a node never seeds it. A drop notice says "unpushed work included" only when the project held rows main never took, and counts them ("project beta: dropped from this box with the 3 rows it held here, unpushed work included (1 row never pushed) — main no longer grants this box the project"). Each settlement and refusal prints once per round (it printed on stderr as it happened and again in the round's summary); `--json` still carries them. Parked conflicts print as a warning, "⚠ 1 conflict(s) need your decision. Run 'sail conflicts' to resolve.", and the round, which succeeded, exits 0; `sail sync status --json` carries `pending_conflicts`.
- **Every printed command through one seam, and every container trusts the box's key.** Field rehearsal round 4 on v0.52.0 found a definition with no `ssh:` block (the README's minimal form) making a container no key could reach: `sudo sail project connect gamma` warned that no workstation key was registered and said to re-create the project, and after `sudo sail host config set ssh-public-key` the next apply reported "converged" with `/home/dev/.ssh/authorized_keys` still absent. Step 6 now writes the box's registered workstation key first, whatever the definition says, then the definition's own keys, each key once by fingerprint; with neither it writes an empty file. The key's fingerprint is an input of the run's digest (with no key the digest is the descriptor's alone, as before), so registering, replacing or revoking it makes the next `sudo sail project apply` of every project check every step again and put the key in, or take it out; a pairing's "trust this key after their next apply" now holds for every project. Connect's warning now ends "…register the one you connect with, then apply the project to put it in the container:" and lists `sudo sail host config set ssh-public-key` and `sudo sail project apply gamma`. The re-provisioning line says why: "The descriptor or this box's workstation key changed since the last provisioning run: every step is checked again." A workstation key file that holds no readable key is refused ("This box's workstation key (…) is not an SSH public key: …") rather than read as none, which would have taken the key out of every container on the next apply; a definition key that carries options (`from=…`, `restrict`) is the workstation key once when it is the same key. `sail project demo` reports the registered workstation key as found and names its identity file, and never writes that key into its definition: step 6 trusts it as the box holds it, so a revoked key is never put back by a later apply. An `ssh.authorized_keys` entry that is empty, not text or more than one line is refused when a descriptor is provisioned ("ssh.authorized_keys holds a key of more than one line. Write each key on a line of its own in the descriptor you applied, then apply again."; only a file can hold one, since a catalog row keeps `${SSH_PUBLIC_KEY}` alone), and each key is written stripped. `sail project demo` resolves its definition in the parsed tree as every descriptor is, so a quoted placeholder or an apostrophe in a git name no longer breaks it, and fills `${SSH_PUBLIC_KEY}` from the caller's own key file only when that file holds a key. A pairing replaces a workstation key file that holds no key without asking, and the API's connect answers a damaged key file with 412 and the same sentence. **Fleet note:** on a box with a workstation key, the first apply of each project after upgrade provisions it once, every step reporting what is already there. Every `sail` command sail prints is rendered by `OperatorCommand` (a test scans every string literal in `src/main` and fails on `sudo sail` or `sail <verb>` outside it): `sail()` for the caller on this box, so root logged in directly reads plain `sail …` and `ssh-keygen -R …` where a `sudo` caller reads `sudo sail …`, always, since root's sail acts on root's state and the caller's own plain `sail` never reaches it, also on a box that has installed nothing yet (the sync-handle, migrate, connect, host-key, server-IP, `sail host init` next-step and every "Root privileges required" hint among them, which no longer hardcode `sudo` or drop it); `elsewhere()`, always `sudo sail …`, for a reader the box cannot know (main's admin, another box, a journal refusal or an API answer, the join's `fde add` line); and `client()`, always plain, for a thin client's enroll and login hints. A command only root runs (`init`, `host init`, `host keys sync`, `host ssh-identity`, `host status`, `host update`, `host config set`, `host sync`, `project apply`, `project destroy`, `project rename`, `project resources set`, `project demo`) reads `sudo` for a non-root process too, and upgrade's and `host config set slack-token`'s `host service install` remedy carries `sudo` when the missing service is root's system unit. sail-api, the pty host and an agent watcher, which cannot see who reads their answers, render the README's form (`sudo sail …`) when they run as root, so `sudo sail spec create` of an id taken is told "edit it with: sudo sail spec update <id>" and a stored sync error names `sudo ssh-keygen -R <main>`; a non-root sail-api answers plain. Hints that named a command sail does not have are fixed (the watcher's `agent run`, the room wake prompt's `spec dispatch --spec <id> --restart`, enroll's `sail client <host>` and `sudo sail upgrade`), and a test parses every rendered command against the command tree; a non-root `host config set` with no value is no longer told to re-run with `null`, and `install.sh` on a Mac says `sail client <host>`. Help texts and prose that only named a command no longer spell one, and `sail agent run`'s prompt ends "When complete, stop." (it named a `sail spec status` that does not exist, then told the agent to pick up the next spec, which nothing had moved on). Apply no longer re-applies an unchanged git identity: it reads `user.name` and `user.email` as `dev` and, when both match, prints "[skip] Git config already current" and counts a skip, so three applies in a row no longer each report "converged: 1 added". The README's Quick start types every command with `sudo` (`sudo sail fde pair`, `sudo sail fde grant`, `sudo sail project apply`), and a test fails on one without it.

## 0.52.0

- **Every command a box tells you to type works there, as printed.** A `sudo` caller on a box whose sail-api is root's system unit, where that account's plain `sail` is refused, is now told every `sail` command with `sudo`: the sync-handle and sync-role hints of `sail host sync`, `sail host ssh-identity`'s "Register engineers with 'sudo sail fde add …'", apply's `→ Connect:`/`→ Shell:` lines and its server-IP hint, `sail project connect`'s, `sail host init`'s `'sudo sail upgrade'`, the join's closing `sudo sail sync`, and every other command a refusal or hint on this box names (a missing descriptor's `sail project init`/`sail sync`, a stopped or absent project's `sail project start`/`sail project apply`, "Server not initialized", the `sail fde`, `sail agent`, `sail conflicts`, `sail spec` and `sail upgrade` hints, and those of the operations layer the CLI runs in-process, such as `sudo sail spec dispatch`'s "Start it with sudo sail project start gamma." and its `sail spec update … --assignee` and `sail server start`); a command the box names for another box set up by `sudo sail init` (main's `sail fde grant`/`fde update`/`fde add`, a node's `sail fde pair`) and `sail project destroy` read `sudo sail` for every reader; root logged in directly, and an account on a box it owns in its own home, see plain `sail` as before. One seam (`OperatorCommand`) renders them from the inputs `RuntimeMode.detect` reads. `install.sh` ends with the README's `sudo sail init --as-main` and `sudo sail init --main <main>`, and `sail host init` run as `sudo sail init`'s first step no longer tells the operator to run `sudo sail init`. `sudo sail init --as-main` gains `--name` and `--email`, and both `--as-main` and `--main` give the box's git identity (root's, on a box init set up) the `user.name`/`user.email` they name, asked for when left out, when it has none, never overwriting one it has, and a box that would be left with none is refused before any step ("This box has no git user.name set, so a project here can't take on your identity. Re-run with --name \"...\"."); so after `sudo sail project apply -f gamma.yaml`, `sudo sail project apply gamma` resolves `${GIT_NAME}`/`${GIT_EMAIL}` and converges. The missing-identity refusal names `sudo git config --global user.name "..."` when the identity read is root's for a `sudo` caller. Apply no longer reports a project converged over another repository's checkout: an existing repo directory whose `origin` is not the definition's repository, or that is not the root of a git checkout (an empty directory inside another checkout included), is refused before the step is recorded and left untouched ("Repo 'web' is a clone of https://github.com/org/old.git, but the definition says https://github.com/org/web.git. Move it aside (it may hold work) or fix the URL."); the same host, case-insensitively, and path are one repository whatever the scheme, user, port, `:` or `/`, trailing `.git` or `/`, so a checkout switched between SSH and HTTPS still converges, and a token in the checkout's URL is never echoed. A refusal inside `sudo sail init` prints its sentence once, with no Java trace, and "Setup stopped — the step above failed." once; every command's failure is printed once (it was printed twice, the second time without the `✗`). A node whose sync fails on main's host key is told the whole recovery, in `MainHostKey`'s own words: "If main was rebuilt, remove the old key with 'sudo ssh-keygen -R <host>' and re-run 'sudo sail init --main <target>'; otherwise something between this box and main is answering for it." A re-join with the sync key this box already had asks main, in one sync session as the joined handle, whether it holds that key, and when main welcomes it prints "Main already has this box's key. Finish with sudo sail sync." instead of the `fde add` line (`--json` still carries `authorize_command`); a key main does not welcome as that handle, a key made by hand, or a main that cannot be asked, still get the line. A sync denial's "Yours is in its history: sail spec history <id>." and the kept-file remedy "capture with 'sail project files add'" name the command with `sudo` for a `sudo` caller too. The SSH config `sail project apply` and `sail project connect` print jumps through the box by its hostname (`Host <hostname>`, `ProxyJump <hostname>`) instead of `singular-server`.

## 0.51.0

- **A sudo user's documented setup ends with a working box.** `sudo sail init` stopped at the sail-api step for a normal sudo user ("Failed to connect to bus: No medium found"), because it installed a per-user service through `su`, which opens no logind session. Init now requires root and always installs the system service, whoever invoked `sudo`, and never runs a step as another account; a `sudo` user who already runs sail-api as a user service is refused before anything is written ("dev already runs sail-api as a user service (…), so this box is set up for dev: run sail as dev, without sudo. To set the box up as root instead, first run 'sail host service uninstall' as dev."). `sudo sail host init` now names `sudo sail init` as its next step. On a box whose sail-api is root's system unit, a non-root account that sees no host config no longer reads its own empty `~/.sail`: every command but `--version` and `--help` is refused with the same command prefixed by `sudo` ("sail-api on this box runs as root, so dev's sail would see none of its specs, projects or FDEs. Run it with sudo: sudo sail spec list"). On a box an account owns in its own home, `sudo sail project apply` resolves `${SSH_PUBLIC_KEY}`, `${GIT_NAME}` and `${GIT_EMAIL}` from that account, so its plain apply right after converges only. `sail join` (and so `sudo sail init --main`) pins main's ed25519 SSH host key into `known_hosts` before it writes the host config, refusing a key that differs from the one pinned or that cannot be read, and its `fde add` line now reads `sudo sail fde add … --main-host-key SHA256:…` (`--json` adds `main_host_key`); main refuses the add when that is not its own host key, before writing anything. A sync that dies before main replies quotes ssh's last stderr line ("ssh said: Host key verification failed. — re-run 'sudo sail init --main <main>' to pin main's host key."). **Fleet note:** a node's new `fde add` line needs main on this release (upgrade main first, as always). A box left by a failed v0.48–v0.49 sudo init clears it with `sail host service uninstall` as that user, then `rm -r ~/.sail` as that user, then re-runs `sudo sail init`.
- **Apply records a provisioning step only when its effect exists.** A descriptor without `ssh:` (valid, and the README's own shape) advanced step 6 without creating a user, so the sail machinery, linger and everything after it failed on a missing `/home/dev`; and a step recorded for one descriptor was trusted for the next, so a fix to a failed run never reached it. A descriptor without `ssh:`, or with an `ssh:` block that names no `user`, now provisions `dev`, and step 6 is recorded only once `dev` exists at uid 1000, in `sudo`, with sshd enabled; a `dev` at another uid (an image with its own user at 1000) fails the step naming the fix. `ssh.user` other than `dev` is refused by `sail project apply` (single and `--all`) before any container is created or started: "ssh.user is 'bob', but sail runs every agent, shell and hook as 'dev' (uid 1000, /home/dev). Remove 'ssh.user' from acme's descriptor (or set it to dev) with 'sail project edit acme', then apply again. …". Sync, connect and listing still read such a definition. `sail project init` no longer asks for an SSH user and writes `user: dev`. The provision state carries the SHA-256 of the resolved descriptor (`inputs`): an interrupted apply whose descriptor changed starts over from step 1 ("The descriptor changed since the interrupted run: every step is checked again."). A finished run records that SHA-256 on the container itself (Incus key `user.sail.inputs`), so a plain `sail project apply` reads the record `sudo` wrote, on main or a node, and converges without provisioning again; a run clears that record before it changes the container, so after a run that failed part way every apply provisions again, even with the descriptor that last finished; an apply whose descriptor differs from the container's record, or finds none, runs every step again, so a runtime or key added to an existing container's descriptor reaches it. Each step re-checks its own effect, so only the difference is installed; step 3 no longer restarts a container whose limits are already set, and a restart it owes (it marks the container `user.sail.restart_pending` with the limits and clears the mark only after the restart) is retried by the next apply, since `raw.lxc` takes effect only at start. Step 6 writes `authorized_keys` to exactly the descriptor's list on an existing user, so a key removed from the descriptor loses its access. **Fleet note:** the first `sudo sail project apply` after upgrade provisions each existing container once, every step reporting what is already there; provisioning needs root, so an apply after a descriptor edit does too.

## 0.50.0

- **A connect code opens only its own FDE's box.** A code logs in as the box's operator, root or the account `sail-api` runs as, which reads every project, container and secret on the box, yet any admin could mint one for any FDE on main, so a scoped client handed one held a root shell there. `sail fde pair` now mints a code only for the box's own FDE (its sync handle), on main or on a node, and refuses any other handle before writing anything, naming where it pairs: "A connect code logs in as root@m.acme.dev, which sees every project here. Pair client on its own box instead: set it up with 'sudo sail init --main m.acme.dev', then run 'sail fde pair client --host <its address>' there." A box with no sync handle names `sudo sail host config set sync-handle`. On a node, pairing is no roster edit: its FDE must be in the replicated roster and active, and `--name`, `--email` and `--role` are refused with the roster sentence. Revoking a pairing (`unpair`, `rm`, a re-pair) now also removes its key from the workstation key file when it is that key and, by the exact key, from the `authorized_keys` of every `/home` account (read from the container's own `/etc/passwd`, not the catalog) in every container of the box (never a virtual machine), running or stopped; other keys stay, whatever their comments mention. A workstation key file that cannot be read fails the revocation and keeps the record, so a re-run finishes it. A revocation waits for a `project apply` in flight, which holds the pairing lock from reading the workstation key to installing it, so an apply never puts a revoked key back. **Fleet note: pairings of any FDE but the box's own are revoked on upgrade** (`pairings-own-box-v1`, by `migrate` on the box itself, never in a `SAIL_DATA_DIR` rehearsal), each one named. A box with no sync handle names no FDE of its own, so every pairing on it is revoked, and each notice says how its operator pairs again (`sudo sail host config set sync-handle <handle>`, then pair). Onboard any other FDE on its own box (`sudo sail init --main <main>`, grant it its projects on main, pair there).
- **Every FDE has a standing conversation with each project's agent.** `sail project apply` on a box whose FDE has a handle leaves that FDE a home room, `<project>--<handle>`, titled with the project's name, assigned to the FDE and engaged with the project's member, the moment the container runs with the member's harness installed. A second apply changes nothing, and a room its FDE disengaged or re-engaged stays as they left it. The member is the definition's new optional `agent.member` (`agent`, default `agent.type`; `model`, default the agent's; `mode`, `full` or `read_only`, default `full`), read once when the room is created; a member the project does not install (declared, or the `agent.type` default), an unknown mode or an unsafe model is refused when the definition is read, naming the field. The member is admitted before the room is written and seated in the same write (`POST /v1/rooms` accepts an optional `member`), so a refused member leaves no room and the next apply creates it. A pair a room id cannot carry verbatim (upper case, `.`, `_`, a `--` in the handle, or too long) gets `home--` and the pair's SHA-256, which no handle can spell. An id a spec already holds, its own room included, refuses the apply with the API's sentence; a room of another project under the id refuses it naming that project, and a room someone else owns refuses it naming the owner. A box that names no FDE and a definition with no agent get no home room, and the apply says why. `sail project apply --json` reports `home_room: {id, created, engaged}` (or `{skipped}`), and `GET /v1/projects/{p}` names the project's `member` and the reader's `home_room` id (none for a reader with no FDE, such as a machine token). The room is a room: it syncs, is scoped and wakes as any other.
- **A run's log stream relays the last lines its tail wrote before exiting.** `GET /v1/runs/{id}/stream` stopped as soon as it saw the tail process gone, so lines written between an empty read and the exit were dropped. A tail seen gone is now read to the end of its pipe first.

## 0.49.0

- **A scoped FDE sees exactly its granted projects, on every box.** Sync was a full replica: main paged every row of every synced type to every node that authenticated, handed out any blob by hash, and sent the whole roster with emails; roles gated writes and no read was narrowed by who asked. An FDE can now be scoped to the projects main grants it, and one predicate, "is this row's project in this actor's scope" (`ProjectScope.admits`), is asked at every door that reads or writes a row: main's sync server, main's API and the journal that decides writes. An unscoped FDE, which is every FDE until one is added scoped, sees and does exactly what it did. **Upgrade main before granting anyone**: a node of an earlier release under the new main is sent only its scope all the same, but ignores the scope main names and so never drops a project on a revoke.
  - **`sail fde add <handle> --scoped`** adds an FDE that sees nothing until granted; **`sail fde grant <handle> <project>`** and **`sail fde revoke <handle> <project>`** (admin, on main or a standalone box, refused on a node with the roster sentence; `--dry-run`, `--json`; idempotent) edit its grants. A grant names a project in the catalog; a revoke takes back whatever was granted. `sail fde list` gains a `SCOPE` column (`all`, or the count and names), and `GET /v1/fdes` names each FDE's `scope` (`all`, or `granted` with its `projects`) to an admin reader and to no one else. An admin is never scoped and a scoped FDE never made admin: an admin edits the roster, and so could grant itself any project; main's own FDE is an admin, and so never scoped, whatever its roster row says. A project's erasure takes back its grants: one created under the name later is another project, granted anew. There is no command yet that scopes an FDE who was added unscoped.
  - **A grant shows after the node's next round; a revocation removes the project in the same round.** Main's welcome names the node's scope. A project newly in it has its history below the node's checkpoints, so they are reset and main's heads walked again; what the node held re-adopts unwritten. A project the node holds that the scope no longer admits is dropped before anything is asked of main: rows, history, heads, offers, erase requests, conflicts and content, **unpushed work included**, and the round says so ("project beta: dropped from this box with the 12 rows it held here, unpushed work included — main no longer grants this box the project"). A project with a run still under way on the box is kept, named with the run, and dropped on the first round after the run ends; nothing of it is offered meanwhile, so nothing of it is denied and withdrawn under the run. The project's container and the files already written to disk are the operator's to remove.
  - **Main decides scope alone and remembers nothing per node.** Every change head now carries its project (`change_heads.project`; a review's is the one its spec had when the review was first written, a message's its conversation's, a file's the one its id begins with), kept through a deletion, a withdrawal and an erasure, so a page skips a foreign head without reading its row and still moves the node's checkpoint past it, and an erasure reaches the nodes that held the row and no other. Every entry main sends names the project of its head and the node files its own head there, so the two agree on what a revocation takes: a spec moved between projects leaves its room, thread, runs and earlier reviews in the project it came from, and they stay or go with that project. A review is filed once: writing it again after its spec moved (a re-dispatch supersedes it) leaves it where it was, and main's API reads it by that filing as sync does, so a spec moved into a scope shows none of its earlier reviews or their findings there. Its owner, if scoped, cannot prune it while anything belonging to it is filed outside the scope ("Pruning spec 'x' would erase work outside your project scope."); an admin can. The `scoped-heads-v1` data migration fills the heads written before; a head whose entity was pruned before this release names no project and reaches unscoped nodes only.
  - **Nothing of another project crosses the wire.** A scoped session is served a blob only once an entry main sent it referenced it, and a chunk only once a manifest it was served listed it. It reads the roster of the FDEs who share a project with it, without their emails, and its own row whole. A push outside its scope is denied before anything is read of what main holds, naming the id and carrying no version of main's: "Spec 'x' is in no project this FDE is granted.", or, for an id main holds in another project, "Spec 'x' already exists."; the node withdraws its row. An erase request for an id outside the scope is answered as one main does not hold. A sync session that names no FDE, which used to be an anonymous viewer that received everything, receives nothing.
  - **The journal refuses a scoped actor's write outside its scope on every lane**, ahead of the type's own rule (`ScopeRule`): a create naming a hidden project is "Project 'beta' was not found." (`not_found`), and a write over a row in one says only that the id is taken. What a write goes over is read from where that row is filed, so a review written before its spec moved projects is not the new project's to rewrite; a dispatch that would close such a review is refused for a scoped caller ("Dispatching spec 'x' would close a review outside your project scope.", `conflict`) until an admin dispatches the spec once.
  - **Main's API and its event stream filter by the caller's scope.** Every read route, and every write route that addresses a row, answers a row outside the caller's scope exactly as one that does not exist, lists leave such rows out, and `GET /v1/events/stream` delivers no event of another project. A conversation is read message by message: what was said in a spec's room before the spec moved projects stays with the project it left, in the room's pages, its open question, a run's inbox and an agent's prompt alike. A run whose FDE lost its project is answered `run_not_found` on the local socket from the next request on. The SSH gateway's `spec`, `agent` and `events` commands reach the same routes as the same caller, so `ssh sail@main spec list` with a scoped node's key lists that node's projects. A page of events is as long as its limit of what its reader sees, however busy a project outside the scope. A local post is announced under the project the message is filed in, a reply's being its thread's, so a stream that cannot read the message is never told of it. A shared-file route of a project outside the scope answers as one the catalog does not hold, so no path of another project can be probed. A run narrates its own lifecycle under the project it is filed in though an admin moved its spec away. A spec moved into the scope is its owner's to delete, its identity room staying where the spec left it; its wake lives on that room, which is the room that was not found. A room's refusal to be deleted counts the specs the caller sees, and says only that the room holds work outside the scope when it sees none. A dispatch that names no spec passes over one whose earlier review is filed outside the caller's scope. A request whose auth stamped no scope reads none: the API fails closed.
  - `sail project init` and `sail project apply` on a scoped node refuse a project outside its scope before writing anything, naming `sail fde grant`.
  - Not in this release: a role per grant, a Mast surface for grants, and following a project rename (a rename is a new project to a grant; grant the new name).

## 0.48.0

- **A live member's turn says when it begins.** Nothing was published between a prompt reaching a live member and the first text or tool step of its answer, so a room showed nothing while the member thought. `agent_turn_began` is published at the first line of every turn, a thinking one included, carrying the `run_id`, `room_id`, `principal` and `turn` every live event does; like them it is never persisted and no client may publish it. A client names it in the room stream's `type=` list.

## 0.47.0

- **An engaged agent is one live process, and every turn streams into the room.** An engaged agent's answer was a new process per turn: a 5 s debounce, a `systemd-run` launch behind a dozen container execs, a hook on every tool call, a relay that read the room at most every 15 s, and the agent posting its own reply with `spec comment`. Nothing streamed. A member whose harness has a streaming mode (Claude Code; Codex has none and keeps the per-turn wake unchanged) is now one process that stays up: sail starts `claude --print --input-format stream-json --output-format stream-json --verbose --include-partial-messages --permission-prompts none --settings ~/.sail/claude-live.json` over pipes, writes each human message to its stdin the moment it lands, and reads what it writes.
  - **Sail posts the reply.** The text of a turn is posted to the room as the run's principal (`claude/<runId>`, `claude/room-<runId>` for a read-only member) through the door the `spec` CLI uses. The member is told so, and runs `spec comment` only with `--question`; it runs no hook, and its settings file names none. A message posted during a turn is written at once and starts the next turn.
  - **Three events carry the stream**, never persisted and never publishable by a client: `agent_text_delta` (`text`; fragments within 50 ms are told as one), `agent_tool_call` (`tool`, `summary`, `status` `started` or `finished`) and `agent_turn_ended` (`message_id`, `is_error`, `reason`). Each carries `run_id`, `room_id`, `principal` and `turn`, and its `spec` is the room id. `GET /v1/events/stream` takes `room=<id>` beside `project` and `type`. What a reader sees afterwards is the message.
  - **A live member is one run** (`room` or `room-full`, unit `live-<runId>`, no watcher). It ends after the lane's `max_idle` with nothing said, at the lane's `max_duration`, when the room's seat changes, and when sail stops or restarts; ending closes its stdin, and a process that does not exit is sent `INT` and then `TERM`. A turn cut short is posted as far as it got. The next message starts a process that resumes the recorded session, so the conversation continues. The guardrail `action` does not apply: there is nothing to snapshot.
  - **A dispatch no longer waits on a conversation.** A build, ad-hoc, reviewer or fix claim on repos a live member holds ends the member (at its open turn's result, or by force after 30 s) and lands, where it was refused; the member resumes on the room's next message once the repos are free, with what was posted meanwhile. A claim another run holds is refused as before.
  - **`sail agent attach` on a live member's run ends the member first**, then opens the terminal on its session, where it refused a live run; while that terminal is open the member is not started. From the CLI the member is ended by signal, since only the server holds its stdin.
  - **Nothing posted is cut or dropped on its way to the member.** Messages are written oldest first, 32,000 code points to a prompt; what does not fit is the next prompt, and a message longer than one prompt is written across several, each part ending `[continues in the next prompt]` and the next beginning `[continued from the previous prompt]`. A message counts as delivered only once all of it was written, so one a process took part of is handed whole to the next process.
  - **A member that lost its run or its seat while it was starting is never prompted.** A claim or a terminal that ended its run, or a dismissal or a change of agent, mode or model, between the reservation and the first prompt ends the process with nothing written to it; the message waits for the next process.
  - **What a member reserved is freed only once its process is known gone.** A member is prompted only after the pid its wrapper wrote is recorded on its run (sail waits up to 30 s for it; a process that writes none is ended unprompted and its run finished at once, and the server log says "The room's live member did not start: its process wrote no pid."). Another sail process on the box (a CLI dispatch, `sail agent attach`) ends a run that records no pid as never started, and the member's own sail then ends the process with nothing written to it; a run that records one is signalled through its pid file and kept `running` until the container answers that the process is gone. After a restart, a member whose container does not answer, or whose process outlived its signals, keeps its run and its reservation: the server's stderr says "Could not confirm that the live member of run `<runId>` left from before the restart has stopped, so what it reserved stays held.", and the run is finished once the container says its process is gone. Before, a restart freed it unconfirmed.
  - **A member that stopped reading its stdin is still read and still ended.** A prompt is written outside the member's lock and its stdin is closed on a thread of its own, so a full pipe holds up neither the lines the process writes nor the `INT` and `TERM` that end it. A process that exits unasked before its run recorded its pid fails its run with the end of its log as the reason.
  - **A member whose process ended is gone from its room, whatever its end met.** A read-only member's guard that cannot look as the process exits (the container did not answer) loses that look alone, told on the server's stderr; the run is still finished and the room's next message starts the next process, where a failed look left a member behind that took no prompt. A restart reclaims only the live runs reserved before this sail started: a message that lands as sail starts is answered by the member it starts, where `reclaim` could end that member's run as one left from before.
  - **A session host that cannot be asked holds the conversation.** When sail cannot ask the pty host whether a terminal is open on the member's session (a refused credential, a host that does not answer), no process is started over it and the server log says "Could not ask the session host whether a terminal holds the conversation of run `<runId>`, so no process was started over it."; the room's next message asks again. No host socket at all still means no terminal.
  - A read-only member's commit guard now looks after every turn, not once at the run's end.
  - `RoomWakePrompt` wrote the question form as `spec comment <id> --question --body <text>`, which the CLI refuses; it now writes `--body <text> --question`.

- **A box hands its FDE one connect code, and Mast needs nothing else.** Reaching a box from Mast took a key pair, a pasted public key, an `~/.ssh/config` entry, `ssh-add`, `sail client <host>` and a token pasted by hand. `sail fde pair <handle> --host <ip-or-dns> [--email <email>] [--name <name>] [--role <role>] [--ttl-days N | --no-expiry] [--as-workstation-key]` now mints all of it as one string and prints it once: `sail1.` followed by unpadded base64url of a JSON object naming the FDE (`handle`, `email`), the box (`host`, `port`, `user`, `host_key`, `server`), a passphrase-less ed25519 private key made for the pairing (`key`) and an API token bound to the FDE (`token`, named `mast-<handle>`, 365 days by default). Nothing secret stays on the box: the pair is made in a temporary directory that is removed, and only the public key line, the token's hash and the pairing's record (`fde_pairings`, a new box-local table) remain.
  - **Mast logs in as the box's operator**, the account `sail-api` runs as (root on a SYSTEM-mode box), because the API's loopback port and the pty socket are that account's and the `sail` gateway user forbids forwarding. Run the command as that account; the code's `user` is the account it ran as. The key line is appended to that account's `~/.ssh/authorized_keys` (`0600`, `~/.ssh` created `0700` when absent) with `no-agent-forwarding,no-X11-forwarding,no-user-rc` and the comment `sail-mast:<handle>`. The login ends with the code: the line carries the token's deadline as sshd's `expiry-time` (UTC, to the second), so sshd itself refuses the key once the token has expired, and `--no-expiry` writes none. Each edit of the file holds `~/.ssh/authorized_keys.sail.lock` from its read to its rename, so a pairing made while another is revoked never writes the revoked line back. A paired person can open a shell on the box as its operator.
  - **Containers trust the paired key.** It becomes the box's workstation key, and the command names the projects whose containers trust it only after their next `sail project apply`. A box whose workstation key is someone else's refuses, naming that key's fingerprint and comment, unless `--as-workstation-key` is passed; pairing the same FDE again replaces its own earlier key without it.
  - **`sail fde unpair <handle>` revokes a pairing**: every `sail-mast:<handle>` line, the token and the record, saying what it removed; a second run changes nothing. `sail fde rm <handle>` does the same after removing the FDE, so a refused removal (the last active admin) leaves the pairing whole. Pairing again unpairs first: one live code per FDE per box.
  - **No login outlives the record that revokes it.** A key line that cannot be removed, or a key file that cannot be looked at, fails `unpair` and `rm` before anything is forgotten ("Could not remove ada's key line from root's authorized_keys (AccessDeniedException), so nothing of ada was removed and its pairing still works. Check that /root/.ssh is writable by root, then run the command again."); `rm` takes the FDE's removal back with it. `pair` writes the record before the line. And `pair`, `unpair` and `rm` run one at a time on a box, holding `<database>.pairings.lock` from their first read to their last write, so one never forgets the record of a login another has just written. `fde rm` now asks its confirmation before it looks the FDE up, so an unanswered prompt holds nobody.
  - The FDE is added when the box holds none (`member` unless `--role` says otherwise) and otherwise keeps its role, name and email unless the options change them; the code always names an email, so one is asked for when neither the option nor the FDE has it. A node refuses to pair as it refuses `fde add`; a disabled FDE, a missing `--host` and a box with no `/etc/ssh/ssh_host_ed25519_key.pub` are refused before anything is written.
  - **A pairing is made and revoked on the host, never through the SSH gateway.** An admin's `fde` commands over an SSH-key session run as the `sail` user, whose `authorized_keys` must never take a login and who cannot reach the operator's. There, `fde pair` and `fde unpair` are refused, and so is `fde rm` of a paired FDE, before anything is removed ("A pairing is a login of this box's operator, and an SSH-key session runs as the gateway's account. Run 'sail fde rm <handle>' on the host, as the account sail-api runs as."). Removing an FDE that has no pairing works through the gateway as before.

- **Sail says the loop's rules in two prompts; the project says everything else in its own files.** What an agent in the loop was told came from four places: three stage skills sail pasted into the prompt (`sail-build`, `sail-review`, `sail-fix`, replaceable per stage), the envelope each prompt builder added, a generated context file per harness (`~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`, `~/.codex/AGENTS.md`) built from `agent_context`, `methodology`, `runtimes`, `repos` and `services`, and language rules from `agent_context.rules`. There are now two prompts, sail's and fixed: the *work prompt* (`WorkPrompt`), read by the build and by the fix run, which is the build's with one section added, the findings; and the *judge prompt* (`JudgePrompt`), read by every agent stage, which carries the spec's title and body (cut at a section boundary to the prompt budget, saying what it left out: whole sections, or the rest of a section cut inside), what to review, the stage's brief and the verdict contract. Nothing a project writes replaces, prepends or appends to either; the verdict parser and the decision table are untouched.
  - **A stage's brief is the one agent-facing text a project writes:** `agent.review_pipeline.stages[].brief`, text of at most 32,000 code points on an agent stage, in the judge prompt where the stage skill's body went. A stage with no brief judges under sail's default brief (the spec first — its stated design, its bar and its non-goals — then correctness, security, error handling, concurrency, resource use, API contracts, duplication and tests; formatting and style are not findings). A brief that orders a project skill ("run the project's `e2e` skill …") is a validation stage.
  - **A fix run is a build run with findings.** It ends as a build does: commit, push, a pull request open, CI green. The stop gate holds a fix run to the pull-request check and to the build's reason; the fix lane runs under `agent.guardrails` (4h / 20m by default) and the lifetime that follows, where it ran under `review_pipeline.guardrails`, which now bounds reviewers only. The work prompt asks for CI checks in short polls, never one long watch: the stall guard counts from the agent's last tool call, and a single long `--watch` was killed as a stall.
  - **Skills are installed, never pasted.** Every project skill (`.sail/skills/<name>/`) and sail's one skill, `spec-board`, are installed whole into the skills folder of every harness the project installs, at provisioning, at `project apply` and before every launch (stamped, so an unchanged folder is not pushed again; a stamped folder the project no longer holds is removed; a folder without a stamp is left alone, and never replaced: a held skill whose folder is unstamped fails the install naming the folder, except the `spec-board` sail wrote before it stamped, known by its content — regular files of its own names holding what a release wrote — which it replaces). An interactive `sail agent run` installs the same way before its `ssh`. A folder that is no skill is skipped, the valid ones install, and the spec's room is told which folder was skipped and why. No prompt names a skill. `spec-board` now carries a description a model acts on and no longer disables model invocation, so an interactive session finds the `spec` CLI without a context file.
  - **`sail project skills` manages skills:** `ls` (name, files, `ok` or `invalid: <why>`), `add <folder> [--name <name>]` (every file under a local folder becomes `.sail/skills/<name>/<path>`, through the project files store, so it syncs; `sail-*` and `spec-board` refused), `rm <name>` and `show <name>`. The stage table it printed is gone with the stage skills.
  - **Sail writes no context file and generates no rule.** `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` and `~/.codex/AGENTS.md`, their generator and installer, `sail agent context regen`, `sail agent run --no-regen`, the methodology skills (`spec`, `verify`) and the language rules are gone. What they said is the project's (`AGENTS.md`, read by both harnesses natively), already discoverable in the container, or the loop's (now in the work prompt).
  - **Deleted keys are refused, naming their replacement:** `agent.build_skill`, `agent.review_pipeline.fix_skill`, `stages[].skill`, `stages[].categories`, `agent.methodology` and `agent_context` (every sub-key) fail when a definition is read, one sentence each saying where the text now goes (a brief, the repo's `AGENTS.md`, a project skill). A data migration (`stages-are-prompts-v1`, run by `sail migrate`, which `sail upgrade` runs last, and by the sync lane) rewrites every definition this box's catalog holds without those keys, as a journalled revision that syncs, and lists per project what it dropped and where it belongs. Two consequences for a mixed fleet: a row stripped on one box reaches a 0.46.5 box, where a dropped `stages[].skill` means that box judges under `sail-review` until it upgrades; and a row with a deleted key that syncs to an upgraded box before that box's own migrate ran is unreadable until it runs, a window `sail upgrade` closes, not a steady state. A 0.46.5 box reading a definition with `stages[].brief` or `repos[].forge` ignores both.
  - **`gh` is logged in.** Where provisioning or `project apply` writes a token into `~/.git-credentials` for a GitHub host (`github.com`, or a repo marked `repos[].forge: github` for a GitHub Enterprise host), it also logs the installed `gh` in for that host as the dev user, the token pushed to a `0600` file under the dev home and consumed on stdin by one command that removes it, success or not; a failed login fails the step naming the host. The room lane's read-deny rules now also cover `~/.config/gh/**`. A run on a repo whose forge has no authenticated CLI in the container is told to say so in the room instead of claiming CI green.
  - Provisioning phase `CONTEXT_GENERATED` is now `SKILLS_INSTALLED`; a box whose provisioning state file names the old phase resumes from the start of provisioning.

## 0.46.5

- **The journal decides every local write, so no door can forget.** Who may write a synced row was one rule per type, which each door had to remember to ask; an audit found nine doors that did not, and two that answered a rule's refusal with a code of their own. Every write that is not a push is now decided where its revision is recorded (`RevisionJournal`, and `MessageStore.append` for a post), a pushed one at main's commit as before, each exactly once. Doors keep only the admission that precedes a side effect.
  - **Doors that were unchecked are now decided by their type's rule.** `sail project apply`, `project edit`, the `project add-`/`remove-`/`resources` edits and `sail project files add` on a node whose FDE is a viewer are refused (`read_only_credential`: "Your credential is read-only and cannot change projects." / "… change files."), before a command clones, starts or ingests anything. `sail agent run` and `sail agent sweep` there are refused the same way, with no run reserved and no agent launched. A conflict resolved with `--mine` or `--merge` is an edit of the row, decided by its rule against main's version: a merge that sets another assignee is `forbidden_admin_only`, and an agent resolves only what its FDE may edit. `--theirs` only adopts main's version and decides no row, but it gives up what the box parked, so a read-only credential is refused it (`read_only_credential`); any credential that can write may. The door's own "Only this node's owner or an admin can resolve its conflicts." (`forbidden`) is gone.
  - **Renaming a project is admin-only**, on a node and at main's commit: it moves every spec and file in the project, whoever's they are. A member's `sail project rename` is refused before its container is stopped or anything is written (`forbidden_admin_only`: "Renaming project '<name>' moves every spec and file in it, whoever's they are, and is an admin-only action."), where it used to re-journal other members' specs that main then denied.
  - **A run is created only for the owner of the spec or room it names**, by an admin, or — naming neither — for the FDE it acts for, on every lane and at main's commit. A node can no longer push a run onto another FDE's spec (denied: "Run <id> belongs to spec '<spec>', owned by '<owner>', not you."). A run names a spec or a spec-less room, never both, as every launcher has always written it: one naming both is refused for everyone (`invalid_request`: "Run '<id>' names both spec '<spec>' and room '<room>', and a run works one."), so a run on the pusher's own spec cannot be pushed into another member's room, where it would have silenced that room's wake. A run that reaches main ahead of a spec or room its own box made lands as before. A spec an admin reassigns while another FDE's box is running it keeps the run it has; the next run that box starts on it is denied on its push. A credential naming another FDE can no longer start an ad-hoc run on a box that is not its own (`forbidden_not_assignee`).
  - **One code per refusal kind at every door.** Over HTTP a read-only credential's write was `forbidden` ("Your role is not permitted to perform this action.") from a route tier that ran before any rule; it is now the rule's `read_only_credential` with the rule's message and fix, as on the socket and the host CLI. HTTP tiers a route for read, and for admin where it administers the control plane, never for write: `POST /v1/sync` with no `main` and `POST /v1/projects/{p}/agent/report` are now served to a read-only credential, as `sail sync` and `sail agent report` are on its box; restoring or deleting a snapshot is refused it (`read_only_credential`). Managing a spec-less room you do not own was `not_your_spec` from the membership door and is now the room rule's `forbidden_not_assignee` ("Room '<id>' belongs to '<owner>', not you."), the code deleting it already gave; a room that belongs to another box is still `not_your_spec`. Kinds map to codes in one place: `READ_ONLY` → `read_only_credential`, `NOT_OWNER` → `forbidden_not_assignee`, `ADMIN_ONLY` → `forbidden_admin_only`, `NOT_AUTHOR` → `forbidden_not_author`, `FIXED` → `invalid_request`, `NOT_PRUNABLE` → `spec_not_prunable`, `NOT_PUBLISHABLE` → `forbidden`.
  - **A spec may be born over a room that is already its owner's.** `POST /v1/specs` with the id of an existing room was `conflict` (409) for everyone, while main's commit took the same birth from a node. The spec rule now decides it at the door as on main: the room's owner, or an admin, creates the spec, which adopts that room as its own and mints none; anyone else gets `forbidden_not_assignee` ("Room '<id>' already exists, and a spec's id is reserved for its own room."), and so does everyone, on a node and at main's commit, for a spec with that id born in another room (`room_id`), which would have left the room answering as the other one. A room of another project is `invalid_request`. Creating a spec whose id is already a spec's is now `conflict` (409, "Spec '<id>' already exists."), where it was an `internal` 500.
  - **An operation that writes several rows commits them once.** A spec update (its row, its wake, and the findings a move into or out of `done` resolves), a delete (with the shipped follow-up catch-up that used to run before it), a follow-up (the draft, its body, and its links to the findings it was drafted from) and a dispatch or restart (the run's reservation, the reset to `pending`, the claim and the superseded reviews) each used to commit in up to four transactions; a refusal or failure of a later row left the earlier ones. A refused write now rolls back everything its operation wrote, even where a caller swallowed the error.
  - **Refusals read in a new order where a rule used to run first.** A write is validated, then decided as it is recorded, so a request that is both invalid and not permitted now answers the validation: a read-only session creating a spec in a room that does not exist gets `room_not_found` (it was `read_only_credential`), restoring a revision that does not exist gets `invalid_request` whoever asks, deleting a room that still holds specs gets `conflict` before the owner check, and approving a review with no human stage waiting gets `invalid_request`. Nothing a credential could not already read is disclosed.
  - **The socket answers a refusal in HTTP's envelope.** An error thrown on the in-container socket was `{"error": "<message>"}`; it is now `{"schema_version": 1, "error": {"code", "message", "action", "field_errors"}}`, as HTTP's. Route, method and credential problems on the socket keep the short form.
  - A project purge's handling of unfinished runs is the erase rule's (`EraseAuthority.idle`), asked by the local prune and by main's decision on a node's request, where each kept its own copy. Behaviour is unchanged: a project is purged whole once its container is gone, its runs with it.

## 0.46.4

- **A project says how each stage works; sail still runs the loop.** What a build, a reviewer and a fix agent are told about how to do their work used to be three hard-coded strings, each mixing advice a project may want to change ("Only report genuine issues") with contracts the loop parses or enforces ("Respond with exactly one JSON object", "push the branch and open a pull request"). The instructions of each stage are now a skill, a folder with a `SKILL.md` and optional scripts and reference files, that sail ships a default for and a project can replace.
  - Sail ships `sail-build`, `sail-review` and `sail-fix`. A project that configures nothing gets them, and every sentence of the three prompts is still said, in the default skill or after it: each prompt gains a `## How to do this work (skill: <name>)` section, and two sentences are split around it (the build's "When complete, run the full local verification …, commit …, push the branch, and open a pull request" and the fix agent's "When every finding is addressed: run the project's verification locally, commit …"). The reviewer's rules 1–4 moved into `sail-review`; rules 5 and 6 are now 1 and 2.
  - A project's skill is project files under `.sail/skills/<name>/` (`sail project files add <file> --as .sail/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`), synced and limited like any project file and never copied into the workspace. Three keys name which skill a stage uses: `agent.build_skill`, `agent.review_pipeline.fix_skill` and `agent.review_pipeline.stages[].skill`. A key accepts its own default or a project skill's name; a name that is not `[a-z0-9][a-z0-9-]{0,63}`, another name starting `sail-`, `spec`, `spec-board`, `verify`, one of the project's `agent_context.rules` names, or a `skill` on a human stage is refused when the definition is read, naming the key. That holds for the defaults too: a rule named `sail-build`, or `sail-review` or `sail-fix` in a project whose pipeline runs them (no `review_pipeline`, or one with no stages), is refused. A `review_pipeline` block with a `fix_skill` and no stages runs the default stages and keeps that skill. A box on an older sail ignores the three keys and runs the defaults.
  - Sail puts the skill's body in the prompt itself on every launch and both harnesses, and what the loop parses or enforces follows it, so no skill replaces the verdict envelope, the push and pull request, or the dispute lane. The skill's folder is installed where the harness looks for skills (`~/.claude/skills/<name>/`, `~/.agents/skills/<name>/`) before the agent starts: stamped with a fingerprint of its files, not pushed again while it is unchanged, and replaced whole, in one step, when it changed. Launches that replace one folder at once take turns (`flock` on the skills directory), and a skill is built beside the skills directory, never in it, in a hidden `.sail-stage-build-<name>.<runId>` folder that no skill or rule can be named like; one a dead launch left is removed by the next install of that skill once it is a day old. A skill is read at each launch and bounded (a body of at most 32,000 code points, a folder of at most 32 files and 1 MiB). One that cannot be read stops its stage: a dispatch is refused with `bad_request` naming `.sail/skills/<name>/SKILL.md` before anything is reserved, a reviewer's review errors and is retried within the errored-review budget, a fix agent's review escalates.
  - **`sail project skills [-p <project>] [--json]`** lists the build, each agent review stage and the fix with the skill each runs under and how it stands (`built-in`, `project (n files)`, `missing`, `invalid: …`); **`sail project skills show <name>`** prints a skill's `SKILL.md`, so a project starts its own from sail's default.
  - A dispatch for an agent sail does not know is now `bad_request` before the spec is claimed, where it was `agent_launch_failed` after; a spec with no `agent` in a project with no `agent.type` is refused the same way ("Project '<name>' names no agent; set agent.type or the spec's agent."). The provisioner's "files/ directory is empty" notice also shows for a project whose only files are skills.
- **What runs a project reads it from the catalog, never from a file.** The review loop, every API lane (dispatch, ad-hoc runs, room wakes, reviewer and fix launches, watcher relaunches, stops, snapshots, membership), Slack and webhook notifications, and `sail agent watch`, `status`, `report`, `attach`, `context regen` and `run` read a project's definition from its catalog row (`projects.definition`), where they used to read `~/.sail/projects/<name>/sail.yaml`. The file is still written, as a copy nothing running reads.
  - A project revision that arrives by sync, or that `project apply` records when it creates the project, `project edit` or a mutation command records, is what the next dispatch, the next loop event and the next notification use, with no `sail project apply` run; a box that already had the project used to keep its old file until someone ran `project apply`. A review in flight whose stages no longer match escalates as before. A watcher already running keeps the notifications it started with. A `sail.yaml` edited by hand, with the row unchanged, is read by nothing that runs the project.
  - A project with a file and no row is not found: a dispatch is refused with `Project '<name>' is not in the catalog.` and the fix (`sudo sail migrate` imports a descriptor that is on the box; `sail project apply -f <file>` creates the project). No row is ever substituted by a file. A row that cannot be read fails naming the project: a lane with `project_load_failed`, a running review by escalating ("The definition of project '<name>' in the catalog could not be read: …; fix the project's definition with `sail project edit`, then re-dispatch with --restart"), and notifications with a warning and nothing sent. `agent status` and `agent attach` still go on without a definition for a project with no row.
  - A definition that does not reach the catalog is not written to the file either: `project apply -f` creating a project, `project edit`, `project add-repo`, `add-service`, `remove-service` and `resources set` now exit non-zero naming the project and the cause where they printed a note and went on. `project add-repo` has already cloned the repo into the container when it records, and `add-service` and `remove-service` have already changed the container's services, as before.
  - `agent context regen` and `agent run` write the box's git identity into the agent's context from the row: `${GIT_NAME}` and `${GIT_EMAIL}` are replaced where the box has a git identity and left in place where it has none, which no longer fails the command; on the box that authored a project the context used to name what was typed into the file. A git name with an apostrophe (`O'Neil`) is written as it is: the identity is substituted into the parsed definition, never into the row's text, where the quoted placeholder made the substituted text unparseable, on this path and when a project is provisioned.
  - `-f/--file` on `spec dispatch`, `agent sweep`, `agent run`, `agent watch`, `agent status`, `agent report`, `agent attach` and `agent context regen` is accepted and ignored: the project is read from the catalog. The spawned watcher no longer receives it. The `project` commands keep theirs.
- **Every agent run launches, is watched and stops one way, and each lane's limits are the project's.** A reviewer and a fix agent used to run as a foreground `incus exec` the server blocked on, bounded by a 30-minute limit no setting changed. At the limit only the host side of the exec was killed: the agent kept running in the container, the review loop recorded a failure, and the agent then pushed its work into a loop that had stopped listening. Four specs in two weeks needed a manual takeover for it.
  - A reviewer and a fix agent are now runs like a build: each invocation is its own run row (role `review` or the new `fix`) that names the review it serves (`review_id`), launched as its own `sail-agent-<runId>` systemd unit with the same hooks and environment, watched by its own `sail agent watch`, and ended by the one authoritative `agent_session_stopped`. The review pipeline no longer waits on an agent: it routes each stop by the lane of the run that stopped. A build's stop starts the review; a reviewer's resolves its stage from the findings in that run's own log; a fix agent's commits what it left and starts the re-review.
  - **`agent.review_pipeline.guardrails`** (`max_duration`, `max_idle`, `action`) bounds reviewers and fix agents, default `45m` / `20m` / `stop`. `agent.guardrails` keeps bounding builds, ad-hoc runs and chat turns, default `4h` / `20m` / `stop`. Both blocks parse the same way, and an invalid duration, a zero limit, an unknown action or a `guardrails:` value that is not a block is now refused when `sail.yaml` is read, naming the block, the key, the file and the accepted forms, where an invalid duration used to surface only when a run launched or its watcher started. A block that names only some keys sets only those: `max_idle` left out of a block is no stall limit. The watcher takes its run's limits on its command line (`--max-duration`, `--max-idle`, `--action`), so an edit applies to the next run; a watcher re-armed after its own death takes the limits as they are then.
  - **The watcher publishes the stop it causes.** On a trip it kills the unit's whole cgroup in the container and, once the container answers that the unit is gone, records the trigger beside the run (`~/.sail/runs/<runId>/guardrail-triggered.yaml`, no longer one `/home/dev/guardrail-triggered.yaml` per container) and publishes the run's stop with `reason` (`time limit (45m)`, `stall (20m)`). A unit that survives is killed again at the next poll and never reported ended, and no kill is tried while the container cannot say whether it worked, so an agent that ends before one was tried is reported as the plain exit it was; a watcher that finds its container stopped, or its unit's manager and process gone, ends without a stop and leaves the run to the reconciler. This holds for builds too, where the stop used to come from the reconciler's next sweep with no reason. A stop that carries a `reason` carries no `exit_code`: a killed run has none of its own. A build ended this way still goes to review, as before. The trigger file gains `cause`, the reason as the stop says it.
  - **The watcher notices an exit on the clock and survives a daemon restart.** It asks the container whether its unit is still active at least every 15 seconds, where it used to ask only after 15 seconds with no event from any run in the project. When its event stream ends with a daemon restart it subscribes again at its next poll, and the stall window starts afresh: a watcher that outlived a restart used to go deaf and kill a working agent for a stall one `max_idle` later.
  - **The missed-stop reconciler leaves a run to its watcher.** It publishes no stop for a run while a `sail agent watch` process for it is running, so a sweep landing between a watcher's kill and its stop, or before its next poll, no longer gets in first with a stop that names no exit code and no reason. For a run no watcher covers it says what is still known: the non-zero exit code a failed unit holds, and the limit the watcher recorded beside the run when its stop never reached the daemon.
  - **A reaped agent is dead, and the room says why.** A killed reviewer errors its review (`review_errored`: `reviewer killed: time limit (45m)`) and is retried within the same budget as any errored review. A killed or failed fix agent publishes `review_iteration_failed` (`fix agent killed: stall (20m)`, `fix agent failed: exit 1`), nothing of its is committed, and the review escalates. `review_iteration_failed` is now narrated in Slack and in Mast's room, where it used to be shown nowhere.
  - **The fix agent no longer waits for CI.** Its task ends at a pushed commit ("verify locally, commit, push, end your turn; do not watch CI"), and the stop gate asks a fix agent for a clean, pushed tree only. The re-review judges the branch, and the pull request shows its checks to whoever merges. This is what makes 45 minutes a real budget for a fix that runs full verification. A build is still asked to watch its checks to green. A reviewer is never blocked at its stop: its last message is the verdict the pipeline parses.
  - **A daemon restart fails nothing.** The server no longer marks running reviews failed at start. A reviewer or fix agent that is still running has its watcher re-armed; one that ended while the server was down has its stop published by the missed-stop reconciler, which reconciles them as it does builds; and a review left `running` with nothing running it goes on from its stage rows.
  - **`review.log` is retired.** Every run's log is `~/.sail/runs/<runId>/agent.log`, whatever its lane, and `GET /v1/runs/{id}/log` and `/stream` read it the same way. `sail agent log --review` shows the latest reviewer run's log, and the new `--fix` the latest fix run's. `sail agent log`, `status`, `report` and `attach` with no flag read the newest run of any lane. A review that ran before this release keeps its log: its run recorded `~/.sail/runs/<id>/review.log`, and the log endpoints and `sail agent log --review` still read that file for it.
  - A reviewer and a fix agent reserve their spec's repos through the same gate as a dispatch, so neither starts beside a live build of its own spec, a full chat turn or another spec's run over the same repos; a claim refused for a run in the way makes the review wait, with no attempt spent and nothing escalated. **The wait is recorded**: the review names the run that holds its claim (`reviews.waiting_on`, local to the box and never synced), the stage it could not start stays `pending` (it used to read `running` with no reviewer, and main announced `review_stage_started` for it), and the room is told in the same write, so once per run waited on: "Review is waiting for run `<runId>` (`<role>` of `<specId>`) to finish." The holder's stop launches the waiting step; a holder that ends with no stop is rescued by the reconciler once its row has been terminal for longer than the launch grace (2 minutes), exactly once per (review, holder). The reconciler no longer samples the dispatch gate or retries a rescue up to three times: a review refused three times running by runs that end silently is launched after the third, where it used to be stranded, and a review no replay can move is no longer replayed four times. A container held by a snapshot restore is still an error. A review no longer holds the whole container: a spec on other repos can be dispatched while it runs.
  - A launch that reports failure after its agent started no longer escalates the review while that agent works on: the run stands, and the loop waits for its stop. A launch whose run was finished under it tears its agent down unless the agent is verified gone.
  - The loop acts only on a spec still `in_progress` or `review`: a spec cancelled while its reviewer runs gets no fix agent.
